Critical points of the distance function to a generic submanifold
Charles Arnal ∗, David Cohen-Steiner ∗, Vincent Divol ∗

Abstract
Preamble In general, the critical points of the distance function d 𝖬 d_{\mathsf{M}} to a compact submanifold 𝖬 ⊂ ℝ D \mathsf{M}\subset\mathbb{R}^{D} can be poorly behaved. In this article, we show that this is generically not the case by listing regularity conditions on the critical and μ \mu -critical points of a submanifold and by proving that they are generically satisfied and stable with respect to small C 2 C^{2} perturbations. More specifically, for any compact abstract manifold M M , the set of embeddings i : M → ℝ D i:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{D} such that the submanifold i ⁡ ( M ) i(M) satisfies those conditions is open and dense in the Whitney C 2 C^{2} -topology. When those regularity conditions are fulfilled, we prove that the distance function to i ⁡ ( M ) i(M) satisfies Morse-like conditions and that the critical points of the distance function to an ε \varepsilon -dense subset of the submanifold (e.g., obtained via some sampling process) are well-behaved. We also provide many examples that showcase how the absence of these conditions allows for pathological situations.



*
*
footnotetext:
The authors contributed equally to this work.

1
Introduction

Questions regarding the distance function
d_{\mathsf{M}}:x\mapsto\inf\{d(x,y):\;y\in{\mathsf{M}}\}
to a submanifold
{\mathsf{M}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
stand at the intersection of a variety of domains; they feature preeminently in computational geometry
[
CCSL06
]
, statistics on manifolds
[
NSW08
,
AKC+19
,
ABL23
]
and topological data analysis
[
EH22
]
, and their study frequently involves tools from classical differential geometry as well
[
Yom81
,
Mat83
]
.
In this article, we combine notions and methods from computational geometry and transversality theorems
à la
Thom to understand the behaviour of the critical points of the distance function to a generic submanifold of
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, and we show that they behave very nicely. We also explore some consequences in terms of sampling of the submanifold and Morse-like behaviour of
d_{\mathsf{M}}
.

The distance function and its critical points:

In 2004, A. Lieutier
[
Lie04
]
proposed a generalized notion of gradient for the distance function
d_{\mathsf{S}}
to a compact set
{\mathsf{S}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
. Let
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
and
\pi_{\mathsf{S}}(x)
be the set of all projections of
x
on
{\mathsf{S}}
, that is

\forall y\in{\mathsf{S}},\quad y\in\pi_{\mathsf{S}}(x)\Longleftrightarrow d_{\mathsf{S}}(x)=d(x,y).

(1)

The generalized gradient of
d_{\mathsf{S}}
at
x\not\in{\mathsf{S}}
is given by

\nabla d_{\mathsf{S}}(x)=\frac{x-m(\pi_{\mathsf{S}}(x))}{d_{\mathsf{S}}(x)},

(2)

where
m(\sigma)
is the center of the smallest enclosing ball of a non-empty bounded set
\sigma
. This is illustrated in Figure
1
. The gradient is extended by
0
on
{\mathsf{S}}
, so that
\nabla d_{\mathsf{S}}
is defined on
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, and with this definition the norm
\|\nabla d_{S}\|
of the generalized gradient is lower semicontinuous (see
[
Lie04
]
).
The
medial axis

\mathrm{Med}({\mathsf{S}})
is the set of points
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
such that
\pi_{\mathsf{S}}(z)
is of cardinality at least
2
, i.e. the points that do not have a unique projection on
{\mathsf{S}}
. Equivalently,
z\in\mathrm{Med}({\mathsf{S}})
if and only if
\|\nabla d_{S}(z)\|<1
and
z\not\in{\mathsf{S}}
.

Figure 1:
A point
x\in\mathrm{Med}({\mathsf{S}})
with set of projections
\pi_{{\mathsf{S}}}(x)=\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
.

A
critical point
for the distance function is a point
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\backslash{\mathsf{S}}
with
\nabla d_{\mathsf{S}}(z)=0
, in which case
d_{\mathsf{S}}(z)
is called a
singular value
; we let
Z({\mathsf{S}})
be the set of critical points for the distance function to
{\mathsf{S}}
, which we simply call critical points of
{\mathsf{S}}
. These will be our main object of study.
This definition is generalized as follows: for
\mu\in[0,1]
, one defines the set of
\mu
-critical
points as

Z_{\mu}({\mathsf{S}}):=\{z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\backslash{\mathsf{S}}:\;\|\nabla d_{\mathsf{S}}(z)\|\leq\mu\}.

(3)

The generalized gradient and the associated notions of criticality have many uses in computational geometry and data analysis. Among other applications, they lead to Morse-theoretic statements – it is for example shown in
[
Gro93
]
that two offsets
{\mathsf{S}}^{a}=\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}:\;d_{\mathsf{S}}(x)\leq a\}
and
{\mathsf{S}}^{b}
are isotopic whenever the segment
[a,b]
does not contain any critical values of the distance function or
0
.
In topological data analysis, the family of offsets
({\mathsf{S}}^{t})_{t\geq 0}
allows one to compute the Čech persistence diagram of
{\mathsf{S}}

[
CDSO14
]
, which is routinely used in computations to analyze the topology of the set
{\mathsf{S}}
, with numerous applications in machine learning (see e.g.
[
TMO22
]
or the review
[
OPT+17
]
and references therein).
The set of critical values of the distance function corresponds exactly to the coordinates of the points in the Čech persistence diagram, and understanding their behavior in different contexts is a central research topic.
Among other uses, the generalized gradient is also employed to build homotopies between an open bounded set and its medial axis
[
Lie04
]
, to give guarantees on the homology groups of an approximation of a given shape
[
CL05
]
, or to control the convexity defect function of a set
[
ALS11
]
.

Despite its importance, the set of critical points
Z({\mathsf{S}})
is still poorly understood.
This is partly because without further assumptions, the set
Z({\mathsf{S}})
can be almost arbitrarily wild: consider any compact set
{\mathsf{A}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, and let
{\mathsf{S}}:={\mathsf{A}}\times\{-1\}\cup{\mathsf{A}}\times\{1\}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{D+1}
. Then
Z({\mathsf{S}})\cap({\mathbb{R}}^{D}\times[-1/2,1/2])=\left({\mathsf{A}}\cup Z({\mathsf{A}})\right)\times\{0\}
, which is extremely irregular for well-chosen
1
1

1

One can for example let
A
be a Cantor set.

sets
A
.
Another difficulty is the lack of stability of
Z({\mathsf{S}})
with respect to small perturbations of
{\mathsf{S}}
, even under strong regularity assumptions on
{\mathsf{S}}
. Indeed, the map
{\mathsf{S}}\mapsto Z({\mathsf{S}})
is not continuous for the Hausdorff distance
d_{H}
, as is illustrated in Figure
2
.
This lack of stability can be particularly disastrous in practical situations, where the shape of interest
{\mathsf{S}}
(e.g. a submanifold or a polyhedron) is often only accessible through a finite (possibly noisy) sample
{\mathsf{A}}
. The set
Z({\mathsf{A}})
can then be very different from
Z({\mathsf{S}})
, which can lead, in turn, to a poor estimation of the features of interest of
{\mathsf{S}}
.

Figure 2:
The Hausdorff distance between
{\mathsf{S}}_{1}
and
{\mathsf{S}}_{2}
can be made arbitrarily small while keeping the Hausdorff distance between
Z({\mathsf{S}}_{1})
and
Z({\mathsf{S}}_{2})
constant.

The
\mu
-critical points are more stable: as shown by
F. Chazal, D. Cohen-Steiner and A. Lieutier in
[
CCSL06
]
, if
{\mathsf{S}}
and
{\mathsf{A}}
are two compact sets with
d_{H}({\mathsf{S}},{\mathsf{A}})\leq\varepsilon
and if
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, then

z\in Z_{\mu}({\mathsf{A}})\implies

z
is at distance at most
2\sqrt{\varepsilon d_{\mathsf{S}}(z)}
from
Z_{\mu^{\prime}}({\mathsf{S}})
for
\mu^{\prime}=\mu+2\sqrt{\varepsilon/d_{\mathsf{S}}(z)}
.

(4)

Nonetheless, this remains unsatisfactory when one is interested in actual critical points only.

The main goal of this article is to show that unlike what can be expected in general, the set of critical points
of a
generic
compact
C^{2}
submanifold
{\mathsf{M}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
is well-behaved, and remains stable with respect to taking a dense enough sample of
{\mathsf{M}}
.
Furthermore, both these properties are stable with respect to small
C^{2}
perturbations of
{\mathsf{M}}
.

Regularity conditions:

To formalize these statements, let us first clarify what we mean by “well-behaved”.
Here are four conditions that we want our submanifolds to satisfy:

(P1)

For every
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
, the projections
\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})
are the vertices of a non-degenerate simplex of
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
. In particular,
z_{0}
has at most
D+1
projections. Moreover, the point
z_{0}
belongs to the relative interior
of the convex hull of
\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})
.

(P2)

The set
Z({\mathsf{M}})
is finite.

(P3)

For every
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
and every
x_{0}\in\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})
, the sphere
S(z_{0},d_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0}))
is non-osculating
{\mathsf{M}}
at
x_{0}
, in the sense that there exist
\delta>0
and
\alpha>0
such that for all
y\in{\mathsf{M}}\cap B(x_{0},\delta)
,

\|y-z_{0}\|^{2}\geq\|x_{0}-z_{0}\|^{2}+\alpha\|y-x_{0}\|^{2}.

(5)

(P4)

There exist constants
C>0
and
\mu_{0}\in(0,1)
such that for every
\mu\in[0,\mu_{0})
, the set
Z_{\mu}({\mathsf{M}})
is included in a tubular neighborhood of size
C\mu
of
Z({\mathsf{M}})
, that is every point of
Z_{\mu}({\mathsf{M}})
is at distance less than
C\mu
from
Z({\mathsf{M}})
.

Properties
(P1)
and
(P2)
are quite self-explanatory; they control the discreteness of the set of the critical points and of their projections, and the genericity of their relative positions. Property
(P3)
accepts many equivalent formulations, as shown in Section
3
, and is essentially a property of the curvature of
{\mathsf{M}}
at the projection
x_{0}
. It is crucial to the stability of the projections of the critical points, as will become apparent later.
Finally, Property
(P4)
controls the position of the
\mu
-critical points relative to the critical points. It is equivalent to a local condition on the critical points and their projections, as shown in Section
6
; somewhat surprisingly, it also plays a role in the stability of the critical points themselves, as can be seen in Section
8
.

Genericity:

We also need definitions that appropriately capture the idea of genericity for a manifold.
Consider an abstract
C^{r}
manifold
M
for some
r\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{\infty\}
, and let
\mathrm{Emb}^{k}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
be the set of
C^{k}
embeddings from
M
to
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
. For
0\leq l\leq k
, we can endow
\mathrm{Emb}^{k}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
with the Whitney
C^{l}
-topology, see
Section

2
.
Intuitively, two embeddings
i,i^{\prime}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
are close for the Whitney
C^{l}
-topology if their first
l
differentials (including their “0”th differentials, i.e. their values) are uniformly close over
M
when expressed in the coordinates of a fixed, finite atlas of
M
.
In what follows, we are interested in showing that certain properties are such that for any
C^{k}
compact manifold
M
, the set of embeddings
i
such that
i(M)
verifies the property is dense and open in
\mathrm{Emb}^{k}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
for the Whitney
C^{l}
-topology (for some
l\leq k\in{\mathbb{N}}
).
Openness captures the idea that the property should be stable with respect to small perturbations; density captures the idea that most submanifolds should satisfy the condition, as any submanifold can be made to satisfy it with an arbitrarily small modification.
The conjunction of openness and density further reinforces this idea; by contrast, density alone can result in counter-intuitive situations, as
{\mathbb{Q}}
is dense in
{\mathbb{R}}
, yet few would claim that most real numbers are rational.
2
2

2

This nuance can be particularly important for practical applications, as by nature computers can only manipulate rational numbers: one could imagine some algorithm that is guaranteed to perform well on a dense set of irrational inputs, yet fails for any rational input.

The Genericity and Stability Theorems:

We can now formulate our first main result, i.e. that generic
C^{2}
submanifolds have well-behaved critical points and
\mu
-critical points in the sense defined above.

Theorem 1.1

(The Genericity Theorem)
.

Let
M
be a compact
C^{k}
manifold for some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{\infty\}
with
k\geq 2
. Then the set of
C^{k}
embeddings
i:M\to{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
such that
i(M)
satisfies Conditions
(P1-4)
is dense for the Whitney
C^{k}
-topology and open for the Whitney
C^{2}
-topology in
\mathrm{Emb}^{k}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
.

Remark 1.2
.

As the Whitney
C^{l_{1}}
-topology is finer than the Whitney
C^{l_{2}}
-topology for any
l_{1}\geq l_{2}
, the statement implies that for any
2\leq l\leq k
, the set of appropriate embeddings is dense and open in
\mathrm{Emb}^{k}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
for the Whitney
C^{l}
-topology.

Remark 1.3
.

The density part of the statement easily extends to
C^{1}
submanifolds: if
M
is a compact
C^{1}
manifold, then the set of
C^{1}
embeddings
i:M\to{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
such that
i(M)
satisfies Conditions
(P1-4)
is dense in
\mathrm{Emb}^{1}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
for the Whitney
C^{1}
-topology.
We justify this remark in the second part of the proof of Theorem
1.1
, at the end of Section
9
.

While openness is proved through arguments similar to those used in
[
CCSL06
]
or
[
Lie04
]
, we use a more abstract method for the density part of Theorem
1.1
.
Indeed, while it is relatively easy to perturb any local non-generic situation into a generic one, ensuring that the submanifold
i(M)
satisfies
(P1-4)
globally is more challenging.
Inspired by Y. Yomdin’s work in
[
Yom81
]
, we proceed as follows: we define various subsets
W
of the space of multijets such that embeddings whose associated multijet is transverse to
W
satisfy good global geometric properties, and we apply variants of Thom’s transversality theorem to show that the set of transverse embeddings is dense in the appropriate topology (all these notions are properly introduced in Section
9
).

Various examples suggest that the openness part of the statement cannot be easily extended to
C^{1}
submanifolds; in fact, it is even surprising that the theorem holds for
C^{2}
manifolds, as most straightforward arguments seem to force us to resort to the Whitney
C^{3}
-topology (see e.g., Remark
9.26
).
Showing that the property is true for the
C^{2}
-topology greatly increases the difficulty of the proof.
On the other hand, this rather convoluted proof yields as a byproduct a stronger stability result than the openness part of the Genericity Theorem
1.1
, which we call the
C^{2}
Stability Theorem.
Let
{\mathsf{M}}
be a submanifold that satisfies Properties
(P1-4)
; then the theorem states that not only the properties themselves, but the numbers and positions of the critical points of
{\mathsf{M}}
and of their projections are stable with respect to small
C^{2}
perturbations, in the sense detailed below.

Theorem 1.4

(The
C^{2}
Stability Theorem)
.

Let
i\in\mathrm{Emb}^{2}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
be an embedding such that
{\mathsf{M}}=i(M)
satisfies
(P1-4)
. Then, for all
\varepsilon>0
small enough, there exists a neighborhood
\mathcal{U}
of
i
in
\mathrm{Emb}^{2}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
equipped with the Whitney
C^{2}
-topology such that for every
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
, with
{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}=i^{\prime}(M)
:

1.

{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}
satisfies
(P1-4)
.

2.

There is a bijection
\Psi:Z({\mathsf{M}})\rightarrow Z({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})
.

3.

Let
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
; then
\Psi(z_{0})\in B(z_{0},\varepsilon)
.

4.

Let
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
with
\pi_{{\mathsf{M}}}(z_{0})=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{s}\}
. Then
\pi_{{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}}(\Psi(z_{0}))
is included in the union of balls of radius
\varepsilon
centered at the points
x_{j}
, with exactly one point in each ball.

Remark 1.5
.

We only ask that
\varepsilon
be small enough to ensure that the balls of point 4. do not intersect.

The
C^{2}
Stability Theorem shows that Properties
(P1-4)
, which we know to be generic in the sense of Theorem
1.1
, are desirable not only for themselves, but also for the stability of the critical points and of their projections with respect to
C^{2}
perturbations that they guarantee - various examples throughout this paper illustrate how removing some of the properties can lead to somewhat pathological instabilities.

Sampling theory on generic submanifolds:

The following theorem states that Properties
(P1-4)
also induce another type of stability, this time with respect to taking an
\varepsilon
-dense subset
{\mathsf{A}}
of the submanifold
{\mathsf{M}}
- a typical example would be for
{\mathsf{A}}
to be some point cloud sampled on
{\mathsf{M}}
.

Theorem 1.6

(Stability Theorem for Subsets)
.

Let
{\mathsf{M}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
be a compact
C^{2}
submanifold. There exist positive constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},C_{4},C_{5}
and
\varepsilon_{0}
such that the following holds. Let
{\mathsf{A}}\subset{\mathsf{M}}
be a set with
d_{H}({\mathsf{A}},{\mathsf{M}})\leq\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
.

1.

For every critical point
z\in Z({\mathsf{A}})
, exactly one of these two possibilities is true: either
z
is very close to
{\mathsf{M}}
, that is
d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)\leq C_{1}\varepsilon^{2}
, or it is close to a
\mu
-critical point of
{\mathsf{M}}
, that is
d_{Z_{\mu}({\mathsf{M}})}(z)\leq C_{2}\varepsilon
for
\mu=C_{3}\varepsilon
.

2.

If
{\mathsf{M}}
satisfies
(P4)
, then, in the second case, the critical point
z\in Z({\mathsf{A}})
is close to
Z({\mathsf{M}})
, that is
d_{Z({\mathsf{M}})}(z)\leq C_{4}\varepsilon
.

3.

If
{\mathsf{M}}
additionally satisfies conditions
(P1)
,
(P2)
and
(P3)
then, in the second case, the set of projections
\pi_{\mathsf{A}}(z)
of the critical point
z\in Z({\mathsf{A}})
is included in a neighborhood of size
C_{5}\varepsilon
of
\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z^{\prime})
for some
z^{\prime}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
such that
d(z,z^{\prime})\leq C_{4}\varepsilon
.

This is to be compared to the previously available guarantees for general compact sets with positive reach (see definition in Section
2
): let
{\mathsf{S}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
be a compact set with positive reach
\tau({\mathsf{S}})
, and let
{\mathsf{A}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
be such that
d_{H}({\mathsf{A}},{\mathsf{S}})\leq\varepsilon
.
Then Chazal, Cohen-Steiner and Lieutier
[
CCSL06
]
show that all critical points of
{\mathsf{A}}
are either at distance
4\varepsilon
from
{\mathsf{S}}
, or are at distance at least
\tau({\mathsf{S}})-3\varepsilon
from
{\mathsf{S}}
. Hence, assuming that
\varepsilon<\tau({\mathsf{S}})/7
, there are no critical values of
d_{\mathsf{A}}
in the interval
(4\varepsilon,\tau({\mathsf{S}})/2)
. In that case,
Equation

4
implies that the critical points of
{\mathsf{A}}
that are “far from
{\mathsf{S}}
” are only
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})
-close to
\mu
-critical points for
\mu=O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})
.

Hence the added hypotheses on
{\mathsf{M}}
yield several improvements with respect to the general case. Firstly,
3
3

3

This first point was well-known folklore, frequently mentioned by F. Chazal among others.
the critical points that are close to
{\mathsf{M}}
are at distance
O(\varepsilon^{2})
from
{\mathsf{M}}
instead of
O(\varepsilon)
.
Secondly, the stability with respect to
\mu
-critical points is also improved, going from
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})
to
O(\varepsilon)
for both the distance to
Z_{\mu}({\mathsf{M}})
and the value of
\mu
. Note that these two improvements are solely due to the set
{\mathsf{M}}
being a submanifold and to
{\mathsf{A}}
being a subset of
{\mathsf{M}}
, and hold without any of the conditions
(P1-4)
. Thirdly and finally, Properties
(P1-4)
allow us to get the
O(\varepsilon)
-stability of both critical points and their projections.

As a corollary to
1.6
, we get a bound on the number of critical points that a finite approximation (i.e. a point cloud)
{\mathsf{A}}
of
{\mathsf{M}}
can have.
We say that a finite subset
{\mathsf{A}}\subset{\mathsf{M}}
is a
(\delta,\varepsilon)
-sampling of
{\mathsf{M}}
if
d_{H}({\mathsf{A}},{\mathsf{M}})\leq\varepsilon
and
\min_{x\neq y\in{\mathsf{A}}}\|x-y\|\geq\delta
. For
\delta,\varepsilon>0
, the
\delta
-packing number
N(\delta,\varepsilon)
of
{\mathsf{M}}
is the maximal cardinality of a
(\delta,+\infty)
-sampling of a ball
B(x,\varepsilon)\cap{\mathsf{M}}
centered at a point
x\in{\mathsf{M}}
.

Corollary 1.7
.

Let
{\mathsf{M}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
be a compact
C^{2}
submanifold of dimension
m
satisfying
(P1-4)
. Consider the constants
C_{4}
and
C_{5}
from
1.6
. There exists
\varepsilon_{1}>0
such that if
{\mathsf{A}}
is a
(\delta,\varepsilon)
-sampling of
{\mathsf{M}}
for some
0<\delta\leq\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{1}
, and if

z\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
and
s
is the cardinality of the set of projections
\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z)
, then the number of critical points in
Z({\mathsf{A}})
at distance less than
C_{4}\varepsilon
from
z
is bounded by

2^{N(\delta,C_{5}\varepsilon){s}}
.
Furthermore

N(\delta,C_{5}\varepsilon)\leq(C_{m}C_{5}\varepsilon/\delta)^{m}

for some
C_{m}>0
that depends on
m
.

The practical applicability of
1.7
relies on the existence of
(\delta,\varepsilon)
-samplings with
\delta
of the same order as
\varepsilon
. The farthest point sampling algorithm
[
AL18
]
shows that such sets always exist: starting from a finite subset
{\mathsf{A}}_{0}\subset{\mathsf{M}}
with
d_{H}({\mathsf{A}}_{0},{\mathsf{M}})\leq\varepsilon
, it outputs a subset
{\mathsf{A}}_{1}\subset{\mathsf{A}}_{0}
that is an
(\varepsilon,2\varepsilon)
-sampling of
{\mathsf{M}}
. For such a subset
{\mathsf{A}}_{1}
,
1.7
ensures that the number of critical points at distance at least
O(\varepsilon^{2})
from
{\mathsf{M}}
is at most
K2^{(D+1)(2C_{m}C_{5})^{m}}
, where
K
is the cardinality of
Z({\mathsf{M}})
. In particular, this quantity stays bounded as
\varepsilon
goes to
0
.

Morse-like properties of the distance function to a generic submanifold:
Properties
(P1-4)
and the Genericity and
C^{2}
Stability Theorems were primarily motivated by considerations on the regularity and stability of the critical points of
{\mathsf{M}}
and of their projections.
However, Properties
(P1-4)
also happen to enforce a Morse-like behaviour on the evolution of the topology of the offsets
{\mathsf{M}}^{a}=\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}:\;d_{\mathsf{M}}(x)\leq a\}
.
Indeed, Properties
(P1-4)
are exactly those needed for the critical points of the distance function
d_{\mathsf{M}}
to be
Min-type non-degenerate critical points
as defined by V. Gershkovich and H. Rubinstein in
[
GR97
]
; if they are satisfied, it is easy to show that the restriction of
d_{\mathsf{M}}
to
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\backslash{\mathsf{M}}
is a
topological Morse function
, a natural generalization of the usual definition of (smooth) Morse functions that was first defined in
[
Mor59
]
.
A topological Morse function
f
is one such that for every point
x
of its domain
X
, there is a local homeomorphic change of coordinates
\phi:(U,0)\rightarrow(V,x)
with
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{D},V\subset X
such that either
f\circ\phi(y_{1},\ldots,y_{d})=f(x)+y_{d}
, in which case
x
is a
topological regular point
of
x
, or
f\circ\phi(y_{1},\ldots,y_{d})=f(x)-\sum_{i=1}^{\lambda}y_{i}^{2}+\sum_{i=\lambda+1}^{d}y_{i}^{2}
for some
\lambda\in\{0,\ldots,d\}
, in which case
x
is a
non-degenerate topological critical point of index
\lambda
of
f
(more details can be found in Section
7
).
In particular, topological Morse functions satisfy the same handle attachment property as classical Morse functions.
As a result, the following theorem holds.

Theorem 1.8

(Morse-Like Theorem for the Distance Function)
.

Let
{\mathsf{M}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
be a compact
C^{2}
submanifold satisfying
(P1-4)
.
Then the restriction
d_{\mathsf{M}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\backslash{\mathsf{M}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
of the distance function to
{\mathsf{M}}
is a topological Morse function, whose topological critical points are exactly
Z({\mathsf{M}})
.
It satisfies the two following properties:

(Isotopy Lemma)
Let
0\leq a<b
be such that
d_{\mathsf{M}}^{-1}[a,b]
contains no critical point. Then
{\mathsf{M}}^{a}
is a deformation retract of
{\mathsf{M}}^{b}
.

(Handle Attachment Lemma)

Let
c>0
and
0<\epsilon<c
be such that
d_{\mathsf{M}}^{-1}[c-\varepsilon,c+\varepsilon]
contains no critical point except for
z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}\in d_{\mathsf{M}}^{-1}(c)
, where
z_{j}
is of index
i_{j}
as a topological critical point.
Then
{\mathsf{M}}^{c+\varepsilon}
is homotopically equivalent to
{\mathsf{M}}^{c-\varepsilon}
with cells
D^{i_{1}},\ldots,D^{i_{k}}
of dimension
i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}
attached,
i.e.

{\mathsf{M}}^{c+\varepsilon}\simeq{\mathsf{M}}^{c-\varepsilon}\cup D^{i_{1}}\cup\ldots\cup D^{i_{k}}.

Similar observations have been made in the case of surfaces in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
in
[
SYM23
]
.
Note that without being restricted,
d_{\mathsf{M}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
cannot be a topological Morse function, as the points of
{\mathsf{M}}
themselves are degenerate topological critical points.
Note also that
{\mathsf{M}}
does not need to satisfy Conditions
(P1-4)
for the Isotopy Lemma to hold;
as mentioned above, it was shown in
[
Gro93
]
to be the case for any compact set when
a>0
, and the same arguments easily extend to the case
a\geq 0
when
{\mathsf{M}}
is any submanifold.
We nonetheless include this fact in the statement of the theorem to further stress the connection to usual Morse theory.

Thus, Properties
(P1-4)
grant us control over the changes of topology of the offsets of a generic submanifold; this has interesting consequences regarding the Čech persistence diagram of
{\mathsf{M}}
(see e.g.
[
CDSGO16
]
,
[
CVJ21
]
), a key object in topological data analysis, which will be further explored in future work.

1.1
Related work

Given a compact set
{\mathsf{A}}
, both the medial axis
\mathrm{Med}({\mathsf{A}})
and the skeleton
\mathrm{Sk}({\mathsf{A}})
(the set of centers of maximal balls not intersecting
{\mathsf{A}}
) are well studied in the literature
[
Gib00
,
GK04
,
Lie04
,
CS04
]
. The two concepts are tightly linked, as
\mathrm{Med}({\mathsf{A}})\subset\mathrm{Sk}({\mathsf{A}})\subset\overline{\mathrm{Med}({\mathsf{A}})}
, the latter inclusion being an equality in all “tame” cases. We refer to
[
ABE09
]
for a review of the (in)stability properties of the medial axis.
Regarding specifically the issue of the stability of the critical and
\mu
-critical points with respect to sampling, our main inspiration was
[
CCSL06
]
, which considers the case of general compact sets.

The genericity of transversality is another key theme of this article; the first transversality theorems for jets date back to Thom’s
[
Tho56
]
, later extended to multijets by J. Mather in
[
Mat70
]
.
Early work on the application of those theorems to the distance function in Euclidean space include
[
Loo74
]
and
[
Wal77
]
, which eventually led to the study of the properties of the skeleton
\mathrm{Sk}({\mathsf{M}})
of a generic manifold
{\mathsf{M}}
by Yomdin
[
Yom81
]
, who proves among other results that the set of embeddings
i
of a manifold
M
such that for all
z\in\mathrm{Sk}(i(M))
, the projection set
\pi_{i(M)}(z)
is a non-degenerate simplex, is dense (and in fact residual), then by Mather
[
Mat83
]
. Later, J. Damon and E. Gasparovic gave in a series of papers (see
[
Dam97
,
DG17
]
and references therein) a thorough analysis of the geometry of the skeleton set for generic sets in different contexts.
Very recently, A. Song, K.M. Yim and A. Monod
[
SYM23
]
studied the set of critical points of a generic submanifold, though only for surfaces
{\mathsf{S}}
embedded into
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
and only in terms of density (without openness and stability), with a greater focus on Morse-like properties of the distance function.

1.2
Outline

We recall a few definitions and prove an elementary lemma in
Section

2
, before studying in detail the non-oscularity condition
(P3)
in
Section

3
; in particular, we rephrase it as a curvature condition.
In
Section

4
, we prove some properties of the projection on a submanifold
{\mathsf{M}}
wherever it is defined and in particular under some non-oscularity assumptions; we are also interested in its stability with respect to perturbations of
{\mathsf{M}}
.
Later in
Section

5
, we use some of our findings to prove the Stability Theorem for Subsets
1.6
.
In
Section

6
, we introduce a condition that we call the Big Simplex Property
(BSP)
that is equivalent to
(P4)
, but has the advantage of being phrased in terms of the local behaviour of
{\mathsf{M}}
around the projections of its critical points.
This helps us prove
1.8
in
Section

7
using the theory of Min-type functions developed in
[
GR97
]
, as well as the
C^{2}
Stability Theorem
1.4
in
Section

8
, which implies in particular the openness of the conjunction of Conditions
(P1-4)
.
Finally, the last section is dedicated to proving the density part of the statement of the Genericity Theorem
1.1
; it starts with some exposition, where we introduce variants of Thom’s transversality theorem and some necessary notions regarding multijets, before moving on to the proof proper.

Throughout the paper, we always assume that the dimension
m
of the manifold
M
is greater than or equal to
1
, and that the dimension
D
of the ambient Euclidean space
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
is greater than or equal to
2
, as otherwise all statements in the Introduction are trivially true.

2
Notations, definitions and preliminaries

For
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we let
[s]
be the set
\{1,\dots,s\}
.
We let
B(x,r)
be the open ball centered at
x
of radius
r
and
\overline{B}(x,r)
be its closure; we write
S(x,r)
for the sphere centered at
x
of radius
r
. For a set
{\mathsf{S}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, the
reach of
{\mathsf{S}}
is defined as

\tau({\mathsf{S}}):=\sup\{r\in{\mathbb{R}}:\;\forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\text{ s.t. }d_{\mathsf{S}}(x)<r,\;\exists!y\in{\mathsf{S}}\text{ s.t. }d(x,y)=d_{\mathsf{S}}(x)\}.

Note that if
{\mathsf{S}}
has positive reach, then it is necessarily closed. Sets with positive reach are quite regular in many ways; they were extensively studied by H. Federer in
[
Fed59
]
.

Throughout this article, we consider a fixed compact
C^{k}
abstract
m
-dimensional manifold
M
for some
k\in\{2,3,\dots,\infty\}
and
m\geq 1
,
4
4

4

The different theorems stated in the introduction can easily be shown to be true for
m=0
.
together with a fixed finite atlas
\mathcal{A}=(\phi_{j},U_{j})_{j\in J}
. For
1\leq l\leq k
, we consider the set
C^{l}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
of functions of class
C^{l}
from
M
to
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, and for
1\leq p\leq l
, if
f\in C^{l}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
, we let

\|f\|_{C^{p}}:=\max_{0\leq r\leq p}\max_{j\in J}\sup_{u\in U_{j}}\left\|d^{r}(f\circ\phi_{j})_{u}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}

be the
C^{p}
-norm of
f
, where
d^{r}g_{u}
is the differential of order
r
of a function
g
at the point
u
. We can always assume this quantity to be finite, up to restricting each chart to a slightly smaller subset of its domain. This norm defines a topology on
C^{l}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
, called the
Whitney
C^{p}
-topology
. This topology is independent of the choice of the atlas, and the Whitney
C^{p_{2}}
-topology is finer than the Whitney
C^{p_{1}}
-topology on
C^{l}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
for any
p_{1}\leq p_{2}\leq l
. If
k=\infty
, the union of all the Whitney
C^{p}
-topologies on
C^{\infty}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
for
p\geq 1
defines the Whitney
C^{\infty}
-topology (i.e. the set of open sets of the Whitney
C^{\infty}
-topology is the union over
p\geq 1
of the sets of open sets of the Whitney
C^{p}
-topologies).
For
1\leq l\leq k
, we let
\mathrm{Emb}^{l}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
be the subset of
C^{l}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
consisting of all
C^{l}
embeddings (immersions that are homeomorphic to their image). It is an open set for the Whitney
C^{1}
-topology (and hence for all Whitney
C^{p}
-topologies for
1\leq p\leq l
), as shown in
[
Mic80
, Prop 5.3]
.

Another, more intrinsic definition (based on jets) of the Whitney
C^{p}
-topology is given in
Section

9.1
; in particular, it extends to the case where the domain of the mappings is another
C^{k}
manifold
N
, rather than the Euclidean space
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
. See also
[
GG74
]
for a more complete presentation.

If
i\in\mathrm{Emb}^{l}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
for
l\geq 2
, then
{\mathsf{M}}=i(M)
is a compact
C^{2}
submanifold of
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
.
For
x\in{\mathsf{M}}
, we let
T_{x}{\mathsf{M}}
be the tangent space of
{\mathsf{M}}
at
x
, and
T_{x}{\mathsf{M}}^{\bot}
the normal space, that are endowed with the norm induced by the ambient Euclidean space. When there is no possible ambiguity, we let
\pi_{x}:{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\rightarrow T_{x}{\mathsf{M}}
be the orthogonal projection on
T_{x}{\mathsf{M}}
and
\pi_{x}^{\bot}:{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\rightarrow T_{x}{\mathsf{M}}^{\bot}
be the orthogonal projection on
T_{x}{\mathsf{M}}^{\bot}
. For
\eta\in T_{x}{\mathsf{M}}^{\bot}
, we let
W_{x,\eta}:T_{x}{\mathsf{M}}\to T_{x}{\mathsf{M}}
be the shape operator in the normal direction
\eta
. This is a self-adjoint operator such that
\langle W_{x,\eta}(v),v\rangle
is the normal curvature in the normal direction
\eta\in T_{x}{\mathsf{M}}^{\bot}
along the tangent vector
v\in T_{x}{\mathsf{M}}
, see
[
Lee18
, Chapter 8]
.

Finally, we will make frequent use of the following elementary lemma.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
{\mathsf{S}}
be any compact set.
Let
t_{0}>0
and let
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{S}})
. Then, there exists
r_{0}>0
such that all the points in
B(z_{0},d_{\mathsf{S}}(z_{0})+r_{0})\cap{\mathsf{S}}
are at distance less than
t_{0}
from the projections
\pi_{\mathsf{S}}(z_{0})
.

Proof.

Assume that the statement is false. Then, there exist sequences
r_{n}\to 0
and
x_{n}\in{\mathsf{S}}
, such that
\|x_{n}-z_{0}\|<d_{\mathsf{S}}(z_{0})+r_{n}
, but
x_{n}
is at distance at least
t_{0}
from
\pi_{\mathsf{S}}(z_{0})
. By compactness, we can assume without loss of generality that
x_{n}\to x\in{\mathsf{S}}
. But by continuity, we have
\|x-z\|\leq d_{\mathsf{S}}(z_{0})
, so that
x\in\pi_{\mathsf{S}}(z_{0})
, contradicting the condition that all the points
x_{n}
are at distance at least
t_{0}
from
\pi_{\mathsf{S}}(z_{0})
.
∎

3
Osculation

We fix an embedding
i\in\mathrm{Emb}^{2}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
, and consider the compact
C^{2}
submanifold
{\mathsf{M}}=i(M)
.
In this section, we give several equivalent formulations of the condition of non-osculation from Property
(P3)
.
Some of the material presented here is already well-known; as the proofs are short, we nonetheless include them for the sake of completeness.

If
z_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\backslash{\mathsf{M}}
and
x_{0}\in\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})
, then the sphere of radius
d_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})
centered at
z_{0}
is tangent to the manifold
{\mathsf{M}}
at the point
x_{0}
. As the inside of this sphere does not intersect
{\mathsf{M}}
, the curvature at
x_{0}
in the normal direction
z_{0}-x_{0}
has to be smaller than or equal to the one of the sphere along any tangent vector of
T_{x_{0}}{\mathsf{M}}
. Two different situations can arise: either the curvature of the manifold is strictly smaller than the one of the sphere (as will be shown later, this is generically the case whenever
z_{0}
is a critical point of
{\mathsf{M}}
), or the curvature of the manifold in the normal direction
\eta:=z_{0}-x_{0}
and in at least one of the directions of
T_{x_{0}}{\mathsf{M}}
is equal to the sphere’s, that is there exists
v\in T_{x}{\mathsf{M}}
with
\langle W_{x_{0},\eta}(v),v\rangle=\|v\|^{2}
. In that case, we say that the sphere of radius
d_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})
centered at
z_{0}
is
osculating
the manifold
{\mathsf{M}}
at
x_{0}
. Both situations are illustrated in Figure
3
.

Figure 3:
The sphere centered at
z_{0}
of radius
d_{{\mathsf{M}}}(z_{0})
is osculating
{\mathsf{M}}
at
x_{0}
, but not at
x_{1}
.

In the following lemma, we show that this condition is equivalent to a purely metric one, and that due to the
C^{2}
regularity of
{\mathsf{M}}
, this metric condition is equivalent to a local version of itself.
Finally, though the non-osculation of
S(z_{0},d_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0}))
at
x_{0}
only depends on
{\mathsf{M}}
, and not on the embedding
i
such that
{\mathsf{M}}=i(M)
, it will be convenient later on to have a formulation of the condition in terms of
i
, which is also provided by the lemma.

Lemma 3.1

(Osculation Characterization Lemma)
.

Let
z_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\backslash{\mathsf{M}}
,
x_{0}\in\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})
and define
\eta:=z_{0}-x_{0}
. Then the sphere of radius
d_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})
centered at
z_{0}
not osculating the submanifold
{\mathsf{M}}
at
x_{0}
is equivalent to the existence of
\alpha>0
such that one of the following equivalent conditions holds:

(i)

The largest eigenvalue
\lambda_{\max}(W_{x_{0},\eta})
of the shape operator
W_{x_{0},\eta}:T_{x_{0}}{\mathsf{M}}\to T_{x_{0}}{\mathsf{M}}
at
x_{0}
in the direction
\eta
satisfies
\lambda_{\max}(W_{x_{0},\eta})<1-\alpha
.

(ii)

Let
m_{0}\in M
be such that
i(m_{0})=x_{0}
, with
(\phi,U)
a chart of
M
around
m_{0}
with
\phi(u_{0})=m_{0}
.
Then, the quadratic form

\begin{split}{\mathbb{R}}^{m}&\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}\\
v&\longmapsto(1-\alpha)\|d(i\circ\phi)_{u_{0}}(v)\|^{2}-\langle\eta,d^{2}(i\circ\phi)_{u_{0}}(v,v)\rangle\end{split}

is positive definite.

(iii)

There exists
\alpha^{\prime}>\alpha
and
\delta>0
such that, if
{\mathsf{M}}_{0}:={\mathsf{M}}\cap\overline{B}(x_{0},\delta)
, then for any
y\in{\mathsf{M}}_{0}
,

\|y-z_{0}\|^{2}\geq\|x_{0}-z_{0}\|^{2}+\alpha^{\prime}\|y-x_{0}\|^{2}.

(6)

(iv)

There exists
\alpha^{\prime}>\alpha
and
\delta,\delta^{\prime}>0
such that, if
{\mathsf{M}}_{0}:={\mathsf{M}}\cap\overline{B}(x_{0},\delta)
, then for any
z\in B(z_{0},\delta^{\prime})
,
x\in\pi_{{\mathsf{M}}_{0}}(z)
and
y\in{\mathsf{M}}_{0}
, it holds that

\|y-z\|^{2}\geq\|x-z\|^{2}+\alpha^{\prime}\|y-x\|^{2}.

(7)

When the sphere of radius
d_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})
centered at
z_{0}
does not osculate
{\mathsf{M}}
at
x_{0}
, we call a number
\alpha>0
that satisfies the conditions of Lemma
3.1

a degree of non-osculation at
x_{0}
of the sphere
S(z_{0},d_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0}))
.

Proof.

The equivalence between the sphere being non-osculating and the existence of some
\alpha>0
that satisfies condition
(i)
is clear. The equivalence between
(i)
and
(ii)
follows from the fact that, if
w=d(i\circ\phi)_{u_{0}}(v)
, then

\langle W_{x_{0},\eta}(w),w\rangle=\langle\eta,d^{2}(i\circ\phi)_{u_{0}}(v,v)\rangle,

(8)

as
\eta\in T_{x_{0}}{\mathsf{M}}^{\top}
(see
[
Lee18
, Chapter 8]
).

Let us now show the equivalence between
(ii)
and
(iii)
.
Observe that for any
\alpha^{\prime}>0
and any
y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
,

\|y-z_{0}\|^{2}-\alpha^{\prime}\|y-x_{0}\|^{2}-\|\eta\|^{2}=(1-\alpha^{\prime})\|y-x_{0}\|^{2}-2\langle\eta,y-x_{0}\rangle.

Let
i,\phi
and
u_{0}
be as in the statement of condition
(ii)
; then condition
(iii)
is equivalent to the existence of some
\alpha^{\prime}>\alpha
such that

(1-\alpha^{\prime})\|i\circ\phi(u)-x_{0}\|^{2}-2\langle\eta,i\circ\phi(u)-x_{0}\rangle>0

for any
u\neq u_{0}
close enough to
u_{0}
.
But, as
i\circ\phi
is
C^{2}
and
\mathrm{Im}(d(i\circ\phi)_{u_{0}})=T_{i\circ\phi(u_{0})}{\mathsf{M}}
is orthogonal to
\eta
, it holds that

\displaystyle(1-\alpha^{\prime})\|i\circ\phi(u)-x_{0}\|^{2}-2\langle\eta,i\circ\phi(u)-x_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad=(1-\alpha^{\prime})\|d(i\circ\phi)_{u_{0}}(u-u_{0})\|^{2}-\langle\eta,d^{2}(i\circ\phi)_{u_{0}}(u-u_{0},u-u_{0})\rangle+o(\|u-u_{0}\|^{2}).

Hence, the existence of some
\alpha^{\prime}>\alpha
such that
(1-\alpha^{\prime})\|d(i\circ\phi)_{u_{0}}(v)\|^{2}-\langle\eta,d^{2}(i\circ\phi)_{u_{0}}(v,v)\rangle+o(\|v\|^{2})
is strictly positive for
v\neq 0
small enough is equivalent to the form

v\longmapsto(1-\alpha)\|d(i\circ\phi)_{u_{0}}(v)\|^{2}-\langle\eta,d^{2}(i\circ\phi)_{u_{0}}(v,v)\rangle

being positive definite (due to
d(i\circ\phi)_{u_{0}}
being an isomorphism). This shows that conditions
(ii)
and
(iii)
are equivalent.

Condition
(iv)
obviously implies condition
(iii)
.
It remains to show that
(iii)
implies
(iv)
. We postpone the proof to Section
4
, in Corollary
4.7
.
∎

In the next section, we show, among other things, that the non-oscularity with respect to
{\mathsf{M}}
of the sphere
S(z_{0},d_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0}))
at
x_{0}\in\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})
gives nice regularity and stability properties to the projection map around
z_{0}
on a neighborhood of
x_{0}
.

4
Regularity and stability of projections

Let as earlier
i\in\mathrm{Emb}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
be a
C^{2}
embedding of the abstract manifold
M
, and
{\mathsf{M}}:=i(M)
be the resulting submanifold.
In this section, we prove various statements regarding the projections on
{\mathsf{M}}
or on some subsets of
{\mathsf{M}}
, particularly under some assumptions of non-osculation. These results will be instrumental later in the article.

4.1
Lipschitz continuity and local unicity of the projection

The following lemma states that if the sphere
S(z_{0},d_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0}))
is not osculating
{\mathsf{M}}
at
x_{0}\in\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})
, then one can define a Lipschitz projection function from a neighborhood of
z_{0}
to a neighborhood of
x_{0}
in
{\mathsf{M}}
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
z_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\backslash{\mathsf{M}}
and
x_{0}\in\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})
. Assume that the sphere
S(z_{0},d_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0}))
does not osculate
{\mathsf{M}}
at
x_{0}
, with
\alpha>0
a degree of non-osculation. Let
\delta,\delta^{\prime}>0
be the constants given in
Lemma

3.1
.
(iv)
. If
{\mathsf{M}}_{0}={\mathsf{M}}\cap\overline{B}(x_{0},\delta)
and
z\in B(z_{0},\delta^{\prime})
, then
\pi_{{\mathsf{M}}_{0}}(z)
contains exactly one element
x
, which satisfies

\|x-x_{0}\|\leq\frac{1}{\alpha}\|z-z_{0}\|.

(9)

Proof.

Let
\delta,\delta^{\prime}>0
and
\alpha^{\prime}>\alpha
be the constants given in
Lemma

3.1
.
(iv)
.
Let
x\in\pi_{{\mathsf{M}}_{0}}(z)
.
Lemma

3.1
.
(iv)
implies that every point
y\in{\mathsf{M}}_{0}
different from
x
satisfies
\|y-z\|>\|x-z\|
. This proves that
\pi_{{\mathsf{M}}_{0}}(z)
contains exactly one element
x
. Let us prove the second claim. Assume that
x\neq x_{0}
, for otherwise the claim is trivial. According to (
7
) with
y=x_{0}
,

\displaystyle\|x_{0}-z\|^{2}\geq\|x-z\|^{2}+\alpha^{\prime}\|x_{0}-x\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\|x-x_{0}\|^{2}+\|x_{0}-z\|^{2}+2\langle x-x_{0},x_{0}-z\rangle+\alpha^{\prime}\|x_{0}-x\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\|x-x_{0}\|^{2}+\|x_{0}-z\|^{2}+2\langle x-x_{0},x_{0}-z_{0}\rangle+2\langle x-x_{0},z_{0}-z\rangle+\alpha^{\prime}\|x_{0}-x\|^{2},

that is, using Cauchy-Schwartz inequality

(1+\alpha^{\prime})\|x-x_{0}\|^{2}\leq-2\langle x-x_{0},x_{0}-z_{0}\rangle+2\|x-x_{0}\|\|z_{0}-z\|.

(10)

Also, we may use (
7
) with
y=x
and
x=x_{0}
to obtain

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}\|x_{0}-x\|^{2}+\|x_{0}-z_{0}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|x-z_{0}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}\|x_{0}-x\|^{2}+\|x_{0}-z_{0}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|x-x_{0}\|^{2}+\|x_{0}-z_{0}\|^{2}+2\langle x-x_{0},x_{0}-z_{0}\rangle.

that is

-2\langle x-x_{0},x_{0}-z_{0}\rangle<(1-\alpha^{\prime})\|x_{0}-x\|^{2}.

(11)

Putting together (
10
) and (
11
) yields

2\alpha^{\prime}\|x-x_{0}\|^{2}\leq 2\|x-x_{0}\|\|z_{0}-z\|.

Dividing by
2\alpha^{\prime}\|x-x_{0}\|
and using that
\alpha^{\prime}>\alpha
give the result.
∎

4.2
Differential of the projection

The following proposition, which can be found e.g. in
[
LS21
]
, describes the differential of the projection on
{\mathsf{M}}
wherever it is well-defined.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
\overline{\mathrm{Med}}({\mathsf{M}})
be the closure of the medial axis of
{\mathsf{M}}
, and let
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\backslash({\mathsf{M}}\cup\overline{\mathrm{Med}}({\mathsf{M}}))
. Then the projection on
{\mathsf{M}}
uniquely defines a
C^{1}
map
p
on a neighborhood of
z
, and the differential of
p
in
z
is given by

dp_{z}=\left(\mathrm{id}_{T_{p(z)}{\mathsf{M}}}-W_{p(z),z-p(z)}\right)^{-1}\circ\pi_{p(z)}:{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\rightarrow T_{p(z)}{\mathsf{M}},

where we recall that
\pi_{p(z)}:{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\rightarrow T_{p(z)}{\mathsf{M}}
is the orthogonal projection onto
T_{p(z)}{\mathsf{M}}
.

The following lemma serves two purposes: it shows that under simple conditions, the projection at
z_{0}
on the image of the germ of a function
f:M\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
(rather than on the image of some embedding
i:M\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
) and the differential of that projection are well-defined, and in that context it expresses the formula for the differential of the projection given in Proposition
4.2
in terms of coordinates of charts of
M
.
5
5

5

Such an expression must be available somewhere in the literature, but we could not find it.
This will be needed when working with jets in Section
9
; sadly, it involves some rather tedious verifications.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
m_{0}\in M
and
x_{0},z_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
and let
f\in C^{2}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
be some function (not necessarily an embedding) that maps
m_{0}
to
x_{0}
.
Furthermore, suppose that

df_{m_{0}}
has full rank and that
\mathrm{Im}(df_{m_{0}})\perp z_{0}-x_{0}
. Let
\phi:(V,u_{0})\rightarrow(M,m_{0})
be any chart of
M
around
m_{0}
.
If the quadratic form

\begin{split}{\mathbb{R}}^{m}&\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}\\
v&\longmapsto\|d(f\circ\phi)_{u_{0}}(v)\|^{2}-\langle z_{0}-x_{0},d^{2}(f\circ\phi)_{u_{0}}(v,v)\rangle\end{split}

is positive definite,
then there exist
\delta>0
and a neighborhood
U\subset M
of
m_{0}
such that

•

f|_{U}:U\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
is an embedding,

•

each
z\in B(z_{0},\delta)
admits a unique projection on
\overline{f(U)}
and that unique projection belongs to
f(U)
, and

•

this unique projection defines a
C^{1}
map
p:B(z_{0},\delta)\to f(U)
.

In that case, let as above
\phi:(V,{u_{0}})\rightarrow(M,m_{0})
be any chart of
M
around
m_{0}
. For any
v\in\mathrm{Im}(df_{m_{0}})
, let
\Lambda(v)\in\mathrm{Im}(df_{m_{0}})
be the only vector in
\mathrm{Im}(df_{m_{0}})
such that

\langle z_{0}-x_{0},d^{2}(f\circ\phi)_{u_{0}}(d(f\circ\phi)_{u_{0}}^{-1}(v),d(f\circ\phi)_{u_{0}}^{-1}(w))\rangle=\langle\Lambda(v),w\rangle

for any
w\in\mathrm{Im}(df_{m_{0}})
.
This defines a linear endomorphism
\Lambda:\mathrm{Im}(df_{m_{0}})\rightarrow\mathrm{Im}(df_{m_{0}})
that is independent from the choice of the chart
\phi
, and

\begin{split}Q:\mathrm{Im}(df_{m_{0}})&\longrightarrow\mathrm{Im}(df_{m_{0}})\\
v&\longmapsto v-\Lambda(v)\end{split}

is a linear isomorphism.

The differential at
z_{0}
of the map
p
is then equal to

dp_{z_{0}}=Q^{-1}\circ\pi_{\mathrm{Im}(df_{m_{0}})}:{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\rightarrow\mathrm{Im}(df_{m_{0}}),

where
\pi_{\mathrm{Im}(df_{m_{0}})}:{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\rightarrow\mathrm{Im}(df_{m_{0}})
is the orthogonal projection.

Remark 4.4
.

As shown in the proof below, the linear map
\Lambda
is simply the shape operator at
x_{0}
in normal direction
z_{0}-x_{0}
of the submanifold
f(U)
.

Proof.

The fact that
f|_{U}:U\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
is an embedding for a small enough neighborhood
U
of
m_{0}
is simply a consequence of the full rank of
df_{m_{0}}
. Let us choose such a neighborhood.

If the form
v\mapsto\|d(f\circ\phi)_{u_{0}}(v)\|^{2}-\langle z_{0}-x_{0},d^{2}(f\circ\phi)_{u_{0}}(v,v)\rangle
is positive definite for a given chart
\phi:(V,{u_{0}})\rightarrow(M,m_{0})
of
M
around
m_{0}
(we can assume that
\phi(V)\subset U
), it is so for any such chart (as
\mathrm{Im}(df_{m_{0}})\perp z_{0}-x_{0}
). In that case, consider the function

g:V\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}},u\mapsto\|f\circ\phi(u)-z_{0}\|^{2}
. Its first differential at
{u_{0}}
is
0
, as
\mathrm{Im}(df_{m_{0}})\perp z_{0}-x_{0}
, and its second differential is

(v_{1},v_{2})\mapsto 2(\langle d(f\circ\phi)_{u_{0}}(v_{1}),d(f\circ\phi)_{u_{0}}(v_{2})\rangle-\langle z_{0}-x_{0},d^{2}(f\circ\phi)_{u_{0}}(v_{1},v_{2})\rangle).

By hypothesis, the associated quadratic form is positive definite, hence
{u_{0}}
is a strict local minimum of
g
, and
x_{0}
is a strict local minimum of the distance function to
z_{0}
restricted to
f(U)
. By making the neighborhood
U
of
m_{0}
in
M
smaller, we can thus assume that
x_{0}
is the unique projection of
z_{0}
on
\overline{f(U)}
.

Furthermore, we have shown that the sphere
S(z_{0},d_{\overline{f(U)}}(z_{0}))
does not osculate
\overline{f(U)}
at
x_{0}

(our definition of osculation naturally extends to this case), using characterization
(ii)
from the Osculation Lemma
3.1
.
If
\overline{f(U)}
were a submanifold (without boundary), we could now use the Lipschitz continuity of the projection from
Lemma

4.1
to conclude that there exists
\delta>0
such that each
z\in B(z_{0},\delta)
admits a unique projection
p(z)
on
\overline{f(U)}
that belongs to
f(U)
(possibly by making
U
yet smaller). This would also mean that
B(z_{0},\delta)
would be included in the interior of the complement of the medial axis of
\overline{f(U)}
, hence the projection map
p:B(z_{0},\delta)\rightarrow f(U)
would be
C^{1}
with its differential given by
Proposition

4.2
.
One can see that the argument still works in the same way despite the boundary.
Let
\delta
(and
U
) satisfy the conditions above.

The only part of the proof left is to adapt the formula for the differential of the projection from Proposition
4.2
to express
dp_{z_{0}}
in the coordinates of
f\circ\phi|_{V}
.
Let us show that
\Lambda
is indeed the shape operator at
x_{0}
in normal direction
z_{0}-x_{0}
of
f(U)
, as claimed in the remark above.
In
[
DCF92
, p. 126]
, a bilinear map
B:\mathrm{Im}(df_{m_{0}})\times\mathrm{Im}(df_{m_{0}})\rightarrow\mathrm{Im}(df_{m_{0}})^{\perp}
is defined as follows: for
w_{2},w_{2}\in\mathrm{Im}(df_{m_{0}})=T_{x_{0}}f(U)
, let
W_{1},W_{2}:f(U)\supset\tilde{U}\rightarrow Tf(U)
be vector fields on the submanifold
f(U)
defined around
x_{0}
such that
W_{1}(x_{0})=w_{1}
and
W_{2}(x_{0})=w_{2}
, and let
\overline{W_{1}}
and
\overline{W_{2}}
be local extensions of
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
to
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
.
Then

B(w_{1},w_{2})=\pi_{T_{x_{0}}f(U)^{\perp}}(d(\overline{W_{1}})_{x_{0}}(\overline{W_{2}}(x_{0}))),

where
\pi_{T_{x_{0}}f(U)^{\perp}}
is the orthogonal projection on
T_{x_{0}}f(U)^{\perp}
. The same reference shows that
B(w_{1},w_{2})
does not depend on the choice of
W_{1},W_{2},\overline{W_{1}},\overline{W_{2}}
and is symmetric.

Let us express
B
in terms of the embedding
f|_{U}
. We can assume without loss of generality that
T_{x_{0}}f(U)={\mathbb{R}}^{m}\times\{0\}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
and
x_{0}=0
. Then the projection
\pi:{\mathbb{R}}^{m}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D-m}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
restricted to
f(U)
is a diffeomorphism around
0
, hence
\pi\circ f:U\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
is locally the inverse of a chart
\psi:(\tilde{V},0)\rightarrow(M,m_{0})
of
M
around
m_{0}
. Around
0\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, the submanifold
f(U)
is parameterized as the graph of the map
f\circ\psi:{\mathbb{R}}^{m}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
.

Now let
w_{1},w_{2}\in T_{x_{0}}f(U)
. We define a vector field
W_{i}
for
i=1,2
as follows:

\begin{split}\overline{W_{i}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{D}={\mathbb{R}}^{m}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D-m}&\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\\
(x,y)&\longmapsto d(f\circ\psi)_{x}((d(f\circ\psi)_{0})^{-1}(w_{i})).\end{split}

Then the vector fields
\overline{W_{i}}
are such that their restrictions to
f(U)
take values in the tangent bundle of
f(U)
, and such that
\overline{W_{i}}(0)=w_{i}
. By definition,

\displaystyle B(w_{1},w_{2})=\pi_{T_{0}f(U)^{\perp}}(d(\overline{W_{1}})_{0}(\overline{W_{2}}(0)))=\pi_{T_{0}f(U)^{\perp}}(d^{2}(f\circ\psi)_{0}((d(f\circ\psi)_{0})^{-1}(w_{1}),w_{2}))

\displaystyle=\pi_{T_{0}f(U)^{\perp}}(d^{2}(f\circ\psi)_{0}((d(f\circ\psi)_{0})^{-1}(w_{1}),(d(f\circ\psi)_{0})^{-1}(w_{2})))

where the last equality is true because
d(f\circ\psi)_{0}
is the inclusion
{\mathbb{R}}^{m}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{m}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
.
In Definition 2.2 of
[
DCF92
, Chapter 6]
, the shape operator at
x_{0}=0
in normal direction
z_{0}-x_{0}
of the submanifold
f(U)
is defined as the linear operator
W_{0,z_{0}-x_{0}}:T_{0}f(U)\rightarrow T_{0}f(U)
such that

\langle W_{0,z_{0}-x_{0}}(w_{1}),w_{2}\rangle=\langle z_{0}-x_{0},B(w_{1},w_{2})\rangle

for any
w_{1},w_{2}\in T_{0}f(U)
, i.e. such that

\langle W_{0,z_{0}-x_{0}}(w_{1}),w_{2}\rangle=\langle z_{0}-x_{0},d^{2}(f\circ\psi)_{0}((d(f\circ\psi)_{0})^{-1}(w_{1}),(d(f\circ\psi)_{0})^{-1}(w_{2}))\rangle.

This is precisely the definition that we gave of the operator
\Lambda
, hence
\Lambda=W_{0,z_{0}-x_{0}}
is the shape operator in normal direction
z_{0}-x_{0}
at
x_{0}
. The fact that
\Lambda
does not depend on the choice of the chart
\psi
can then be deduced either from the fact that the shape operator does not depend on the choice of the vector fields
\overline{W_{i}}
(as shown in
[
DCF92
]
), or by direct computation using the expression
\langle z_{0}-x_{0},d^{2}(f\circ\psi)_{0}((d(f\circ\psi)_{0})^{-1}(w_{1}),(d(f\circ\psi)_{0})^{-1}(w_{2}))\rangle
.

Proposition
4.2
then states the invertibility of
Q=\mathrm{id}-\Lambda
and the fact that
dp_{z_{0}}=Q^{-1}\circ\pi_{\mathrm{Im}(df_{m_{0}})}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

4.3
C^{2}
stability of the projection

In this subsection, we show that a non-osculatory projection on
i(M)
behaves nicely locally, and that this is stable with respect to small
C^{2}
perturbations of the embedding
i
. In particular, we finish the proof of
Lemma

3.1
.
We first prove an almost trivial lemma.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
V\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
be an open set, let
\Lambda
be a topological space and let
F:V\times\Lambda\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
be a continuous function. For
\lambda\in\Lambda
, write
F_{\lambda}:v\mapsto F(v,\lambda)
and assume that the second differential

V\times\Lambda\rightarrow\mathrm{Bil}({\mathbb{R}}^{m},{\mathbb{R}}^{m},{\mathbb{R}}),\ (v,\lambda)\mapsto d^{2}(F_{\lambda})_{v}

of
F
with respect to
v\in V
exists and is continuous with respect to
(v,\lambda)\in V\times\Lambda
. Assume further that the map
\Lambda\rightarrow C^{0}(V,{\mathbb{R}}),\lambda\mapsto F_{\lambda}
is continuous with respect to the
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
norm, that
d^{2}(F_{\lambda_{0}})_{v_{0}}
is positive definite for some
v_{0}\in V
,
\lambda_{0}\in\Lambda
and that
v_{0}
is a global minimum for
F_{\lambda_{0}}
.
Then there exist open neighborhoods
U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{m}}\subset V
of
v_{0}
and
U_{\Lambda}\subset\Lambda
of
\lambda_{0}
such that
\overline{U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{m}}}\subset V
, that for any
\lambda\in U_{\Lambda}
the function
F_{\lambda}
admits exactly one minimum
v_{\lambda}
on
\overline{U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{m}}}
with
v_{\lambda}\in U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{m}}
and that
d^{2}(F_{\lambda})_{v}
is positive definite for any
v\in U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{m}},\lambda\in U_{\Lambda}
.

Proof.

By assumption the second differential
d(v,\lambda)\mapsto d^{2}(F_{\lambda})_{v}
is continuous in
(v,\lambda)\in V\times\Lambda
, and being definite positive is an open property, hence there exists an open neighborhood of
(v_{0},\lambda_{0})
on which
d^{2}(F_{\lambda})_{v}
is positive definite. By restricting it, we can assume that it is of the shape
U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{m}}\times U_{\Lambda}\subset V\times\Lambda
and that
U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{m}}
is a convex subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
. For any fixed
\lambda\in U_{\Lambda}
, the second differential
d^{2}(F_{\lambda})_{v}
in
v
of the function
F_{\lambda}:U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{m}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
is positive definite, hence
F_{\lambda}
is strictly convex and admits at most one minimum
v_{\lambda}
on the convex set
U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{m}}
.

As
v_{0}
is a minimum for
F_{\lambda_{0}}
and
d^{2}(F_{\lambda_{0}})_{v_{0}}
is positive definite, there exist open neighborhoods
U_{1},U_{2}
of
v_{0}
and
\beta>0
such that
U_{1}\subset U_{2}\subset\overline{U_{2}}\subset U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{m}}
, that
U_{2}
is convex and that
F_{\lambda_{0}}(v_{0})<\min\{F_{\lambda_{0}}(x):\ x\in\overline{U_{2}}\backslash U_{1}\}-\beta
. By choosing
U_{\Lambda}
small enough that
\|F_{\lambda}-F_{\lambda_{0}}\|_{\infty}<\beta/4
for any
\lambda\in U_{\Lambda}
, we have that
F_{\lambda}(v_{0})<\min\{F_{\lambda}(x):\ x\in\overline{U_{2}}\backslash U_{1}\}-\beta/2
for such a
\lambda\in U_{\Lambda}
. Thus
F_{\lambda}
admits a minimum
v_{\lambda}
on
\overline{U_{2}}
and
v_{\lambda}
must belong to
U_{1}\subset U_{2}
. As
U_{2}
is convex, the argument used above applies again and this minimum must be unique. By redefining
U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{m}}
as its subset
U_{2}
, we conclude that
F_{\lambda}
does admit exactly one minimum
v_{\lambda}
on
\overline{U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{m}}}
with
v_{\lambda}\in U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{m}}
.
∎

Now we specialize the lemma above to the special case of interest to us.

Proposition 4.6

(
C^{2}
stability of the projection)
.

Let
i_{0}\in\mathrm{Emb}^{2}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
and
z_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
. Let
x_{0}=i_{0}(m_{0})
be a projection of
z_{0}
on
i_{0}(M)
such that
S(z_{0},d_{i_{0}(M)}(z_{0}))
is non-osculating
i_{0}(M)
at
x_{0}
. Then there exist
\alpha>0
and neighborhoods
m_{0}\in U_{M}\subset M
,
z_{0}\in U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
and
i_{0}\in U_{\mathrm{Emb}}\subset\mathrm{Emb}^{2}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
such that for any
i\in U_{\mathrm{Emb}}
, any
z\in U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}
has a unique projection
p_{i}(z)
on
\overline{i(U_{M})}
, that
p_{i}(z)
belongs to
i(U_{M})
, and that the sphere
S(z,d_{i(U_{M})}(z))
does not osculate
i(U_{M})
at
p_{i}(z)
with
\alpha
a degree of non-osculation.

In particular, there exist
\delta,\delta^{\prime}>0
such that for any
i\in U_{\mathrm{Emb}}
and
z\in U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}
, if
x=p_{i}(z)
and
{\mathsf{M}}=i(M)
, then the function
p_{i}:B(z,\delta^{\prime})\to{\mathsf{M}}\cap\overline{B}(x,\delta)={\mathsf{M}}_{0}
is the orthogonal projection on
{\mathsf{M}}_{0}
, which is
C^{1}
and
\alpha^{-1}
-Lipschitz continuous.

Proof.

Let
\phi:V\rightarrow M
be a chart of
M
with
\phi(u_{0})=m_{0}
, and consider the function

F:V\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\times\mathrm{Emb}^{2}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D}),\ (u,z,i)\mapsto\|i\circ\phi(u)-z\|^{2}-\alpha\|i\circ\phi(u)-i(m_{0})\|^{2}.

Its second differential with respect to
u\in V
is the map

V\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\times\mathrm{Emb}^{2}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})\rightarrow\text{Bil}({\mathbb{R}}^{m},{\mathbb{R}}^{m},{\mathbb{R}})

that sends
(u,z,i)
to the bilinear map

d^{2}(F_{(z,i)})_{u}:(v_{1},v_{2})\mapsto 2(\langle d(i\circ\phi)_{u}(v_{1}),d(i\circ\phi)_{u}(v_{2})\rangle+\langle i\circ\phi(u)-z,d^{2}(i\circ\phi)_{u}(v_{1},v_{2})\rangle).

Both
F
and
d^{2}(F_{(z,i)})_{u}
are continuous with respect to
(u,z,i)
(with
\mathrm{Emb}^{2}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
equipped with the Whitney
C^{2}
-topology).
Using Characterization
(ii)
from the Osculation Lemma
3.1
, we see that if
i\circ\phi(u)\in i(M)
is a projection of
z
on
i(M)
, the sphere
S(z,d_{i(M)}(z))
not osculating
i(M)
at
i\circ\phi(u)
is equivalent to
d^{2}(F_{(z,i)})_{u}
being positive definite.

Hence we can apply Lemma
4.5
to
F
and
(u_{0},z_{0},i_{0})
(with
\Lambda={\mathbb{R}}^{D}\times\mathrm{Emb}^{2}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
) to get that there exist neighborhoods
u_{0}\in U_{V}\subset V
,
z_{0}\in U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
and
i_{0}\in U_{\mathrm{Emb}}\subset\mathrm{Emb}^{2}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
such that for any
(z,i)\in U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}\times U_{\mathrm{Emb}}
, the function
F_{(z,i)}:u\rightarrow\|i\circ\phi(u)-z\|^{2}
admits exactly one minimum on
\overline{U_{V}}
and that this minimum belongs to
U_{V}
.
Define
U_{M}:=\phi(U_{V})
. For a given
i\in U_{\mathrm{Emb}}
, this property is equivalent to the projection
U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}
on
\overline{i(U_{M})}
being uniquely defined and its image being contained in
i(U_{M})
. Let us call
p_{i}
this projection.
Lemma
4.5
also guarantees that if
i\in U_{\mathrm{Emb}}
,
z\in U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}
and if we write
x:=p_{i}(z)
and
u:=(i\circ\phi)^{-1}(x)\in U_{V}
, then

d^{2}(F_{(z,i)})_{u}:(v_{1},v_{2})\mapsto 2(\langle d(i\circ\phi)_{u}(v_{1}),d(i\circ\phi)_{u}(v_{2})\rangle+\langle i\circ\phi(u)-z,d^{2}(i\circ\phi)_{u}(v_{1},v_{2})\rangle)

is positive definite. By possibly further restricting
U_{\mathrm{Emb}},U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}
and
U_{V}
, we can assume that there exists
c>0
such that the smallest eigenvalue of
d^{2}(F_{(z,i)})_{u}
is greater than
c
for any such
i,z,u
and that the operator norm of
d(i\circ\phi)
is bounded on
U_{\mathrm{Emb}}
. Hence there exists
\alpha>0
such that the form
v\mapsto(1-\alpha)\|d(i\circ\phi)_{u}(v)\|^{2}-\langle i\circ\phi(u)-z,d^{2}(i\circ\phi)_{u}(v,v)\rangle
is definite positive for any such
i,z,u
.
Thus Characterization
(ii)
from the Osculation Lemma
3.1
guarantees that the sphere
S(z,d_{i(U_{M})}(z))
does not osculate
i(U_{M})
at
p_{i}(z)
, with
\alpha
a degree of non-osculation. This proves the first part of the proposition.

For each
i\in U_{\mathrm{Emb}}
, the map
p_{i}
is the orthogonal projection from
U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}
to
\overline{i(U_{M})}
. The non-osculation of each sphere
S(z,d_{i(U_{M})}(z))
at
p_{i}(z)
for all
z\in U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}
entails that
p_{i}
is
\alpha^{-1}
-Lipschitz continuous, as the proof of
Lemma

4.1
shows. Besides,
p_{i}
is
C^{1}
as the orthogonal projection on the manifold
i(U_{M})
. Consider
\delta^{\prime}>0
and
U\subset U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}
such that
B(z,\delta^{\prime})\subset U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}
for all
z\in U
. Let
\delta=\delta^{\prime}/\alpha
. Then, for all
z\in U
, the function
p_{i}
is defined on
B(z,\delta^{\prime})
and takes its values in
B(p_{i}(z),\delta)\cap i(U_{M})\subset\overline{B}(p_{i}(z),\delta)\cap i(M)
. We replace
U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}
by
U
to conclude.
∎

As a corollary, we get the final part of the proof of the Osculation Characterization Lemma.

Corollary 4.7
.

Characterization
(iii)
from Lemma
3.1
implies Characterization
(iv)
.

Proof.

Let
z_{0},x_{0}
and the embedding
i
from the statement of Lemma
3.1
and assume that they satisfy Characterization
(iii)
.
We already know that Characterization
(iii)
is equivalent to Characterization
(ii)
, which is the one used in the first part of Proposition
4.6
above (hence there is no circularity in our argument). Thus the proposition can be applied to
z_{0},x_{0}
and
i
to get the existence of a neighborhood
U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}
of
z_{0}
and a neighborhood
{\mathsf{M}}_{0}
of
x_{0}
in
i(M)
such that any
z\in U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}
and
x
its unique local projection on
{\mathsf{M}}_{0}
satisfy
(iii)
. This, in turn, proves that
z_{0},x_{0}
and
i
verify Condition
(iv)
.
∎

4.4
Multiple local projections

This subsection is dedicated to an easy but handy lemma that helps us manage multiple local projections on a neighborhood of a critical point.

Assume that the
C^{2}
submanifold
{\mathsf{M}}
satisfies
(P1)
,
(P2)
and
(P3)
, and let
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
be a critical point with projections
\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\}
, for some
s\in\{2,\ldots,D+1\}
. As condition
(P3)
is satisfied, the sphere
S(z_{0},d_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0}))
does not osculate
{\mathsf{M}}
at any of the
x_{i}
.
Using Lemma
4.1
, we can pick radii
\delta
and
\delta^{\prime}
so that there exist local projections
p_{j}:B(z_{0},\delta^{\prime})\to{\mathsf{M}}_{j}=\overline{B}(x_{j},\delta)\cap{\mathsf{M}}
for all
j\in[s]
. Moreover, by making
\delta^{\prime}
smaller, we can ensure using the Lipschitz continuity of the projections that the image of
p_{i}
is included in the relative interior of
{\mathsf{M}}_{i}
. Hence, these functions are equal to the projection on a
C^{2}
manifold, and they are in particular of regularity
C^{1}
, with known expressions for their gradients, as stated in Proposition
4.2
. By
(P2)
, the number of critical points is finite, so that the constants
\delta
and
\delta^{\prime}
can be chosen independently of
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
. We summarize these properties in the next lemma.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
{\mathsf{M}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
be a
C^{2}
compact submanifold satisfying
(P1)
,
(P2)
and
(P3)
.
There exist
\delta^{\prime},\delta>0
such that for any
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
with
\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\}
, there are
C^{1}
functions
p_{i}:B(z_{0},\delta^{\prime})\rightarrow{\mathsf{M}}_{i}=\overline{B}(x_{i},\delta)\cap{\mathsf{M}}
for
i=1\dots,s
such that for any
z\in B(z_{0},\delta^{\prime})
, we have
\pi_{{\mathsf{M}}_{i}}(z)=\{p_{i}(z)\}\subset B(x_{i},\delta)\cap{\mathsf{M}}
and
\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z)\subset\{p_{1}(z),\ldots,p_{s}(z)\}
. Furthermore, if
p_{i}(z)=x
and
\eta=z-x
, then
(dp_{i})_{z}=(\mathrm{id}-W_{x,\eta})^{-1}\circ\pi_{x}
.

Proof.

The only point that remains to be proven is the inclusion
\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z)\subset\{p_{1}(z),\ldots,p_{s}(z)\}
. Let
z\in B(z_{0},\delta^{\prime})
and
y\in\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z)
. Then,

\|y-z_{0}\|\leq\|y-z\|+\|z-z_{0}\|\leq\|x_{0}-z\|+\|z-z_{0}\|\leq\|x_{0}-z_{0}\|+2\|z-z_{0}\|.

Hence,
y\in B(z_{0},d_{{\mathsf{M}}}(z_{0})+2\delta^{\prime})
. By
Lemma

2.1
, if
\delta^{\prime}
is small enough, this implies that
y
is in one of the sets
M_{1},\dots,M_{s}
, implying that
y\in\pi_{{\mathsf{M}}_{i}}(z)
for some
i=1,\dots,s
.
∎

5
Sampling theory for generic manifolds

In this section, we prove the Stability Theorem for Subsets (
1.6
), which can be seen as a stronger analogue to the results for compact sets from
[
CCSL06
]
in the special case of well-behaved manifolds sampled without noise.
We restate the theorem for the reader’s convenience.

See
1.6

Proof of
1.6
.

Let
{\mathsf{A}}\subset{\mathsf{M}}
be a subset with
d_{H}({\mathsf{A}},{\mathsf{M}})\leq\varepsilon
, and let
\varepsilon<\tau({\mathsf{M}})/8
.

1.

According to the critical values separation theorem
[
CCSL06
, Theorem 4.4]
, the distance function
d_{\mathsf{A}}
has no critical values in the interval
(4\varepsilon,\tau({\mathsf{M}})-3\varepsilon)
.
This proves that all critical points in
Z({\mathsf{A}})
are either at distance less than
4\varepsilon
from
{\mathsf{A}}
, or at distance at least
\tau({\mathsf{M}})-3\varepsilon
from
{\mathsf{A}}
.
Let us focus on the first case. Let
z\in Z({\mathsf{A}})
be a point with
d_{\mathsf{A}}(z)\leq 4\varepsilon
. By definition of critical points,
z
is the center of the smallest enclosing ball of
\pi_{\mathsf{A}}(z)\subset{\mathsf{A}}
, and this ball is at most of radius
4\varepsilon
. According to
[
ALS13
, Lemma 12]
, this implies that

d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)\leq\tau({\mathsf{M}})\left(1-\sqrt{1-\frac{16\varepsilon^{2}}{\tau({\mathsf{M}})^{2}}}\right)\leq\frac{8\varepsilon^{2}}{\tau({\mathsf{M}})}\left(1+\frac{16\varepsilon^{2}}{\tau({\mathsf{M}})^{2}}\right)\leq\frac{10\varepsilon^{2}}{\tau({\mathsf{M}})},

(12)

where we use the condition
\varepsilon<\tau({\mathsf{M}})/8
and the inequality
\sqrt{1-u^{2}}\geq 1-u/2-u^{2}/2
for
u\in[0,1]
. This yields the first alternative in
1.6
.

We now consider the second case, where
d_{\mathsf{A}}(z)>\tau({\mathsf{M}})-3\varepsilon
. In that case, the inequality
\varepsilon\leq\tau({\mathsf{M}})/8
implies that
d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)\geq d_{\mathsf{A}}(z)-\varepsilon\geq\tau({\mathsf{M}})-4\varepsilon>\tau({\mathsf{M}})/2
.
We require a lemma, which is a variant of Lemma 3.3 from
[
CCSL06
]
suited to manifolds.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
{\mathsf{M}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
be a compact
C^{2}
submanifold.
Let
{\mathsf{A}}\subset{\mathsf{M}}
be a subset with
d_{H}({\mathsf{A}},{\mathsf{M}})\leq\varepsilon
, and let
z\in Z({\mathsf{A}})
with
0<r<d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)<R
. Then, there exists a
\mu
-critical point
z^{\prime}\in Z_{\mu}({\mathsf{M}})
, with
\|z-z^{\prime}\|\leq\varepsilon
and

\mu\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{r}\left(1+\frac{R}{2\tau({\mathsf{M}})}\right).

(13)

Proof.

We adapt the proof of Lemma 3.3 in
[
CCSL06
]
. We follow the gradient flow of
\nabla d_{\mathsf{M}}
starting at
z\in Z({\mathsf{A}})
along a trajectory parameterized by arc length. If the gradient flow reaches a critical point of
{\mathsf{M}}
before time
\varepsilon
, the result holds. Otherwise, let
w
be the point reached by the gradient flow after time
\varepsilon
. We have

d_{\mathsf{M}}(w)-d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)=\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\|\nabla d_{\mathsf{M}}(C(t))\|\mathrm{d}t,

(14)

where
t\mapsto C(t)
is the flow curve. Hence, there exists some point
z^{\prime}
along this curve with

\|\nabla d_{\mathsf{M}}(z^{\prime})\|\leq\frac{d_{\mathsf{M}}(w)-d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)}{\varepsilon}.

Furthermore, it holds that
\|z-w\|\leq\varepsilon
. Let us show that
z^{\prime}
is a
\mu
-critical point of
{\mathsf{M}}
for
\mu=\frac{\varepsilon}{r}\left(1+\frac{R}{2\tau({\mathsf{M}})}\right)
. As
z\in Z({\mathsf{A}})
, we have according to
[
CCSL06
, Lemma 3.2]

d_{\mathsf{M}}(w)^{2}\leq d_{\mathsf{A}}(w)^{2}\leq d_{\mathsf{A}}(z)^{2}+\|z-w\|^{2}\leq d_{\mathsf{A}}(z)^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}.

(15)

Let
x\in\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z)
and
y\in\pi_{\mathsf{A}}(x)
, whence
\|x-y\|\leq\varepsilon
.
We have

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{A}}(z)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|z-y\|^{2}=\|z-x\|^{2}+\|x-y\|^{2}+2\langle z-x,x-y\rangle

\displaystyle\leq d_{{\mathsf{M}}}(z)^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}+2\langle z-x,\pi_{x}^{\perp}(x-y)\rangle,

(16)

where we use that
z-x
is orthogonal to
T_{x}{\mathsf{M}}
. According to
[
Fed59
]
,
\|\pi_{x}^{\perp}(x-y)\|\leq\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{2\tau({\mathsf{M}})}
. Hence, (
15
) and (
16
) yield that

\displaystyle\|\nabla d_{\mathsf{M}}(z^{\prime})\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{d_{\mathsf{M}}(w)-d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)}{\varepsilon}=\frac{d_{\mathsf{M}}(w)^{2}-d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)^{2}}{\varepsilon(d_{\mathsf{M}}(w)+d_{\mathsf{M}}(z))}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\varepsilon^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\frac{R}{\tau({\mathsf{M}})}}{2\varepsilon r}=\frac{\varepsilon}{r}\left(1+\frac{R}{2\tau({\mathsf{M}})}\right).

This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark that we always have the trivial bound
d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)\leq\mathrm{diam}({\mathsf{M}})
for
z\in Z({\mathsf{A}})
. Hence, the lemma implies that for
z\in Z(A)
with
d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)>\tau({\mathsf{M}})/2
, there exists a
\mu
-critical point
z^{\prime}\in Z_{\mu}({\mathsf{M}})
with
\|z-z^{\prime}\|\leq\varepsilon
and
\mu=C_{2}\varepsilon
for some constant
C_{2}
depending on
{\mathsf{M}}
, which completes the proof of the first point.

2.

This is a straightforward consequence of the first item and
(P4)
- we only need to make
\varepsilon_{0}>0
small enough that
C_{2}\varepsilon_{0}<\mu_{0}
(where
\mu_{0}
is from the statement of
(P4)
).

3.

Let
\delta
and
\delta^{\prime}
be radii such that the statement of
Lemma

3.1
.
(iv)
holds uniformly for all the critical points
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
and
x_{0}\in\pi_{{\mathsf{M}}}(z_{0})
(these are in finite number, as
{\mathsf{M}}
satisfies
(P1)
and
(P2)
). Let
z\in Z({\mathsf{A}})
be a critical point close to some critical point
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
, with
\|z-z_{0}\|\leq C_{4}\varepsilon
(where
C_{4}
is the constant from
1.6
.
2
). Let
x\in\pi_{\mathsf{A}}(z)
. Note that
\|x-z_{0}\|\leq\|x-z\|+\|z-z_{0}\|\leq\|x_{0}-z\|+\|z-z_{0}\|\leq d_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})+2\|z-z_{0}\|
. Hence, according to
Lemma

2.1
, for
\|z-z_{0}\|
small enough, the point
x
is at distance less than
\delta
from some vertex
x_{0}\in\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})
(and this uniformly for each critical point
z_{0}
). According to
Lemma

4.1
, there exists a unique projection
x_{1}
of
z
on
{\mathsf{M}}_{0}={\mathsf{M}}\cap\overline{B}(x_{0},\delta)
, and
\|x_{1}-x_{0}\|\leq\|z-z_{0}\|/\alpha
. In particular, for
\|z-z_{0}\|
small enough,
\|x_{1}-x_{0}\|<\delta
. Hence,
x_{1}
belongs to the relative interior of
{\mathsf{M}}_{0}
and the vector
z-x_{1}
is orthogonal to
T_{x_{1}}{\mathsf{M}}
.

As
d_{H}({\mathsf{A}},{\mathsf{M}})\leq\varepsilon
, there exists
y\in{\mathsf{A}}
with
\|x_{1}-y\|\leq\varepsilon
. We write thanks to
[
Fed59
, Theorem 4.18]

\displaystyle\|z-x\|^{2}=d_{\mathsf{A}}(z)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|z-y\|^{2}=\|z-x_{1}\|^{2}+\|x_{1}-y\|^{2}+2\langle z-x_{1},\pi_{x_{1}}^{\perp}(x_{1}-y)\rangle

\displaystyle\leq d_{{\mathsf{M}}_{0}}(z)^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\left(1+\frac{d_{{\mathsf{M}}_{0}}(z)}{\tau({\mathsf{M}})}\right).

Note that
1+d_{{\mathsf{M}}_{0}}(z)/\tau({\mathsf{M}})
can be roughly upper bounded by

1+\frac{\mathrm{diam}({\mathsf{M}})}{\tau({\mathsf{M}})}=\vcentcolon C^{\prime}.

Hence,
x\in B(z,\sqrt{d_{{\mathsf{M}}_{0}}(z)^{2}+C^{\prime}\varepsilon^{2}})
. As
x\in{\mathsf{M}}_{0}
, this implies by
(P3)
that

\|x_{1}-z\|^{2}+C^{\prime}\varepsilon^{2}\geq\|x-z\|^{2}\geq\|x_{1}-z\|^{2}+\alpha\|x-x_{1}\|^{2},

so that
\|x-x_{1}\|\leq\sqrt{C^{\prime}/\alpha}\varepsilon
. As we have
\|x_{1}-x_{0}\|\leq\frac{1}{\alpha}\|z-z_{0}\|
, we obtain

\|x-x_{0}\|\leq\|x-x_{1}\|+\|x_{1}-x_{0}\|\leq\sqrt{C^{\prime}/\alpha}\varepsilon+\frac{1}{\alpha}C_{4}\varepsilon.\qed

Remark 5.2
.

The reader will have noticed that the proof does not make use of all of Condition
(P1)
, but rather only of the property that each critical point has only finitely many projections.

Finally, we prove
1.7
.

Proof of
1.7
.

Consider the constants
C_{4},C_{5},\varepsilon_{0}>0
from Theorem
1.6
, and let
\varepsilon_{1}=\min(\tau({\mathsf{M}})/8,\varepsilon_{0}/C_{5})
.
Let
{\mathsf{A}}
be a
(\delta,\varepsilon)
-sampling of
{\mathsf{M}}
for some
0<\delta\leq\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{1}
. Let
z\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
with
\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z)=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{s}\}
. According to
1.6
, if
z^{\prime}\in Z({\mathsf{A}})
is at distance less than
C_{4}\varepsilon
from
z
, then
\pi_{\mathsf{A}}(z)\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{s}B(x_{i},C_{5}\varepsilon)
. Hence, the map

\begin{split}({\mathsf{A}}\cap B(x_{1},C_{5}\varepsilon))\times\cdots\times({\mathsf{A}}\cap B(x_{s},C_{5}\varepsilon))&\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\\
(y_{1},\dots,y_{s})&\longmapsto m(\{y_{1},\dots,y_{s}\})\end{split}

contains all the points of
Z({\mathsf{A}})
at distance less than
C_{4}\varepsilon
from
z
in its image. As the cardinality of
{\mathsf{A}}\cap B(x_{i},C_{5}\varepsilon)
is at most
N(\delta,C_{5}\varepsilon)
, the total number of such points is
2^{N(\delta,C_{5}\varepsilon)s}
.

Let us now prove the second bound. Let
x\in{\mathsf{M}}
and let
\{y_{1},\dots,y_{K}\}
be a maximal
(\delta,+\infty)
-sampling of
B(x,\varepsilon)\cap{\mathsf{M}}
. Then, the balls
B(y_{i},\delta/2)
are pairwise disjoint for
i=1,\dots,K
. According to
[
AL18
, Proposition 8.7]
, as
\delta/2\leq 2\varepsilon\leq 2\varepsilon_{1}\leq\tau/4
, it holds that

\mathrm{Vol}(B(y_{i},\delta/2)\cap{\mathsf{M}})\geq c_{m}\delta^{m}\text{ and }\mathrm{Vol}(B(x,2\varepsilon)\cap{\mathsf{M}})\leq C_{m}\varepsilon^{m}

for two positive constants
c_{m},C_{m}
. Hence, as
\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{K}B(y_{i},\delta/2)\cap{\mathsf{M}}\subset B(x,2\varepsilon)\cap{\mathsf{M}}
, it holds that

c_{m}K\delta^{m}\leq C_{m}\varepsilon^{m}

that is
N(\delta,\varepsilon)\leq\frac{C_{m}}{c_{m}}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}\right)^{m}
.
∎

6
The
\mu
-critical points of
{\mathsf{M}}
and the Big Simplex Property

Let as above
M
be a
C^{k}
compact manifold of dimension
m\geq 1
for some
k\geq 2
, and let
i\in\mathrm{Emb}^{2}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
and
{\mathsf{M}}=i(M)
.
In this section, we concern ourselves with the position of the
\mu
-critical points of
{\mathsf{M}}
. In particular, whenever
{\mathsf{M}}
satisfies
(P1)
,
(P2)
and
(P3)
, we define a purely local condition on the critical points of
{\mathsf{M}}
and their projections, called the Big Simplex Property, that turns out to be equivalent to the apparently more global condition
(P4)
, yet is easier to manipulate in terms of the embedding
i
. This will help us show that
(P4)
is verified for a dense subset of the space
\mathrm{Emb}^{2}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
of embeddings later in Section
9
, using the language of jets.

Let us start with some preliminary observations.
Consider the compact
C^{2}
submanifold
{\mathsf{M}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{D}

and let us define the function

\begin{split}\eta_{\mathsf{M}}:[0,1]&\longrightarrow[0,+\infty]\\
\mu&\longmapsto\sup\{r:\ \exists x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\text{ s.t. }\|\nabla d_{\mathsf{M}}(x)\|\leq\mu\text{ and }d(Z({\mathsf{M}}),x)=r\},\end{split}

the supremum of the distances to
{\mathsf{M}}
at which a
\mu
-critical point can be found.
If
{\mathsf{M}}
is of diameter
\text{diam}({\mathsf{M}})>0
and
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
is at distance
L+\text{diam}({\mathsf{M}})
from
Z({\mathsf{M}})\subset\mathrm{Conv}({\mathsf{M}})
, then it is at distance at least
L
from
{\mathsf{M}}
and the norm of the generalized gradient
\nabla d_{\mathsf{M}}(x)
is lower-bounded by
\frac{\sqrt{L^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\text{diam}({\mathsf{M}})^{2}}}{L}
. Hence,
\eta_{M}
is finite for
\mu\in[0,1)
, and for such a
\mu
the supremum of the definition is a maximum due to the lower semicontinuity of the norm of the gradient.
On the other hand, we always have
\eta_{\mathsf{M}}(1)=+\infty
. We are more interested in
\eta_{\mathsf{M}}(\mu)
for small values of
\mu
, and we see that
\lim_{\mu\stackrel{{\scriptstyle>}}{{\rightarrow}}0}\eta_{\mathsf{M}}(\mu)=0
. Indeed, assume that it is not the case: there exists
\varepsilon>0
and a sequence
(x_{n})_{n}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
of points such that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|\nabla d_{\mathsf{M}}(x_{n})\|=0
and such that
d(Z({\mathsf{M}}),x_{n})\geq\varepsilon
for all
n
. But thanks to the same argument as above, the sequence
(x_{n})_{n}
is bounded, hence a subsequence that converges to some point
x\not\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
can be extracted, which yields a contradiction with the lower semicontinuity of the norm of the generalized gradient.

Property
(P4)
states that there exist
C=C({\mathsf{M}})
and
\mu_{0}\in(0,1)
such that for any
0\mu\in[0,\mu_{0})
we have
\eta_{\mathsf{M}}(\mu)\leq C\mu
, and will be our main object of interest in this section. Note that Property
(P4)
is equivalent to the apparently stronger Property
(P4’)
:

(P4’)

For any
\mu_{0}\in(0,1)
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for every
\mu\in[0,\mu_{0})
, the set
Z_{\mu}({\mathsf{M}})
is included in a tubular neighborhood of size
C\mu
of
Z({\mathsf{M}})
, that is every point of
Z_{\mu}({\mathsf{M}})
is at distance less than
C\mu
from
Z({\mathsf{M}})
.

Indeed, let
\mu_{0}>0
and
C>0
be given to us by
(P4)
, and let
\mu_{0}^{\prime}>0
be as in the statement of
(P4’)
. We have shown above that
\eta_{\mathsf{M}}(\mu_{0}^{\prime})<\infty
. Let
z
be a
\mu
-critical point of
{\mathsf{M}}
; if
\mu<\mu_{0}
, then
d(z,Z({\mathsf{M}}))<C\mu
using
(P4)
, and if
\mu\in[\mu_{0},\mu_{0}^{\prime})
, then
d(z,Z({\mathsf{M}}))\leq\eta_{\mathsf{M}}(\mu_{0}^{\prime})=\mu_{0}\frac{\eta_{\mathsf{M}}(\mu_{0}^{\prime})}{\mu_{0}}\leq\mu\frac{\eta_{\mathsf{M}}(\mu_{0}^{\prime})}{\mu_{0}}
, hence in all cases
d(z,Z({\mathsf{M}}))\leq\mu\left(C+\frac{\eta_{\mathsf{M}}(\mu_{0}^{\prime})}{\mu_{0}}\right)
and
{\mathsf{M}}
satisfies
(P4’)
.

6.1
Position of the
\mu
-critical points

As a first step to reformulating Condition
(P4)
, we need to understand the behavior of the medial axis
\mathrm{Med}({\mathsf{M}})
around
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
.
Assume that
{\mathsf{M}}
satisfies properties
(P1)
,
(P2)
and
(P3)
, and let
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
with projections
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\}=\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})
, for some
s\in\{2,\ldots,D+1\}
. Let

E=\mathrm{Vec}(\{x_{j_{1}}-x_{j_{2}}:\ j_{1},j_{2}\in[s]\}).

As
{\mathsf{M}}
satisfies
(P1)
, the dimension of
E
is
s-1
. Let us also write
\pi_{E}:{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\rightarrow E
and
\pi_{E}^{\perp}:{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\rightarrow E^{\perp}
the perpendicular projections. We will show that for
\mu_{0}
small enough, the tangent space of the set
Z_{\mu_{0}}({\mathsf{M}})=\{z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}:\ \|\nabla d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)\|\leq\mu_{0}\}
at
z_{0}
(in the sense of Federer
[
Fed59
]
) is equal to the orthogonal
E^{\perp}
of the vector space
E
.
Namely, we show that every point
z
of the medial axis close enough to
z_{0}
are such that
\|\pi_{E}(z-z_{0})\|
is of order
\|z-z_{0}\|^{2}
, while for every direction
h\in E^{\perp}
, there is a point of
Z_{\mu_{0}}({\mathsf{M}})
at distance of order
\|h\|^{2}
from
z_{0}+h
.
This is illustrated in Figure
4
.

Let
p_{j}:B(z_{0},\delta^{\prime})\to B(x_{j},\delta)\cap{\mathsf{M}}
for
j\in[s]
be the local projections from Lemma
4.8
(for some
\delta^{\prime},\delta>0
), which we know to be Lipschitz continous. We define

\begin{split}I:B(z_{0},\delta^{\prime})&\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}([s])\\
z&\longmapsto\{j\in[s]:\ p_{j}(z)\in\pi_{{\mathsf{M}}}(z)\}.\end{split}

(17)

In particular,
\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z)=\{p_{j}(z):\ j\in I(z)\}
. We also introduce the notation
m_{I}(z)=m(\{p_{j}(z):\ j\in I\})
for
I\subset[s]
and
z\in B(z_{0},\delta^{\prime})
, where we recall that
m(\sigma)
denotes the center of the smallest enclosing ball of the set
\sigma
.

Figure 4:
