Using the notations of Subsection
6.1
, for
\mu_{0}>0
small enough, the tangent space of
Z_{\mu_{0}}({\mathsf{M}})
at the critical point
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
is equal to
E^{\perp}
, and the points of
Z_{\mu_{0}}({\mathsf{M}})
that are at a small distance
h
from
z_{0}
must be at distance
O(h^{2})
from the affine space
z_{0}+E^{\perp}
.

For
I\subset[s]
, let

\begin{split}g_{I}:B(z_{0},\delta^{\prime})&\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}\\
z&\longmapsto\frac{\|z-m_{I}(z)\|}{d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)}.\end{split}

(18)

Note that
\|\nabla d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)\|=g_{I(z)}(z)
for any
z\in B(z_{0},\delta^{\prime})
.

The following Lemma states that
g_{[s]}
and
g_{I}
(for any
I\subset[s]
) are Lipschitz continuous and Hölder continuous respectively around
z_{0}
; it also yields some control over their Lipschitz and Hölder constants, which will be needed later in Section
8
where we let the embedding
i:M\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
vary.

Lemma 6.1
.

There exist constants
\delta^{\prime\prime},\ell_{1},\ell_{2}>0
that depend only, and continuously, on the Lipschitz constants of the projections
p_{j}:B(z_{0},\delta^{\prime})\to B(x_{j},\delta)\cap{\mathsf{M}}
and on the distance between
z_{0}
and the boundary of the simplex with vertices
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{s}\}

such that the function
g_{[s]}:B(z_{0},\min(\delta^{\prime},\delta^{\prime\prime}))\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is
\ell_{1}
-Lipschitz continuous and that for any
I\subset[s]
,
the function
g_{I}:B(z_{0},\min(\delta^{\prime},\delta^{\prime\prime}))\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is
(1/2)
-Hölder continuous with Hölder constant
\ell_{2}
.

Proof.

The distance
d_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})
is lower bounded by the distance between
z_{0}
and the boundary of the simplex with vertices
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{s}\}
and the function
z\mapsto d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)
is also lower bounded and Lipschitz continuous on a ball of radius
d_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})/2
around
z_{0}
.
We know from Lemma
4.1
that the projections
p_{j}:B(z_{0},\delta^{\prime})\to B(x_{j},\delta)\cap{\mathsf{M}}
are Lipschitz continuous.
As the function
A\mapsto m(A)
is locally
1/2
-Hölder continuous with respect to the Hausdorff distance
[
ALS13
, Lemma 16]
, the statement regarding Hölder continuity follows.

By hypothesis,
z_{0}
belongs to the interior of the simplex spanned by
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{s}\}
. Hence,
m_{[s]}(z)
also belongs to the interior of the simplex spanned by
\{p_{1}(z),\dots,p_{s}(z)\}
for
z
close enough to
z_{0}
(depending on the Lipschitz constants of the
p_{j}
s and how far
z_{0}
is from the boundary of the simplex spanned by
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{s}\}
). When this is the case, the function
m_{[s]}(z)
is actually equal to the center of the circumsphere of the points
p_{1}(z),\dots,p_{s}(z)
.
When restricted to the simplices whose circumsphere has its center at distance at least
c>0
from their boundary, the coordinates of the center of the circumsphere is a Lipschitz function of the coordinates of the vertices for a Lipschitz constant that depends on
c
.
Thus
m_{[s]}(z)
is Lipschitz continuous with respect to the coordinates of the points
p_{1}(z),\dots,p_{s}(z)
, hence with respect to those of
z
, when
z
is close enough to
z_{0}
, and so is the function
g_{[s]}
as a composition of Lipschitz maps, with the definition of “close” and the Lipschitz constant depending on the Lipschitz constants of the
p_{j}
s and how far
z_{0}
is from the boundary of the simplex spanned by
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{s}\}
.
∎

As another step towards understanding the local behavior of the
\mu
-critical points around
z_{0}
, the next lemma states that
\mu
-critical points (for
\mu
small enough) that are close to
z_{0}
must belong to
z_{0}+E^{\perp}
as a first approximation.

Lemma 6.2
.

There exist
\mu_{0},\delta_{0},C>0
such that for every
\mu_{0}
-critical point
z\in B(z_{0},\delta_{0})
, we have
I(z)=[s]
and

\|\pi_{E}(z-z_{0})\|\leq C\|z-z_{0}\|^{2}.

Proof.

Let
\delta,\delta^{\prime}>0
and
p_{j}:B(z_{0},\delta^{\prime})\rightarrow B(x_{j},\delta)\cap{\mathsf{M}}
for
j=1,\ldots,s
be as in
Lemma

4.8
, and such that the functions
g_{I}
are Hölder continuous (
Lemma

6.1
).
Note that
g_{I}(z_{0})>0
for all
I\subsetneq[s]
. Hence, it holds that if
I\subsetneq[s]
, then

g_{I}(z)>\frac{1}{2}\min_{I\subsetneq[s]}g_{I}(z_{0})=\vcentcolon\mu_{0}

for
\|z-z_{0}\|\leq\delta_{1}
for some
\delta^{\prime}>\delta_{1}>0
. If
z\in B(z_{0},\delta_{1})
is
\mu_{0}
-critical, then

\|\nabla d_{{\mathsf{M}}}(z)\|=\frac{\|z-m_{I(z)}(z)\|}{d_{{\mathsf{M}}}(z)}=g_{I(z)}(z)\leq\mu_{0},

hence
I(z)=[s]
. This proves the first point.

Let us now prove the second statement. Let
z\in B(z_{0},\delta_{1})
be a
\mu_{0}
-critical point and
h=z-z_{0}
.
For any
j\in[s]
,

\displaystyle\|z-p_{j}(z)\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\|(z_{0}-x_{j})-(p_{j}(z)-h-x_{j})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\|z_{0}-x_{j}\|^{2}+2\langle z_{0}-x_{j},h+x_{j}-p_{j}(z)\rangle+\|h+x_{j}-p_{j}(z)\|^{2}.

As
z_{0}\in\mathrm{Conv}(\{x_{j}:\ j\in[s]\})
, we have
z_{0}-x_{j}\in E
. This implies that
\langle z_{0}-x_{j},h\rangle=\langle z_{0}-x_{j},\pi_{E}(h)\rangle
. Also, as
p_{j}
is Lipschitz continuous, it holds that
\|p_{j}(z)-x_{j}\|\leq Lh
for some constant
L>0
. Furthermore, as
z_{0}-x_{j}\in T_{x_{j}}M^{\bot}
and
p_{j}(z)\in B(x_{j},\delta)\cap{\mathsf{M}}
, according to
[
Fed59
, Theorem 4.8.7]
,

|\langle z_{0}-x_{j},x_{j}-p_{j}(z)\rangle|=|\langle z_{0}-x_{j},\pi_{x_{j}}^{\bot}(x_{j}-p_{j}(z))\rangle|\leq\frac{d_{{\mathsf{M}}_{j}}(z)\|x_{j}-p_{j}(z)\|^{2}}{2\tau({\mathsf{M}})}\leq C_{1}\|h\|^{2}

for some constant
C_{1}
depending on
\tau({\mathsf{M}})
,
d_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})
and
L
. In total, we have shown that

\|z-p_{j}(z)\|^{2}=\|z_{0}-x_{j}\|^{2}+2\langle z_{0}-x_{j},\pi_{E}(h)\rangle+R_{j}(h),

(19)

where
|R_{j}(h)|\leq C_{2}\|h\|^{2}
for some positive constant
C_{2}
.
Now, by definition
\|z-p_{j_{1}}(z)\|^{2}=\|z-p_{j_{2}}(z)\|^{2}
for any
1\leq j_{1},j_{2}\leq s
(and
s
is at least
2
). Hence,

\|z_{0}-x_{j_{1}}\|^{2}+2\langle z_{0}-x_{j_{1}},\pi_{E}(h)\rangle+R_{j_{1}}(h)=\|z_{0}-x_{j_{2}}\|^{2}+2\langle z_{0}-x_{j_{2}},\pi_{E}(h)\rangle+R_{j_{2}}(h),

so

|\langle x_{j_{1}}-x_{j_{2}},\pi_{E}(h)\rangle|\leq|R_{j_{1}}(h)+R_{j_{2}}(h)|.

As
E
is spanned by the vectors
x_{j_{1}}-x_{j_{2}}
, this implies that
\|\pi_{E}(h)\|\leq C_{4}h^{2}
for some
C_{4}
that depends on the geometry of the nondegenerate simplex spanned by the points
x_{j}
.
∎

The geometry of the points close to the critical point
z_{0}
that belong to the most central stratum of the medial axis, i.e. those such that
I(z)=[s]
, can be more precisely described:

Lemma 6.3
.

There exists
\delta>0
such that the local projections
p_{j}:B(z_{0},\delta)\to B(x_{j},\delta^{\prime})\cap{\mathsf{M}}
for
j\in[s]
from Lemma
4.8
are well-defined and that the core medial axis of
{\mathsf{M}}
around
z_{0}

M_{c}(z_{0},{\mathsf{M}}):=\{z\in B(z_{0},\delta):\;I(z)=[s]\}

is a
(D-s+1)
-dimensional
C^{2}
submanifold with tangent space
T_{z_{0}}M_{c}(z_{0},{\mathsf{M}})=E^{\perp}
in
z_{0}
.

Proof.

Let
\delta,\delta^{\prime}>0
and
p_{j}:B(z_{0},\delta)\rightarrow B(x_{j},\delta^{\prime})\cap{\mathsf{M}}={\mathsf{M}}_{j}
for
j=1,\ldots,s
be as in
Lemma

4.8
.
When restricted to
B(z_{0},\delta)
, the function
z\mapsto d(z,{\mathsf{M}}_{j})^{2}
is equal to
\phi_{j}:B(z_{0},\delta)\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}},z\mapsto\|z-p_{j}(z)\|^{2}
.
We know that the map
p_{j}
is
C^{1}
, hence so is
\phi_{j}
.
Its differential is

(d\phi_{j})_{z}:h\mapsto 2\langle z-p_{j}(z),h-d(p_{j})_{z}(h)\rangle=2\langle z-p_{j}(z),h\rangle

for
h\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, as
\mathrm{Im}(d(p_{j})_{z}(h))\subset T_{p_{j}(z)}{\mathsf{M}}
and thus
\mathrm{Im}(d(p_{j})_{z}(h))\perp z-p_{j}(z)
.
This shows that
\phi_{j}
is in fact
C^{2}
.

By definition, the core medial axis
M_{c}(z_{0},{\mathsf{M}})
is defined by the
s-1
equations

\phi_{1}(z)-\phi_{j}(z)=0\qquad j=2,\ldots,s

on
B(z_{0},\delta)
.
The differential of the function
z\mapsto\phi_{1}(z)-\phi_{j}(z)
is
h\mapsto 2\langle p_{j}(z)-p_{1}(z),h\rangle
, and the
s-1
vectors
p_{j}(z)-p_{1}(z)
form a linearly independent family for
z_{0}=z
, as
{\mathsf{M}}
satisfies
(P1)
. By continuity, we can make
\delta>0
smaller to ensure that this remains true for all
z\in B(z_{0},\delta)
.
Then
M_{c}(z_{0},{\mathsf{M}})
is the zero set of the submersion
F:B(z_{0},\delta)\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{s-1},z\mapsto(\phi_{1}(z)-\phi_{j}(z))_{j\in[s]}
, and the kernel of the differential
d_{z_{0}}F
is the perpendicular of
\mathrm{Vec}(\{p_{j}(z_{0})-p_{1}(z_{0}):\ j=2,\ldots,s\})=E
, i.e.
E^{\perp}
. This is enough to conclude.
∎

We now show that
\mu_{0}
-critical points can be found by moving from the critical point
z_{0}
in any orthogonal directions
h\in E^{\perp}
, up to a quadratic error of order
\|h\|^{2}
; this is essentially a consequence of the previous lemma.

Lemma 6.4

(Existence of
\mu
-critical points)
.

For any
\mu_{0}\in(0,1)
, there exist
\delta_{1},C>0
such that for any
h\in E^{\perp}
with
\|h\|<\delta_{1}
, there exists a
\mu_{0}
-critical point
z
of
{\mathsf{M}}
with
\pi_{E^{\perp}}(z-z_{0})=h
,
\|\pi_{E}(z-z_{0})\|\leq C\|h\|^{2}
and such that
I(z)=[s]
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of
Lemma

6.3
. Indeed, as
T_{z_{0}}M_{c}(z_{0},M)=E^{\perp}
, the orthogonal projection
\psi:z\in M_{c}(z_{0},M)\to\pi_{E^{\perp}}(z-z_{0})\in E^{\perp}
is a local diffeomorphism at
z_{0}
. Its inverse
\psi^{-1}
is defined on a small ball around
0
in
E^{\perp}
. If
h
is in this ball, then
z=\psi^{-1}(h)
is such that
\pi_{E^{\perp}}(z-z_{0})=h
. Furthermore,

\|\pi_{E}(z-z_{0})\|=\|z-z_{0}-\pi_{E^{\perp}}(z-z_{0})\|=\|\psi^{-1}(h)-\psi^{-1}(0)-h\|\leq C\|h\|^{2}

for some constant
C
as
\psi^{-1}
is of class
C^{2}
and
d\psi^{-1}_{0}
is equal to the identity on
E^{\perp}
. The point
z
is
\mu
-critical for
\mu=\|\nabla d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)\|
. As
I(z)=[s]
, we have
\|\nabla d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)\|=g_{[s]}(z)
, which is a Lipschitz-continuous function on a neighborhood of
z_{0}
according to
Lemma

6.1
. As
\|\nabla d_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})\|=0
,
\mu
will be smaller than
\mu_{0}
for any
\|h\|<\delta_{1}
if
\delta_{1}
is small enough..
∎

6.2
The Big Simplex Property

In the following proposition, we translate the global condition
(P4)
into a more practical and purely local (around a critical point
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
) condition, the
Big Simplex Property
, that can be expressed in terms of the volumes of the simplices formed by the points
z
close to
z_{0}
and their projections
p_{j}(z)
for
j\in[s]
, under the assumption that
z-z_{0}
is perpendicular to
E=\mathrm{Vec}(\{x_{j_{1}}-x_{j_{2}}:\ 1\leq j_{1},j_{2}\leq s\})
. The proof makes direct use of the two previous lemmas shown in
Section

6.1
.

Proposition 6.5

(Big Simplex Property)
.

Let
{\mathsf{M}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
be a compact
C^{2}
submanifold that satisfies conditions
(P1)
,
(P2)
,
(P3)
.
Then the following “Big Simplex Property”:

(BSP)

For any critical point
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
, there are constants
\delta^{\prime},\delta,L>0

such that if we write
\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\}
, the local projections
p_{j}:B(z_{0},\delta^{\prime})\to B(x_{j},\delta)\cap{\mathsf{M}}
from Lemma
4.8
are well-defined and the following holds. For
h\in E^{\perp}
with
\|h\|\leq\delta^{\prime}
, we let
\Delta(h)
be the
s
-simplex with vertices
z=z_{0}+h
and
p_{j}(z)
for
1\leq j\leq s
. Then,
the
s
-volume of
\Delta(h)
satisfies

\mathrm{Vol}_{s}(\Delta(h))\geq L\|h\|.

(20)

is equivalent to Property
(P4)
introduced earlier:

(P4)

There exist constants
C>0
and
\mu_{0}\in(0,1)
such that for every
\mu\in[0,\mu_{0})
, the set
Z_{\mu}({\mathsf{M}})
is included in a tubular neighborhood of size
C\mu
of
Z({\mathsf{M}})
, that is every point of
Z_{\mu}({\mathsf{M}})
is at distance less than
C\mu
from
Z({\mathsf{M}})
.

Before proving the equivalence between
(BSP)
and
(P4)
, we provide an example of a simple manifold
{\mathsf{M}}

not
satisfying either conditions (albeit satisfying
(P1)
,
(P2)
and
(P3)
). See also Figure
5
for an illustration.

Example 6.6
.

Consider the
C^{2}
submanifold
{\mathsf{M}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
that is equal on
[-1,1]\times[-2,2]
to the union of the graph of the functions
x\mapsto 1+x^{3}
and
x\mapsto-1-x^{3}
, see
Figure

5
.
The distance function to
{\mathsf{M}}
admits a single critical point
z_{0}=(0,0)
whose projections define a non-degenerate simplex, and the sphere
S(z_{0},1)
is non-osculating
{\mathsf{M}}
at each projection. On the other hand, for each
x>0
the projections of the point
p(x):=(x+3x^{2}+3x^{5},0)
are
(x,1+x^{3})
and
(x,-1-x^{3})
, hence the norm of the generalized gradient of
d_{\mathsf{M}}
at
p(x)
is

\|\nabla d_{\mathsf{M}}(p(x))\|=\frac{d(p(x),(x,0))}{d(p(x),(x,1+x^{3}))}=\frac{3x^{2}+O(x^{5})}{1+O(x^{3})}=3x^{2}+o(x^{2}),

while the distance from
p(x)
to
z_{0}
is
x+o(x)
. This shows that
{\mathsf{M}}
does not satisfy condition
(P4)
. It is easy to modify this example so that
{\mathsf{M}}
be compact, yet the behaviour of the
\mu
-critical points around
z_{0}
remain the same.

Figure 5
:
The point
p(x)
satisfies
m(\pi_{{\mathsf{M}}}(p(x)))=x
, with
\|\nabla d_{\mathsf{M}}(p(x))\|=3x^{2}+o(x^{2})\ll\|x\|
for small values of
x
. Hence,
p(x)
is a
\mu
-critical point with
\mu=3x^{2}+o(x^{2})
but at distance from
z_{0}
greater than
x\gg\mu
(when
x
is small).

Let us now prove
Proposition

6.5
.

Proof.

Let
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
be a critical point with projections
\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\}
, and let
\delta^{\prime},\delta,\mu_{0}>0
be small enough that
p_{j}:B(z_{0},\delta^{\prime})\rightarrow B(x_{j},\delta)\cap{\mathsf{M}}
is as in Lemma
4.8
for
j=1,\ldots,s
and that the conclusions of Lemma
6.2
stand, i.e. if
z\in B(z_{0},\delta^{\prime})
is
\mu_{0}
-critical, then
I(z)=[s]
and

\|\pi_{E}(z-z_{0})\|\leq C\|z-z_{0}\|^{2}

for some
C>0
that depends on
z_{0}
. Further assume that the conclusion of
Lemma

6.1
hold, that is the function
g_{[s]}
is
\ell
-Lipschitz continuous.
Let
z\in B(z_{0},\delta^{\prime})
be
\mu_{0}
-critical. Then

|\|\nabla d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)\|-g_{[s]}(z_{0}+h)|=|g_{[s]}(z)-g_{[s]}(z_{0}+h)|\leq\ell C_{0}\|z-z_{0}\|^{2}.

(21)

Now for
h\in E^{\perp}
with
\|h\|\leq\delta^{\prime}
, let
\tilde{\Delta}(h)
be the
(s-1)
-simplex whose vertices are the points
p_{j}(z_{0}+h)
for
j\in[s]
, and let
\Delta(h)
be the
s
-simplex obtained by adding the vertex
z_{0}+h
to those of
\tilde{\Delta}(h)
. Let
\tilde{E}(h)
be the affine space spanned by
\tilde{\Delta}(h)
.
The
s
-volume of
\Delta(h)
can be computed as

\mathrm{Vol}_{s}(\Delta(h))=\frac{1}{s}\mathrm{Vol}_{s-1}(\tilde{\Delta}(h))d(z_{0}+h,\tilde{E}(h)).

Note that according to
(P1)
, when
h=0
, the projection of
z_{0}+h
on
\tilde{E}(h)
belongs to the interior of the simplex
\tilde{\Delta}(h)
. By continuity of the projections
p_{j}
, this property is still valid when
h
is small enough. Hence, for
h
small enough,

\mathrm{Vol}_{s}(\Delta(h))=\frac{1}{s}\mathrm{Vol}_{s-1}(\tilde{\Delta}(h))d(z_{0}+h,\tilde{\Delta}(h)).

(22)

(BSP)
\implies
(P4)
: Assume that
(BSP)
is satisfied. Then,
d(z_{0}+h,\tilde{\Delta}(h))\leq\|z_{0}+h-m_{[s]}(z_{0}+h)\|=d_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0}+h)g_{[s]}(z_{0}+h)
. Furthermore, the volume of
\mathrm{Vol}_{s-1}(\tilde{\Delta}(h))
can be crudely bounded by
(\mathrm{diam}(M))^{s-1}
.
Hence, if
z
is a
\mu_{0}
-critical point close enough to
z_{0}
that
h=\pi_{E^{\perp}}(z-z_{0})
satisfies the condition
(BSP)
, we obtain that

L\|h\|\leq\mathrm{Vol}_{s}(\Delta(h))\leq\frac{(\mathrm{diam}(M))^{s-1}}{s}d_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0}+h)g_{[s]}(z_{0}+h)\leq\frac{(\mathrm{diam}(M))^{s}}{s}g_{[s]}(z_{0}+h).

Hence, (
21
) yields

\displaystyle\|\nabla d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)\|\geq\frac{s}{(\mathrm{diam}(M))^{s}}L\|h\|-\ell C_{0}\|z-z_{0}\|^{2}.

Lemma

6.2
also implies that
\|h\|\geq\|z-z_{0}\|/2
if
\|z-z_{0}\|
is small enough. Hence, we obtain for
z\in B(z_{0},\delta^{\prime})
and
\delta^{\prime}
small enough

\|\nabla d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)\|\geq\|z-z_{0}\|\left(\frac{sL}{2(\mathrm{diam}(M))^{s}}-\delta^{\prime}\ell C_{0}\right)=\frac{\|z-z_{0}\|}{C_{1}},

where
C_{1}>0
for
\delta^{\prime}
small enough. In particular, if
z
is a
\mu
-critical point for
z\in B(z_{0},\delta^{\prime})
with
\mu<\mu_{0}
, then
\|z-z_{0}\|\leq C_{1}\mu
.

We have shown that
(BSP)
implies
(P4)
”locally”. To conclude “globally”, we proceed as follows : the constants
\mu_{0},\delta^{\prime}
and
C_{1}
depend on the chosen critical point
z_{0}
. As
{\mathsf{M}}
satisfies property
(P2)
, it admits only finitely many critical points. Let
\overline{\mu}_{0}
,
\overline{\delta}^{\prime}
and
\overline{C}_{1}
be the minima over the critical points of the associated constants
\mu_{0}
,
\delta^{\prime}
and
C_{1}
respectively.
Then any
\overline{\mu}_{0}
-critical
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
that is at distance less than
\overline{\delta}^{\prime}
from a critical point
z_{0}
of
{\mathsf{M}}
verifies

\overline{C}_{1}\|\nabla d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)\|\geq\|z-z_{0}\|\geq d(z,Z({\mathsf{M}})).

(23)

Now remember that we have shown at the start of this section that
\eta_{{\mathsf{M}}}(r)=\sup\{r:\ \exists x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\text{ s.t. }\|\nabla d_{\mathsf{M}}(x)\|\leq\mu\text{ and }d(Z({\mathsf{M}}),x)=r\}
goes to
0
as
\mu\rightarrow 0
. This means that there exists
0<\mu_{1}<\overline{\mu}_{0}
such that any
\mu_{1}
-critical point
z
must be at distance less than
\overline{\delta}^{\prime}
from
Z({\mathsf{M}})
, where (
23
) applies. This allows us to conclude.

(P4)
\implies
(BSP):
Assume now that
(P4)
is satisfied with constants
C
and
\mu_{0}
, and consider again
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
with the associated spaces
E
and
E^{\perp}
. Let
h\in E^{\perp}
be small enough that
Lemma

6.4
applies: there exists a
\mu_{0}
critical point
z
of
{\mathsf{M}}
with
\pi_{E^{\perp}}(z-z_{0})=h
,
I(z)=[s]
and
\|\pi_{E}(z-z_{0})\|\leq C_{2}\|h\|^{2}
for some constant
C_{2}>0
that is independent from the choice of
h
. Condition
(P4)
ensures that

C\|\nabla d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)\|\geq\|z-z_{0}\|\geq\|h\|.

(24)

Let
\tilde{\Delta}(z-z_{0})
be the simplex with vertices
p_{j}(z)
for
j\in[s]
(assuming that
h
is small enough for the projections
p_{j}(z)
to be well-defined).
By continuity, as
\tilde{\Delta}(0)
is a nondegenerate simplex (since
{\mathsf{M}}
satisfies
(P1)
), the volume
\mathrm{Vol}_{s-1}(\tilde{\Delta}(h))
is lower bounded for
h
small enough.
As the projections
\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z)
coincide with the vertices of
\tilde{\Delta}(z-z_{0})
, we also have that
d(z,\tilde{\Delta}(z-z_{0}))=\|z-m_{[s]}(z)\|
(as noted in
[
CCSL06
, Section 2.1]
).
Thus, starting from Equation (
22
) and for
h
small enough,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Vol}_{s}(\Delta(h))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{s}\mathrm{Vol}_{s-1}(\tilde{\Delta}(h))d(z_{0}+h,\tilde{\Delta}(h))

\displaystyle\geq C_{3}(d(z,\tilde{\Delta}(z-z_{0}))-\|z_{0}+h-z\|-d_{H}(\tilde{\Delta}(h),\tilde{\Delta}(z-z_{0})))

\displaystyle\geq C_{3}(\|z-m_{[s]}(z)\|-C_{2}\|h\|^{2}-\max_{j\in[s]}\|p_{j}(z)-p_{j}(z_{0}+h)\|)

\displaystyle\geq C_{3}(d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)\|\nabla d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)\|-C_{2}\|h\|^{2}-KC_{2}\|h\|^{2})

\displaystyle\geq C_{3}(C_{4}\|h\|-C_{2}\|h\|^{2}-KC_{2}\|h\|^{2})

where we use the
K
-Lipschitz continuity of the
p_{j}
s on a neighborhood of
z_{0}
for some constant
K>0
, Inequality (
24
), and the fact that
d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)
is lower bounded on a neighborhood of
z_{0}
. Hence, for
\|h\|
smaller than some constant
\delta^{\prime\prime}
, this quantity is larger than
\frac{C_{3}C_{4}}{2}\|h\|=L\|h\|
. This proves that
(BSP)
holds at any given point
z_{0}\in{\mathsf{M}}
, concluding the proof.
∎

We choose to translate
(P4)
into the Big Simplex Property because the volume of the simplex
\Delta(h)
(in the notations of the proof of Proposition
6.5
above) can be easily expressed in terms of the first derivative of the projections
p_{i}
, which themselves depend on the shape operator of
{\mathsf{M}}
around the points
x_{i}\in\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})
; this is formally stated in the Lemma
6.7
further below. This will be used for both showing that the condition is open in
Section

8
(along with Conditions
(P1)
,
(P2)
and
(P3)
) and that it is dense in
Section

9
.

6.3
Computing the volume of the simplex

We compute an approximation of the volume of the simplex
\Delta(h)
introduced in Subsection
6.2
expressed using the differentials of the local projections
p_{i}:B(z_{0},\delta^{\prime})\rightarrow B(x_{i},\delta)\cap{\mathsf{M}}
, where
{\mathsf{M}}
is as above a
C^{2}
compact manifold satisfying
(P1)
,
(P2)
and
(P3)
. This allows us to characterize
(BSP)
(and hence
(P4)
) purely locally, in terms of the critical points
z_{0}
, their projections
x_{i}
and the curvature of
{\mathsf{M}}
at the points
x_{i}
which determines the differentials of the maps
p_{i}
.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
be a critical point of
{\mathsf{M}}
with projections
\pi_{{\mathsf{M}}}(z_{0})=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\}
. Let
\delta^{\prime},\delta>0
be small enough that the projections
p_{i}:B(z_{0},\delta^{\prime})\rightarrow B(x_{i},\delta)\cap{\mathsf{M}}
from Lemma
4.8
are well-defined. For
h\in E^{\perp}
small enough, let as before
\Delta(h)
be the
s
-simplex with vertices
z_{0}+h
and
p_{j}(z_{0}+h)
for
j\in[s]
. Let
X=[x_{1}-z_{0},\dots,x_{s}-z_{0}]\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D\times s}
, and let
R_{i}(h)
be the matrix obtaining by replacing the
i
th column of
X
by
r_{i}(h)=p_{i}(z_{0}+h)-x_{i}-h
. Define likewise
A_{i}(h)
, where we replace the
i
th column of
X
by
(dp_{i})_{z_{0}}(h)-h
. Then, for any
h\in E^{\perp}
with
\|h\|<\delta^{\prime}
it holds that

\begin{split}\mathrm{Vol}_{s}(\Delta(h))^{2}&=\frac{1}{(s!)^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{s}\det(R_{i}(h)^{\top}R_{j}(h))+O(\|h\|^{3})\\
&=\frac{1}{(s!)^{2}}B(h)+o(\|h\|^{2}),\end{split}

where
B
is the quadratic form given by

B(h)=\sum_{i,j=1}^{s}\det(A_{i}(h)^{\top}A_{j}(h)).

(25)

Furthermore, the constant in the term
O(h^{3})
depends only on
s
,
\mathrm{diam}(M)
, and the Lipschitz constants of the projections
p_{j}
,
j\in[s]
.

Controlling the constant in the term
O(h^{3})
will be needed in Section
8
, once we let the embedding of the manifold into
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
vary.

Proof.

Let
h\in E^{\perp}
be such that
\|h\|<\delta^{\prime}
and let

v_{j}=p_{j}(z_{0}+h)-(z_{0}+h)=(x_{j}-z_{0})+r_{j}(h)=(x_{j}-z_{0})+(dp_{j})_{z_{0}}(h)-h+o(\|h\|)

for
j\in[s]
. Let
L(h)
be the
l\times l
matrix with
L_{i,j}(h)=\langle v_{i},v_{j}\rangle
. Then,

\mathrm{Vol}_{s}(\Delta(h))=\frac{1}{s!}\det\left(L(h)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Define the
D\times s
matrices
V=[v_{1},\dots,v_{s}]
,
X=[x_{1}-z_{0},\dots,x_{s}-z_{0}]
. For
\tau\subset[s]
, let
R_{\tau}(h)
be the matrix obtained from
X
by replacing the
i
th column of
X
by
r_{i}(h)
for all
i\in\tau
. We write
R_{i}(h)=R_{\{i\}}(h)
and
R_{i,j}(h)=R_{\{i,j\}}(h)
.
By multilinearity of the determinant, as the columns of the matrix
V^{\top}V
are of the form
V^{\top}(x_{i}-z_{0})+V^{\top}r_{i}(h)
, it holds that

\displaystyle\det(L(h))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\subset[s]}\det(V^{\top}R_{\tau}(h))

\displaystyle=\det(V^{\top}X)+\sum_{j=1}^{s}\det(V^{\top}R_{j}(h))+\sum_{i\neq j}\det(V^{\top}R_{i,j}(h))+O(\|h\|^{3}).

Similarly, we write for any matrix
M
of size
D\times l
,

\displaystyle\det(V^{\top}M)=\det(X^{\top}M)+\sum_{i=1}^{s}\det(R_{i}(h)^{\top}M)+\sum_{i\neq j}\det(R_{i,j}(h)^{\top}M)+O(\|h\|^{3}).

Hence,

\displaystyle\det(L(h))

\displaystyle=\det(X^{\top}X)+2\sum_{i=1}^{s}\det(R_{i}(h)^{\top}X)+2\sum_{i\neq j}\det(R_{i,j}(h)^{\top}X)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i,j=1}^{l}\det(R_{i}(h)^{\top}R_{j}(h))+O(\|h\|^{3}).

Any matrix of the form
X^{\top}M
is non-invertible as
X
is not of full rank. Hence,

\displaystyle\det(L(h))=\sum_{i,j=1}^{l}\det(R_{i}(h)^{\top}R_{j}(h))+O(\|h\|^{3}).

Note that the
O(\|h\|^{3})
can actually be made explicit, and is given as a sum of terms of the form
\det(R_{\tau}(h)^{\top}R_{\tau^{\prime}}(h))
where
|\tau|+|\tau^{\prime}|\geq 3
.
As the
p_{j}
s are
\ell
-Lipschitz continuous for some
\ell>0
, we have
\|r_{j}(h)\|\leq(\ell+1)\|h\|
for
j\in[s]
. As
\|x_{j}-z_{0}\|\leq\mathrm{diam}({\mathsf{M}})
, we can use the multilinearity of the determinant to show that
|\det(R_{\tau}(h)^{\top}R_{\tau^{\prime}}(h))|
is smaller than

C\mathrm{diam}({\mathsf{M}})^{s-|\tau|}((\ell+1)\|h\|)^{|\tau|}\mathrm{diam}({\mathsf{M}})^{s-|\tau^{\prime}|}((\ell+1)\|h\|)^{|\tau^{\prime}|},

where
C
is the maximal determinant of a
s\times s
matrix of dot products of vectors of
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
of norm smaller than
1
. This is of order
\|h\|^{3}
, with a constant depending only on
\mathrm{diam}({\mathsf{M}})
,
s
and
\ell
.

It remains to prove the second equality in
Lemma

6.7
, which follows from the equality
R_{i}(h)=A_{i}(h)+o(\|h\|)
and that the
i
th columns of
A_{i}(h)
and
R_{i}(h)
are of order
O(\|h\|)
.
∎

We get the following characterization of
(BSP)
as a simple corollary of
Lemma

6.7
:

Corollary 6.8
.

Let
{\mathsf{M}}
be a
C^{2}
compact manifold satisfying
(P1)
,
(P2)
and
(P3)
. Then
{\mathsf{M}}
satisfies
(P4)
if and only if for any
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
the associated quadratic form
B:E^{\perp}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
defined in
Lemma

6.7
is non-degenerate.

Proof.

Property
(BSP)
is satisfied if and only if for any
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
, there exist
L,\delta^{\prime}>0
such that for any
h\in E^{\perp}
with
\|h\|\leq\delta^{\prime}
, the
s
-volume of the associated
s
-simplex
\Delta(h)
satisfies

\mathrm{Vol}_{s}(\Delta(h))\geq L\|h\|.

As we have shown in
Lemma

6.7
that

\mathrm{Vol}_{s}(\Delta(h))=\frac{1}{s!}\sqrt{B(h)+o(\|h\|^{2})}
,
this is true if and only if
B:E^{\perp}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is non-degenerate for any such
z_{0}
. As
(BSP)
is equivalent to
(P4)
according to
Proposition

6.5
, the conclusion holds.
∎

7
Topological Morse theory for distance functions to generic submanifolds

Let
{\mathsf{M}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
be a
C^{2}
compact submanifold.
In this section, we show that Conditions
(P1-4)
on
{\mathsf{M}}
lead to Morse-like properties for the distance function
d_{\mathsf{M}}
to
{\mathsf{M}}
, as stated in Theorem
1.8
.
A similar exposition can be found in
[
SYM23
]
in the case of hypersurfaces.

Let us first define
topological Morse functions
, which were initially introduced in
[
Mor59
]
.
Let
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
be an open set and let
f:U\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a continuous function.
A point
z\in U
is said to be a
topological regular point of
f
if there is a homeomorphism
\phi:V_{1}\to V_{2}
between open neighborhoods
V_{1}
of
0
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
V_{2}
of
U
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
with
\phi(0)=z
and such that for all
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in V_{1}
,

f\circ\phi(x)=f(z)+x_{d}.

(26)

Conversely, a point
z\in U
is said to be a
topological critical point of
f
if it is not a topological regular point of
f
.
Finally, a point
z\in U
is said to be a
non-degenerate topological critical point of
f
of index
i
if there exist an integer
0\leq i\leq d
and a homeomorphism
\phi:V_{1}\to U_{2}
between open neighborhoods
V_{1}
of
0
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
V_{2}
of
U
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
with
\phi(0)=z
such that for all
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in V_{1}
,

f\circ\phi(x)=f(z)-\sum_{j=1}^{i}x_{j}^{2}+\sum_{j=i+1}^{d}x_{j}^{2}.

(27)

The function
f
is said to be a
topological Morse function
if all its topological critical points are non-degenerate.

In what follows, we let “critical points” refer to the gradient-based definition that we have been using up till now, i.e. those
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\backslash{\mathsf{M}}
such that
\nabla d_{\mathsf{M}}(x)=0
, and “topological critical points” to the definition from the previous paragraph.
The first lemma of this section states that non-critical points are also topologically non-critical.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\backslash{\mathsf{M}}
.
If
x\not\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
, then
x
is a topological regular point for
d_{\mathsf{M}}
.

Proof.

One can use similar arguments as those in the proof of Lemma 1.4 from
[
Che91
]
or Proposition 1.7 from
[
Gro93
]
.
Briefly summarized, there exists a small open neighborhood
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
of
x
, a constant
C>0
and a unit vector
X\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
such that the integral flow
\Psi_{X}
of the constant vector field associated to
X
on
U
satisfies

d_{\mathsf{M}}(\Psi_{X}(z,t_{2}))-d_{\mathsf{M}}(\Psi_{X}(z,t_{1}))\geq C(t_{2}-t_{1})

(28)

for all
z\in U
and
t_{1},t_{2}
close enough to
0
.
Up to a rotation and an affine transformation (to simplify notations), we can assume that
x=0\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
and
X=(0,\ldots,0,1)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, and let
\pi:{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{D-1}
be the orthogonal projection on the first
D-1
coordinates.
Then for every
y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D-1}
and every
r\in{\mathbb{R}}
close enough to
0
, Equation (
28
) ensures that there exists a unique
t(y,r)\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
d_{\mathsf{M}}((y,t(y,r)))=d_{\mathsf{M}}(x)+r
, and that
(y,r)\mapsto t(y,r)
is continuous.
This allows us to define the desired local change of coordinates
\phi:(y,r)\mapsto(y,t(y,r))
, whose inverse is
z\mapsto(\pi(z),d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)-d_{\mathsf{M}}(x))
.
∎

Note that the lemma does not assume that
{\mathsf{M}}
satisfies Properties
(P1-4)
.

To handle critical points, we will need the machinery of
Min-type functions
from
[
GR97
]
.
Given
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
and two real-valued functions
f,g
each defined on some neighborhood of
x
, we write
f\simeq_{x}g
if the germs of
f
and
g
at
x
are equal.
Let
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
be an open set and
f:U\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
be
C^{k}
for some
k\geq 1
.
We say that
f
is
C^{k}
Min-type at
x\in U
if there exist real-valued
C^{k}
functions
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}
defined on some neighborhood of
x
such that

f\simeq_{x}\min\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\}.

Furthermore, assume that there exists such a family
\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\}
that also satisfies the following properties:

1.

\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\}
is a representation of
f
at
x
of minimal size; in other words, if a family of
C^{k}
functions
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{m}
is such that
f\simeq_{x}\min\{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{m}\}
, then
m\geq n
.

2.

The gradients
\nabla_{x}\alpha_{1},\ldots,\nabla_{x}\alpha_{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
are the vertices of a non-degenerate simplex.

3.

0\in\mathrm{Conv}(\nabla_{x}\alpha_{1},\ldots,\nabla_{x}\alpha_{n})
.

4.

The restriction of
f
to the locally defined
C^{k}
submanifold
\{\alpha_{1}=\ldots=\alpha_{n}\}
is a Morse function around
x
.

5.

Any subset of cardinality
n-1
of
\{\nabla_{x}\alpha_{1},\ldots,\nabla_{x}\alpha_{n}\}
is linearly independent in
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
.

Then
x
is said to be a
non-degenerate Min-type critical point
of
f
(notions of degenerate Min-type critical points and Min-type regular points also exist, but do not serve our current purpose).
Theorem 1 from Subsection 3.2 of
[
GR97
]
states if
f
is
C^{k}
Min-type (for any
k\geq 1
) at some
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, and if
x
is a non-degenerate Min-type critical point of
f
, then it is also a non-degenerate topological critical point of
f
.
Hence, to prove the following proposition, we only have to show that if
{\mathsf{M}}
satisfies
(P1-4)
and
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
, then the distance function
d_{\mathsf{M}}
is
C^{1}
Min-type at
z_{0}
and satisfies the five conditions listed above.

Lemma 7.2
.

Assume that
{\mathsf{M}}
satisfies Properties
(P1-4)
, and let
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
. Then
z_{0}
is a non-degenerate topological critical point of
d_{\mathsf{M}}
.

Proof.

Let us first show that
d_{\mathsf{M}}
is
C^{1}
Min-type at
z_{0}
. Let
\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\}
; we have shown in Lemma
4.8
that there exists (disjoint) neighborhoods
U
of
z_{0}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
and
{\mathsf{M}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathsf{M}}_{s}
of
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}
in
{\mathsf{M}}
and
C^{1}
maps
p_{i}:U\rightarrow{\mathsf{M}}_{i}
(for
i\in[s]
) such that for any
z\in U
, we have

\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z)\subset\{p_{1}(z),\ldots,p_{s}(z)\}
.
This means in particular that

d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)=\min\{d(z,p_{1}(z)),\ldots,d(z,p_{s}(z))\}

for
z\in U
.
It was also shown in the proof of Lemma
6.3
that the functions
d_{{\mathsf{M}}_{i}}:z\mapsto d(z,p_{i}(z))
were
C^{2}
, hence
d_{\mathsf{M}}
is
C^{2}
Min-type at
z_{0}
(in fact, it is
C^{k}
Min-type, where
k
is the regularity of
{\mathsf{M}}
).

Let us now show that
z_{0}
is a non-degenerate Min-type critical point of
d_{\mathsf{M}}
.
The fact that
d_{{\mathsf{M}}_{1}},\ldots,d_{{\mathsf{M}}_{s}}
is a minimal representation of
d_{\mathsf{M}}
is stated in Lemma 8 of
[
SYM23
]
.
As the gradient of
d_{{\mathsf{M}}_{i}}
at
z_{0}
is equal to
\frac{z_{0}-x_{i}}{\|z_{0}-x_{i}\|}=(z_{0}-x_{i})d_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})^{-1}
, Conditions 2. and 3. from the definition are guaranteed by
(P1)
.
So is Condition 5., as
z_{0}
belonging to the relative interior of
\mathrm{Conv}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})
is equivalent to
0
belonging to the relative interior of
\mathrm{Conv}(x_{1}-z_{0},\ldots,x_{s}-z_{0})=-d_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})\mathrm{Conv}(\nabla_{z_{0}}d_{{\mathsf{M}}_{1}},\ldots,\nabla_{z_{0}}d_{{\mathsf{M}}_{s}})
, which is itself equivalent to
0
not belonging to any strict face of the simplex
\mathrm{Conv}(\nabla_{z_{0}}d_{{\mathsf{M}}_{1}},\ldots,\nabla_{z_{0}}d_{{\mathsf{M}}_{s}})
, which is finally equivalent to the linear independence of any strict subset of
\{\nabla_{z_{0}}d_{{\mathsf{M}}_{1}},\ldots,\nabla_{z_{0}}d_{{\mathsf{M}}_{s}}\}
.

Furthermore, let us remark that the function
g
given by the restriction of
d_{{\mathsf{M}}}
to the core medial axis
M_{c}(z_{0},{\mathsf{M}})=\{d_{{\mathsf{M}}_{1}}=\ldots=d_{{\mathsf{M}}_{s}}\}
described in Lemma
6.3
is
C^{2}
(it is equal to the
C^{2}
function
d_{{\mathsf{M}}_{1}}
on the core medial axis). Besides,
g
has a nondegenerate Hessian at
z_{0}
. Indeed, for
z\in M_{c}(z_{0},{\mathsf{M}})
and any
j\in[s]
, the gradient
\nabla g(z)
is given by the projection on
T_{z}M_{c}(z_{0},{\mathsf{M}})
of
z-p_{j}(z)
, that is
\nabla g(z)=\frac{\pi_{T_{z}M_{c}(z_{0},{\mathsf{M}})}(z-p_{j}(z))}{d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)}
.
As the center
m_{z}=m(\{p_{j}(z):\ j\in[s]\})
of the smallest enclosing ball of the projections
p_{1}(z),\dots,p_{s}(z)
is written as a convex combination of the projections, we also have

\nabla g(z)=\frac{\pi_{T_{z}M_{c}(z_{0},{\mathsf{M}})}(z-m_{z})}{d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)}.

But if
z
is close enough to
z_{0}
, then
m_{z}
is the orthogonal projection of
z
onto the affine space
\mathrm{Aff}(p_{1}(z),\ldots,p_{s}(z))
spanned by the points
p_{j}(z)
.
Indeed, this is the case if and only if
z-m_{z}
is perpendicular to
\mathrm{Aff}(p_{1}(z),\ldots,p_{s}(z))
,
which is equivalent to
\{p_{1}(z),\ldots,p_{s}(z)\}\subset\left(m_{z}+(z-m_{z})^{\bot}\right)\cap\overline{B}(z,d_{\mathsf{M}}(z))
,
which is itself equivalent to the boundary of the smallest enclosing ball of the set
\{p_{1}(z),\ldots,p_{s}(z)\}
(whose center is
m_{z}
) being equal to its circumsphere.
But the boundary of the smallest enclosing ball of a set
\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}\}
and its circumsphere coincide if and only if the center of the circumsphere belongs to the convex hull of the set. This property is satisfied for
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}
(as
z_{0}
is critical), and as
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}
form a non-degenerate simplex in
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, this remains true for
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}
close enough to
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}
, hence for
p_{1}(z),\ldots,p_{s}(z)
with
z
close enough to
z_{0}
.
As
M_{c}(z_{0},M)
is defined as the set of points equidistant to all the sets
M_{j}
s, the vector space spanned by the vectors
\{p_{j}(z)-p_{1}(z):\ j\in[s]\}
is actually the orthogonal of the tangent space
T_{z}M_{c}(z_{0},{\mathsf{M}})
. Hence,
m_{z}
being the orthogonal projection of
z
onto the affine space spanned by the points
p_{j}(z)
means that
z-m_{z}\in T_{z}M_{c}(z_{0},{\mathsf{M}})
, and

\nabla g(z)=\frac{z-m_{z}}{d_{\mathsf{M}}(z)}.

According to
(P4)
, which is the key ingredient here, when
z
is close enough to
z_{0}
, the norm of
\nabla g(z)
satisfies an inequality of the type
\|\nabla g(z)\|\geq c\|z-z_{0}\|
(as
\|\nabla g(z)\|
is also the norm of the generalized gradient of the unrestricted distance function
d_{\mathsf{M}}
, which means that
z
is
\|\nabla g(z)\|
-critical). In particular, the Hessian of
g
at
z_{0}
is non degenerate, and Condition 4. is satisfied.

As mentioned before the statement of the lemma, applying Theorem 1 from Subsection 3.2 of
[
GR97
]
is now enough to conclude.
∎

Remark 7.3
.

The critical points of
d_{\mathsf{M}}
can be more finely characterised as follows: assume as in the lemma that
{\mathsf{M}}
satisfies Properties
(P1-4)
, and let
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
with
\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\}
. As shown in the proof, the restriction of
d_{\mathsf{M}}
to the core medial axis
M_{c}(z_{0},{\mathsf{M}})=\{d_{{\mathsf{M}}_{1}}=\ldots=d_{{\mathsf{M}}_{s}}\}
on a neighborhood of
z_{0}
is a
C^{2}
Morse function, and
z_{0}
is a critical point of this function. Let
i(z_{0},d_{\mathsf{M}}|_{M_{c}(z_{0},{\mathsf{M}})})
be its index (with respect to the restricted function).
Then Theorem 1 from Subsection 3.2 of
[
GR97
]
also states that the index
i
of
z_{0}
as a topological critical point of the (unrestricted) distance function
d_{\mathsf{M}}
is

i=s-1+i(z_{0},d_{\mathsf{M}}|_{M_{c}(z_{0},{\mathsf{M}})}).

The two integers
(s,i(z_{0},d_{\mathsf{M}}|_{M_{c}(z_{0},{\mathsf{M}})})
provide a more precise description of the geometry of
d_{\mathsf{M}}
around
z_{0}
than its index alone does.

We can now prove Theorem
1.8
, which we restate here for the reader’s convenience.
See
1.8

Proof.

The two previous lemmas have shown that all points
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\backslash{\mathsf{M}}
are non-degenerate topological critical points for
d_{\mathsf{M}}
if
z\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
, and topological regular points otherwise.
This makes the restriction of
d_{\mathsf{M}}
to
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\backslash{\mathsf{M}}
a topological Morse function.
As noted in the Introduction, the Isotopy Lemma for
a>0
was proved in
[
Gro93
]
for the distance function to any compact set; it trivially extends to the case
a=0
in the case of submanifolds.
The Handle Attachment Lemma proceeds from arguments that are similar to those used for smooth Morse functions –see
[
SYM23
, Theorem 5]
for a detailed proof.
∎

8
The
C^{2}
stability theorem

Let as usual
M
be a
C^{k}
compact manifold for some
k\geq 2
.
In this section, we show that the set of all embeddings
i\in\mathrm{Emb}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
such that
i(M)
satisfies Conditions
(P1)
,
(P2)
,
(P3)
and
(P4)
is open for the Whitney
C^{2}
-topology, or in other words that the conjunction of the four conditions is stable with respect to small
C^{2}
perturbations.
This proves the “open” part of the Genericity Theorem
1.1
.
We also show that for an embedding
i
that satisfies
(P1-4)
, small
C^{2}
perturbations of
i
leave the critical points of
i(M)
and their projections stable.
The
C^{2}
Stability Theorem below, whose proof makes many of the more technical results of the previous sections necessary, is the combination of these statements.

See
1.4

Before proving
1.4
, we give three (counter)examples showcasing degenerate behaviors when
(P1-4)
is not satisfied.

Example 8.1
.

It is interesting to note that while the conjunction of
(P1)
,
(P2)
,
(P3)
and
(P4)
defines an open condition on the set of embeddings
\mathrm{Emb}^{2}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
, this is e.g. not true of
(P1)
,
(P2)
and
(P4)
without
(P3)
, as showcased by the following example: consider the curve
{\mathsf{M}}
illustrated on the left of Figure
6
. The right part is parameterized around
x=(0,1)
as
\theta\mapsto(1+\theta^{4})(\cos(\theta),\sin(\theta))
for
\theta\in]-\frac{\pi}{4},\frac{\pi}{4}[
, and there is a critical point at
z_{0}=(0,0)
with
\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})=\{x,x_{1},x_{2}\}
. The curve
{\mathsf{M}}
satisfies
(P1)
,
(P2)
and
(P4)
, but not
(P3)
due to its curvature around
x
. Consider now any
C^{\infty}
function
f:{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
such that
f(\theta)=\theta^{2}
for any
\theta\in]-\frac{\pi}{8},\frac{\pi}{8}[
,
f(\theta)\leq\theta^{2}
for any
\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
f(\theta)=0
for any
\theta
with
|\theta|\geq\frac{\pi}{4}
. For
a>0
, consider the modified curve
{\mathsf{M}}_{a}
shown on the right of Figure
6
and obtained by leaving
{\mathsf{M}}
untouched, except around
x
where it is parameterized by

\theta\mapsto(1+\theta^{4}-af(\theta))(\cos(\theta),\sin(\theta))

for
\theta\in]-\frac{\pi}{4},\frac{\pi}{4}[
. Let
i_{0}:M\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
be some embedding whose image is
{\mathsf{M}}
; then for any
k\geq 0
, there is a constant
C_{k}>0
and an embedding
i_{a}:M\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
whose image is
{\mathsf{M}}_{a}
and at distance less than
C_{k}a
in norm
\|-\|_{C^{k}}
from
i_{0}
. Yet for any
a>0
small enough, there is a critical point
z_{a}
of
{\mathsf{M}}_{a}
close to
z_{0}
with four projections (which is more than
D+1=3
), two of them resulting from a “splitting” of
x
, hence
{\mathsf{M}}_{a}
does not satisfy
(P1)
.

Example 8.2
.

Consider (some compact version of) the curve
{\mathsf{M}}
shown on the left of Figure
7
. While Conditions
(P2)
,
(P3)
,
(P4)
and the first part of Condition
(P1)
are satisfied, the second part of Condition
(P1)
is not, as the critical point
z_{0}
belongs to the boundary of the simplex whose vertices are its projections
\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})=\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\}
.
A small perturbation results in the curve
{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}
shown on the right of the figure, where
z_{0}
has split into two critical points (one with three projections and one with only two), illustrating how
(P1)
is necessary to the stability of
Z({\mathsf{M}})
.

Figure 6:
Illustration of Example
8.1
: the curve
{\mathsf{M}}
on the left satisfies Conditions
(P1)
,
(P2)
and
(P4)
, but not
(P3)
. An arbitrarily small modification results in the curve
{\mathsf{M}}_{a}
on the right, which does not fulfill Condition
(P1)
.

Figure 7:
Illustration of Example
8.2
: the critical point
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
splits into two critical points of the perturbed curve
{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}
.

Example 8.3
.

Critical points may not be stable when Condition
(P4)
is not satisfied. Consider the curve
{\mathsf{M}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
given in
Example

6.6
, that is equal on
\Omega=[-1,1]\times[-2,2]
to the union of the graph of the functions
f_{0}:x\mapsto 1+x^{3}
and
-f_{0}:x\mapsto-1-x^{3}
. For
a>0
, consider the curve
{\mathsf{M}}_{a}
given by the union of the graph of
f_{a}:x\mapsto 1+ax+x^{3}
and
-f_{a}
on
\Omega
(and modified outside
\Omega
so that
{\mathsf{M}}_{a}
is close to
{\mathsf{M}}
in the
C^{2}
-Whitney topology).
The point
(0,0)
is a critical point of
{\mathsf{M}}
, but the curve
{\mathsf{M}}_{a}
has no critical points in
B((0,0),1)
. Indeed, if
z
was such a point, it would have a strictly greater
x
coordinate than all of its projections, due to the orthogonality of the projection and to the strict monotonicity of
f_{a}
; hence
z
would not belong to the convex hull of its projections, leading to a contradiction.
Hence, the critical point
(0,0)\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
disappears in the perturbed submanifold
{\mathsf{M}}_{a}
, and the existence of a bijection
\Psi
between critical points as in
1.4
does not hold on a neighborhood of
{\mathsf{M}}
.

The remainder of this section is dedicated to proving
1.4
. We decompose the proof in a sequence of claims.

Let
\varepsilon>0
, and let us assume that
\varepsilon
is small enough that the balls of radius
\varepsilon
around the critical points
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
and around their projections are all pairwise disjoint.

Claim 1
.

For any
\delta>0
, we can make a neighborhood
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathrm{Emb}^{2}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
of
i
small enough that if
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
, the critical points of
{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}
are
\delta
-close to those of
{\mathsf{M}}
.

Proof.

Indeed, let
\tau({\mathsf{M}})>0
be the reach of
{\mathsf{M}}
. If
\|i-i^{\prime}\|_{C^{2}}
is small enough, then
\tau({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})>\tau({\mathsf{M}})/2
(see
[
Fed59
, Theorem 4.19]
) and Theorem 3.4 from
[
CCSL06
]
states that each critical point
z_{0}^{\prime}\in Z({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})
(which is necessarily at distance at least
\tau({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})
from
{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}
) is at distance at most
C_{1}\sqrt{d_{H}({\mathsf{M}},{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})}\leq C_{1}\sqrt{\|i-i^{\prime}\|_{C^{2}}}
from a
\mu
-critical point of
{\mathsf{M}}
, with
\mu=C_{2}\sqrt{d_{H}({\mathsf{M}},{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})}\leq C_{2}\sqrt{\|i-i^{\prime}\|_{C^{2}}}
for some constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
that depend on
{\mathsf{M}}
.
As observed at the start of Section
6
, there exists
\mu_{0}>0
such that any
\mu_{0}
-critical point of
{\mathsf{M}}
is at distance at most
\delta/2
from
Z({\mathsf{M}})
. Hence if
\|i-i^{\prime}\|_{C^{2}}
is small enough, any critical point of
{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}
is at distance at most
\delta/2
of a
\mu_{0}
-critical point of
{\mathsf{M}}
, which is itself at distance at most
\delta/2
from a critical point of
{\mathsf{M}}
, which proves our claim. Note that the proof does not make use of Properties
(P1-4)
.
∎

Now let
\mathcal{U}
be a neighborhood of
i
small enough that for
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
each point
z_{0}^{\prime}\in Z({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})
is
\varepsilon
-close to some
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
; then for
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
we can define a map
\phi:Z({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})\rightarrow Z({\mathsf{M}})
which associates to each critical point
z_{0}^{\prime}
the (unique) critical point
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
at distance less than
\varepsilon
. We make that assumption for the reminder of the proof. We consider a fixed
z_{0}\in{\mathsf{M}}
, for which we write
\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{s}\}
for some
2\leq s\leq D+1
, and a point
z^{\prime}_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})
such that
\phi(z^{\prime}_{0})=z_{0}
.

Claim 2
.

Let
0<\delta<\varepsilon
. We can make
\mathcal{U}
small enough that each projection of
z_{0}^{\prime}
must be
\delta
-close to one of the
x_{j}
s.

Proof.

Let
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
,
z_{0}^{\prime}\in Z({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})
and
x^{\prime}\in\pi_{{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}}(z_{0}^{\prime})
. There exists a point
y\in{\mathsf{M}}
at distance less than
d_{H}({\mathsf{M}},{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})
from
x^{\prime}
. This point is such that

\displaystyle\|\phi(z_{0}^{\prime})-y\|\leq d_{H}({\mathsf{M}},{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})+\|\phi(z_{0}^{\prime})-x^{\prime}\|\leq d_{H}({\mathsf{M}},{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})+\|\phi(z_{0}^{\prime})-z_{0}^{\prime}\|+\|z_{0}^{\prime}-x^{\prime}\|

\displaystyle=d_{H}({\mathsf{M}},{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})+\|\phi(z_{0}^{\prime})-z_{0}^{\prime}\|+d_{{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}}(z_{0}^{\prime})\leq 2d_{H}({\mathsf{M}},{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})+2\|\phi(z_{0}^{\prime})-z_{0}^{\prime}\|+d_{\mathsf{M}}(\phi(z_{0}^{\prime})).

Hence,
y\in B(\phi(z_{0}^{\prime}),2d_{H}({\mathsf{M}},{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})+2\|\phi(z_{0}^{\prime})-z_{0}^{\prime}\|+d_{\mathsf{M}}(\phi(z_{0}^{\prime})))\cap{\mathsf{M}}
. The Hausdorff distance
d_{H}({\mathsf{M}},{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})
and the distance between each
z_{0}^{\prime}\in Z({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})
and the corresponding
\phi(z_{0}^{\prime})\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
can be made arbitrarily small (as shown above) by making
\mathcal{U}
small enough; hence, using
Lemma

2.1
, we see that
y
can be made as close as we want to
\pi_{{\mathsf{M}}}(\phi(z_{0}^{\prime}))
(this is true even without
{\mathsf{M}}
satisfying Conditions
(P1-4)
). Hence,
x^{\prime}
can also be made as close as we want to
\pi_{{\mathsf{M}}}(\phi(z_{0}^{\prime}))
. By applying the same reasoning to each of the finitely many critical points of
{\mathsf{M}}
(here we use
(P2)
, and implicitly
(P1)
to assert that
\pi_{{\mathsf{M}}}(\phi(z_{0}^{\prime}))
is a finite set), we prove the claim.
∎

In the remainder of the proof, we assume that
\mathcal{U}
is small enough that for any
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
, each projection of each critical point
z_{0}^{\prime}
of
{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}
is
\varepsilon
-close to some projection of
\phi(z_{0}^{\prime})
.

Claim 3
.

We can make
\mathcal{U}
small enough for there to be constants
\delta,\delta^{\prime},\alpha>0
such that the following holds for any
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
and any
z_{0}^{\prime}\in Z({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})
: the point
z^{\prime}_{0}
has at most a single projection
x^{\prime}_{j}
that is
\varepsilon
-close to any given projection
x_{j}
of
\phi(z_{0}^{\prime})
. Furthermore, the sphere
S(z_{0}^{\prime},d_{{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}}(z_{0}^{\prime}))
does not osculate
{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}
at
x^{\prime}_{j}
, and there exist local orthogonal projections
p^{\prime}_{j}:B(z^{\prime}_{0},\delta^{\prime})\to{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}_{j}=\overline{B}(x^{\prime}_{j},\delta)\cap{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}
given by
Lemma

4.8
that are
\alpha^{-1}
-Lipschitz continuous. In particular,
{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}
satisfies
(P3)
.

Proof.

This is a consequence of the
C^{2}
stability of the projection proved in Proposition
4.6
, and of the crucial hypothesis that
{\mathsf{M}}
satisfies
(P3)
.
Indeed, let
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
,
x_{0}\in\pi_{{\mathsf{M}}}(z_{0})
and
m_{0}\in M
with
i(m_{0})=x_{0}
. As the sphere
S(z_{0},d_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0}))
is non-osculating at the point
x_{j}\in\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(x_{j})
, there exist
\alpha>0
and neighborhoods
U_{M}\subset M
,
U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
and
U_{\mathrm{Emb}}\subset\mathrm{Emb}^{2}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
of
m_{0}
,
z_{0}
and
i
such that for any
i^{\prime}\in U_{\mathrm{Emb}}
, any
z^{\prime}_{0}\in U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}
has a unique projection
x^{\prime}_{j}=p^{\prime}_{j}(z^{\prime}_{0})
on
\overline{i^{\prime}(U_{M})}
, that
x^{\prime}_{j}
belongs to
i^{\prime}(U_{M})
, and that the sphere
S(z^{\prime}_{0},d_{i^{\prime}(U_{M})}(z^{\prime}_{0}))
does not osculate
i^{\prime}(U_{M})
at
x^{\prime}_{j}
with
\alpha
a degree of non-osculation.

Furthermore, if
{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}=i^{\prime}(M)
, then there exist
\delta,\delta^{\prime}>0
such that the function
p^{\prime}_{j}:B(z^{\prime}_{0},\delta^{\prime})\to{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}\cap\overline{B}(x_{j}^{\prime},\delta)={\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}_{j}
is the orthogonal projection on
{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}_{j}
, which is
C^{1}
and
\alpha^{-1}
-Lipschitz continuous.
We can assume that
\delta,\delta^{\prime}<\varepsilon
.

Let us make
\mathcal{U}
small enough that
\mathcal{U}\subset U_{\mathrm{Emb}}
and that if
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
, any critical point
z_{0}^{\prime}\in Z({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})
in
\phi^{-1}(z_{0})
belongs to
U_{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}}
and any projection
x^{\prime}\in\pi_{{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}}(z_{0}^{\prime})
of
z_{0}^{\prime}
close to
x_{j}
is at distance at most
\delta
from
x_{j}
.
Then for such an embedding
i^{\prime}
, critical point
z_{0}^{\prime}
and projection
x^{\prime}
,
we have shown that
x^{\prime}\in i^{\prime}(U_{M})
. As we know from the conclusions of Proposition
4.6
on the
C^{2}
stability of the projection that
z_{0}^{\prime}
has a unique projection on
{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}
in
i^{\prime}(U_{M})
, the projection
x^{\prime}
is necessarily the only one to be
\delta
-close to
x_{j}
(hence the only one to be
\varepsilon
-close to
x_{j}
).
We also get that the sphere
S(z_{0}^{\prime},d_{i^{\prime}(U_{M})}(z_{0}^{\prime}))=S(z_{0}^{\prime},d_{{\mathsf{M}}}(z_{0}^{\prime}))
does not osculate
i^{\prime}(U_{M})
at
x^{\prime}
.
By making
\mathcal{U}
small enough for the same reasoning to work for each of the finitely many critical points of
{\mathsf{M}}
and their finitely many projections (and by taking the minima of the constants
\delta,\delta^{\prime}
and
\alpha
corresponding to each critical point
z_{0}
and each projection
x_{j}
), we prove the claim.
∎

For the remainder of the proof, we assume that
\mathcal{U}
is small enough for the conclusions of the previous claim to hold.

Claim 4
.

We can make
\mathcal{U}
small enough that there is exactly one projection
x^{\prime}_{j}\in\pi_{{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}}(z_{0}^{\prime})
that is
\varepsilon
-close to each
x_{j}
.

Proof.

Let us consider again
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
with
\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\}
.
If
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
and
z_{0}^{\prime}\in\phi^{-1}(z_{0})
, we already know that for each
x_{j}\in\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})
there can be at most one projection
x^{\prime}_{j}\in\pi_{{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}}(z_{0}^{\prime})
that is
\varepsilon
-close to
x_{j}
, and that each projection of
z_{0}^{\prime}
must be
\varepsilon
-close to one of the
x_{j}
.

As
{\mathsf{M}}
satisfies
(P1)
, the critical point
z_{0}
must belong to the interior of the simplex with vertices
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}
. In particular, for any
J\subsetneq[s]

\frac{\|z_{0}-m(\{x_{j}:\ j\in J\})\|}{d_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})}>0.

For
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
and
z_{0}^{\prime}\in\phi^{-1}(z_{0})
, let
I(z_{0}^{\prime})\subset[s]
be the set of indices
j
such that
z_{0}^{\prime}
has a (unique) projection
x_{j}^{\prime}
that is
\varepsilon
-close to
x_{j}
.
By making
\mathcal{U}
small enough, we can ensure (by continuity) that for any
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
and any
z_{0}^{\prime}\in\phi^{-1}(z_{0})
the following holds: the point
z_{0}^{\prime}
is close enough to
z_{0}
and each of its projections
x_{j}^{\prime}
is close enough to
x_{j}
that if we let
J\subset I(z_{0}^{\prime})
with
J\subsetneq[s]
, then

\frac{\|z_{0}^{\prime}-m(\{x_{j}^{\prime}:\ j\in J\})\|}{d_{{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}}(z_{0}^{\prime})}>0.

In particular, as

\nabla d_{{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}}(z_{0}^{\prime})=\frac{z_{0}^{\prime}-m(\{x_{j}^{\prime}:\ j\in I(z_{0}^{\prime})\})}{d_{{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}}(z_{0}^{\prime})},

the point
z_{0}^{\prime}
being critical forces
I(z_{0}^{\prime})=[s]
. Hence the projections of
z_{0}^{\prime}
are in bijection with those of
z_{0}
. By applying the same reasoning to each of the finitely
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
, we prove the claim.
∎

Again, we assume
\mathcal{U}
to be small enough for its conclusions to hold for the remainder of the proof.

Claim 5
.

We can make
\mathcal{U}
small enough that
{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}
satisfies
(P1)
.

Proof.

This is a simple consequence of the previous claims. As
{\mathsf{M}}
satisfies
(P1)
, we know that for each
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
, the projections
\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})
are the vertices of a non-degenerate simplex to the relative interior of which
z_{0}
belongs.
These properties are open in the coordinates of
z_{0}
and its projections (under the constraint that
z_{0}
necessarily belongs to the simplex, which is satisfied for any critical point).
We have shown that by making
\mathcal{U}
small enough, we can make any critical point
z_{0}^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}
arbitrarily close to a corresponding critical point
\phi(z_{0}^{\prime})\in{\mathsf{M}}
and the projections of
z_{0}^{\prime}
arbitrarily close to the corresponding projections of
\phi(z_{0}^{\prime})
(with which they are in bijection) if
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
.
As there are only finitely many critical points of
{\mathsf{M}}
with finitely many projections, this is enough to conclude that any
{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}
satisfies
(P1)
for
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{U}
small enough, which we assume to be the case for the remainder of the proof.
∎

For
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
with
\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\}
, let
E=\mathrm{Vec}(\{x_{j}-x_{k}:\ j,k\in[s]\})
and
E^{\prime}=\mathrm{Vec}(\{x_{j}^{\prime}-x_{k}^{\prime}:\ j,k\in[s]\})
. For
I\subset[s]
, recall the functions
m_{I}
and
g_{I}
from
Section

6
, and define the functions
m^{\prime}_{I}(z)=m(\{p^{\prime}_{j}(z):\ j\in I\}
and
g^{\prime}_{I}(z)=\frac{\|z-m^{\prime}_{I}(z)\|}{d_{{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}}(z)}
implicitly associated to some
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
.
They are defined for
z\in B(z^{\prime}_{0},\delta^{\prime})
, where
\delta^{\prime}
is the constant of
3
. As the projections
p^{\prime}_{j}
are Lipschitz continuous for a uniform constant
\alpha^{-1}
over
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
and as the distance between
z_{0}^{\prime}
and the boundary of the simplex with vertices
\pi_{{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}}(z_{0}^{\prime})
can be uniformly controlled over
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
(as in the proof of the previous claim),
Lemma

6.1
implies that the functions
g^{\prime}_{I}
are
(1/2)
-Hölder continuous and
g^{\prime}_{[s]}
is Lipschitz continuous
on a small ball
B(z^{\prime}_{0},\delta^{\prime\prime})
, where
\delta^{\prime\prime}
, the Lipschitz constant of
g^{\prime}_{[s]}
and the Hölder constants of the
g^{\prime}_{I}
are uniform over
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
.

Remark also that the various constants in
Lemma

6.2
depend only on the reach and the diameter of
{\mathsf{M}}
, on the Lipschitz continuity of the
p_{j}
s, the Hölder constants of the
g_{I}
s and the Lipschitz constant of
g_{[s]}
, on the level of non-degeneracy of the simplex with vertices
x_{1},\dots,x_{s}
(e.g., measured through its determinant) and on the distance between
z_{0}
and the boundary of this simplex.
As the local projections
p^{\prime}_{j}
s are all
\alpha^{-1}
-Lipschitz continuous and defined on
B(z^{\prime}_{0},\delta^{\prime})
for some
\alpha
and
\delta^{\prime}
uniform over
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
, by making the non-degeneracy of the simplices and the distances of the critical points to their boundaries uniform over
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
in the conclusion of
5
, and using the remark on the Hölder constant of the previous paragraph, the constants of
Lemma

6.2
can be made uniform over
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
as well, thence the following claim holds.

Claim 6
.

We can make
\mathcal{U}
small enough that there exist constants
\delta_{1},\mu_{0},C>0
such that for all
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
and
z_{0}^{\prime}\in Z({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})
, if
z\in B(z^{\prime}_{0},\delta_{1})
is
\mu_{0}
-critical (for
{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}
), then
\|\pi_{E^{\prime}}(z-z^{\prime}_{0})\|\leq C\|z-z^{\prime}_{0}\|^{2}
.

We now turn to proving that Condition
(P4)
, which we have shown in Proposition
6.5
to be equivalent under our hypotheses to Condition
(BSP)
, is open.

Claim 7
.

We can make
\mathcal{U}
small enough that there exist constants
\delta,\delta^{\prime},L>0
such that for all
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
, the submanifold
{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}=i^{\prime}(M)
satisfies
(BSP)
with constants
\delta,\delta^{\prime},L
for any
z_{0}^{\prime}\in Z({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})
. In particular,
{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}
satisfies
(P4)
.

The uniformity of
\delta,\delta^{\prime}
and
L
over
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
is not needed for
{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}
to satisfy
(BSP)
(hence
(P4)
), but it will prove useful for the following claims.

Proof.

Using the same notations as above, for
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
, let
h^{\prime}\in E^{\prime\perp}
be small enough that
p_{j}^{\prime}(z^{\prime}_{0}+h^{\prime})
is well-defined for
j\in[s]
. Let
\Delta^{\prime}(h^{\prime})
be the simplex with vertices
z^{\prime}_{0}+h^{\prime}
and
p^{\prime}_{j}(z^{\prime}_{0}+h^{\prime})
for
j\in[s]
. Define
X^{\prime}=[x^{\prime}_{1}-z^{\prime}_{0},\dots,x^{\prime}_{s}-z^{\prime}_{0}]
,
r^{\prime}_{j}(h^{\prime})=p^{\prime}_{j}(z_{0}^{\prime}+h^{\prime})-x_{j}^{\prime}-h^{\prime}
and
R_{j}^{\prime}(h^{\prime})
the matrix obtained from
X^{\prime}
by replacing the
j
th column of
X^{\prime}
by
r^{\prime}_{j}(h^{\prime})
. According to
Lemma

6.7
, the volume of
\Delta^{\prime}(h^{\prime})
satisfies

\mathrm{Vol}_{s}(\Delta^{\prime}(h^{\prime}))^{2}\geq\frac{1}{(s!)^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{s}\det(R^{\prime}_{i}(h^{\prime})^{\top}R^{\prime}_{j}(h^{\prime}))-C_{0}\|h^{\prime}\|^{3},

where
C_{0}
depends on
\mathrm{diam}({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})
and the Lipschitz constant
\ell
of the projections
p^{\prime}_{j}
(and
s
, which is the same for any
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
and
z_{0}^{\prime}\in\phi^{-1}(z_{0})
). As those quantities are uniformly bounded over
\mathcal{U}
, the constant
C_{0}
can be chosen independent of
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
.

Consider the matrices
\tilde{R}_{j}(X^{\prime},h)
, obtained by replacing the
j
th column of
X^{\prime}
by
r_{j}(h)
(similarly defined as
r_{j}(h)=p_{j}(z_{0}+h)-x_{j}-h
). Let
\delta_{0}>0
be such that the
p_{j}
s are defined and
\ell
-Lipschitz continuous on
B(z_{0},2\delta_{0})
. For
\|h\|\leq\delta_{0}
, define

F(X^{\prime},h)=\sum_{i,j=1}^{s}\frac{1}{\|h\|^{2}}\det(\tilde{R}_{j}(X^{\prime},h)^{\top}\tilde{R}_{i}(X^{\prime},h))

and
F(X^{\prime})=\inf_{h\in E^{\perp},\|h\|\leq\delta_{0}}F(X^{\prime},h)
. As
\|r_{j}(h)\|/\|h\|
is bounded by
\ell+1
for
h\in E^{\perp}
with
\|h\|\leq\delta_{0}
, the family of functions
X^{\prime}\mapsto F(X^{\prime},h)
indexed by
h\in E^{\perp}
with
\|h\|\leq\delta_{0}
is equicontinuous. This implies in particular that the function
X^{\prime}\mapsto F(X^{\prime})
is continuous. Let
X=[x_{1}-z_{0},\dots,x_{s}-z_{0}]
. As the submanifold
{\mathsf{M}}
satisfies Condition
(P4)
(which is equivalent to Condition
(BSP)
),
Lemma

6.7
ensures that
F(X)>C_{1}
for
\delta_{0}
small enough and for some constant
C_{1}>0
. By continuity, as long as
z^{\prime}_{0}
and the projections
x^{\prime}_{j}
are close enough to
z_{0}
and to the projections
x_{j}
respectively, then

\sum_{i,j=1}^{s}\det(\tilde{R}_{j}(X^{\prime},h)^{\top}\tilde{R}_{i}(X^{\prime},h))\geq C_{1}\|h\|^{2}

for
h\in E^{\perp}
with
\|h\|\leq\delta_{0}
.

Let
h^{\prime}\in E^{\prime\perp}
and let
h=\pi_{E^{\perp}}(h^{\prime})
.
For any
\beta>0
, we can ensure that
\|h^{\prime}-h\|\leq\beta\|h^{\prime}\|
for any such
h^{\prime}
by making the
x^{\prime}_{j}
s close enough to the
x_{j}
s. Then

\displaystyle\|r_{j}(h)-r^{\prime}_{j}(h^{\prime})\|

\displaystyle\leq\beta\|h^{\prime}\|+\|p^{\prime}_{j}(z^{\prime}_{0}+h^{\prime})-x^{\prime}_{j}-(p_{j}(z_{0}+h)-x_{j})\|

\displaystyle\leq\beta\|h^{\prime}\|+\|\int_{0}^{1}(d(p^{\prime}_{j})_{z^{\prime}_{0}+th^{\prime}}(h^{\prime})-d(p_{j})_{z_{0}+th}(h))\mathrm{d}t\|

Note that
p^{\prime}_{j}(z^{\prime}_{0}+th^{\prime})
and
p_{j}(z_{0}+th)
belong respectively to
i^{\prime}(U_{M})
and
i(U_{M})
for some open set
U_{M}
of
M
that can be made arbitrarily small (by making
{\mathcal{U}}
smaller).
We know from
Proposition

4.2
that the differential
d(p_{j})_{z}
of the projection can be expressed in terms of the orthogonal projection onto
T_{p_{j}(z)}{\mathsf{M}}
and of the shape operator of
{\mathsf{M}}
at
p_{j}(z)
.
As a result, for any constant
\gamma>0
, if
\|i^{\prime}-i\|_{C^{2}}
,
\delta^{\prime}>0
and the set
U_{M}
are small enough, then for any
z,z^{\prime}\in B(z_{0},\delta^{\prime})
the projections
p^{\prime}_{j}(z^{\prime})
and
p_{j}(z)
are the images by
i
and
i^{\prime}
of points
m,m^{\prime}\in U_{M}
that are sufficiently close for us to have
\left\|d(p^{\prime}_{j})_{z^{\prime}}-d(p_{j})_{z}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\gamma
.
Assuming that this is the case,

\displaystyle\|r_{j}(h)-r^{\prime}_{j}(h^{\prime})\|

\displaystyle\leq\beta\|h^{\prime}\|+\|\int_{0}^{1}d(p_{j})_{z_{0}+th}(h-h^{\prime})\mathrm{d}t\|+\|\int_{0}^{1}(d(p^{\prime}_{j})_{z^{\prime}_{0}+th^{\prime}}-d(p_{j})_{z_{0}+th})(h^{\prime})\mathrm{d}t\|

\displaystyle\leq\beta\|h^{\prime}\|+\ell\beta\|h^{\prime}\|+\gamma\|h^{\prime}\|=(\beta(\ell+1)+\gamma)\|h^{\prime}\|.

As the determinant is locally Lipschitz continuous, it holds that for some constant
C_{2}>0
and under the same hypotheses

\displaystyle|\det(R_{j}^{\prime}(h^{\prime})^{\top}R_{i}^{\prime}(h^{\prime}))

\displaystyle-\det(\tilde{R}_{j}(X^{\prime},h)^{\top}\tilde{R}_{i}(X^{\prime},h))|

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}(\|r^{\prime}_{j}(h^{\prime})-r_{j}(h)\|\|r_{i}(h)\|+\|r^{\prime}_{i}(h^{\prime})-r_{i}(h)\|\|r^{\prime}_{j}(h^{\prime})\|)

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{2}\ell(\beta(\ell+1)+\gamma)\|h^{\prime}\|^{2}.

Hence for any
h^{\prime}\in E^{\prime\perp}
with
\|h^{\prime}\|\leq\delta_{0}
and any
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Vol}_{s}(\Delta^{\prime}(h^{\prime}))^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{C_{1}}{(s!)^{2}}\|h\|^{2}-\frac{1}{(s!)^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{s}2C_{2}\ell(\beta(\ell+1)+\gamma)\|h^{\prime}\|^{2}-C_{0}\|h^{\prime}\|^{3}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{C_{1}}{2(s!)^{2}}\|h^{\prime}\|^{2}

if we choose
\mathcal{U}
small enough so that
\beta
and
\gamma
are small enough. We can then make
\mathcal{U}
even smaller so that the above considerations hold simultaneously for all the finitely many critical points
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
.
∎

Claim 8
.

We can make
\mathcal{U}
small enough that the associated map
\phi:Z({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})\rightarrow Z({\mathsf{M}})
is injective. In particular,
{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}
satisfies
(P2)
.

Proof.

Interestingly, the proof of this claim once again makes use of Condition
(P4)
(see also Example
8.2
regarding the importance of the second part of Condition
(P1)
).
Let
z
and
z^{\prime}
be critical points in
Z({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})
with
\phi(z)=\phi(z^{\prime})=z_{0}
. Recall from
1
that we can make
\|z-z_{0}\|
and
\|z^{\prime}-z_{0}\|
arbitrarily small if needed. Write
\pi_{{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}}(z^{\prime})=\{x^{\prime}_{1},\dots,x^{\prime}_{s}\}
and let
E^{\prime}=\mathrm{Vec}(\{x^{\prime}_{j}-x^{\prime}_{k}:\ j,k\in[s]\})
. According to
6
, whenever
\|z-z^{\prime}\|\leq\|z-z_{0}\|+\|z^{\prime}-z_{0}\|<\delta_{1}
, as
z
is a critical point of
{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}
, we have

\|\pi_{E^{\prime}}(z-z^{\prime})\|\leq C\|z-z^{\prime}\|^{2}.

Let
p^{\prime}_{j}
be the local projections defined on
B(z^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
, for some
\delta^{\prime}
given by
3
. Note that
z\in B(z^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
when
\|z-z^{\prime}\|
is made small enough by taking
\mathcal{U}
small enough. In particular, we have
\pi_{{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}}(z)=\{p_{1}^{\prime}(z),\dots,p_{s}^{\prime}(z)\}
thanks to Claims
3
and
4
.
Let
h=\pi_{E^{\prime\perp}}(z-z^{\prime})
and let
\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime}(h)
be the simplex with vertices
p_{1}^{\prime}(z^{\prime}+h),\dots,p_{s}^{\prime}(z^{\prime}+h)
.
Let
\Delta^{\prime}(h)
be the simplex obtained by adding
z^{\prime}+h
to the vertices of
\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime}(h)
.
As
{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}
satisfies
(BSP)
, it also holds that if
\|z-z^{\prime}\|
is small enough, then

\mathrm{Vol}_{s}(\Delta^{\prime}(h))\geq L\|h\|,

where we use the uniformity with respect to
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
of the constants from
7
.
Let
m^{\prime}(h)
be the center of the smallest enclosing ball of the vertices
p_{1}^{\prime}(z^{\prime}+h),\dots,p_{s}^{\prime}(z^{\prime}+h)
. Remark that, as
g^{\prime}_{[s]}(z)=0
and
g^{\prime}_{[s]}
is
\ell
-Lipschitz continuous for a constant
\ell
that is uniform over all
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Vol}_{s}(\Delta^{\prime}(h))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{s}\mathrm{Vol}_{s-1}(\tilde{\Delta}^{\prime}(h))\|z^{\prime}+h-m^{\prime}(h)\|\leq\frac{1}{s}(\mathrm{diam}({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}))^{s-1}d_{{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}}(z^{\prime}+h)g^{\prime}_{[s]}(z^{\prime}+h)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{s}(\mathrm{diam}({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}))^{s}(g^{\prime}_{[s]}(z^{\prime}+h)-g^{\prime}_{[s]}(z))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{s}(\mathrm{diam}({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}))^{s}\ell\|\pi_{E^{\prime}}(z-z^{\prime})\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{s}(\mathrm{diam}({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}))^{s}\ell C\|z-z^{\prime}\|^{2}.

As
\|h\|\geq\|z-z^{\prime}\|/2
as long as
\|z-z^{\prime}\|
is small enough, we obtain that

\frac{L}{2}\|z-z^{\prime}\|\leq\frac{2}{s}(\mathrm{diam}({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}))^{s}\ell C\|z-z^{\prime}\|^{2}.

When
\|z-z^{\prime}\|
is small enough, this is only possible when
z=z^{\prime}
. We make
\mathcal{U}
small enough so that this argument applies around all critical points
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
to conclude regarding the injectivity of
\phi
.
∎

The last point of the proof of
1.4
is that the map
\phi:Z({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})\to Z({\mathsf{M}})
can also be made surjective. The map
\Psi
from the statement of the theorem is then defined as
\phi^{-1}
.

Claim 9
.

We can make
\mathcal{U}
small enough that for any
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
, the associated map
\phi:Z({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})\rightarrow Z({\mathsf{M}})
is surjective.

Proof.

Let
t>0
be a critical value for the distance function
d_{M}
, and let
z_{1},\ldots,z_{l}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
be all the critical points with associated critical value
t
.
Then the Handle Attachment Lemma from Theorem
1.8
states that for
\eta>0
small enough, the set
d_{\mathsf{M}}^{-1}(-\infty,t+\eta]
has the homotopy type of
d_{\mathsf{M}}^{-1}(-\infty,t-\eta]
with cells (i.e. closed balls)
e_{1,d_{1}},\ldots,e_{l,d_{l}}
attached along their boundary, where
d_{j}\in\{1,\ldots,D\}
is the dimension of
e_{j,d_{j}}
. Conversely, the Isotopy Lemma from the same theorem ensures that there is no change in homotopy type between

d_{\mathsf{M}}^{-1}(-\infty,a]
and
d_{\mathsf{M}}^{-1}(-\infty,b]
if
d_{\mathsf{M}}
has no critical value in the interval
[a,b]
.

Consider now the persistence diagram
\mathrm{dgm}({\mathsf{M}})
of the sublevel filtration of
d_{\mathsf{M}}

[
EH22
]
.
As for smooth Morse functions, the critical points of
d_{\mathsf{M}}
are precisely in bijection with the non-zero coordinates of the points in the persistence diagram; though the arguments are similar, we provide a proof for completeness.
First, the Isotopy Lemma ensures that to each non-zero coordinate (either birth or death) of a point in the persistence diagram
\mathrm{dgm}({\mathsf{M}})
corresponds one critical value of
d_{\mathsf{M}}
.
Now, let
t
be as above some critical value with associated critical points
z_{1},\ldots,z_{l}
and let
\eta>0
be small enough that
d_{\mathsf{M}}^{-1}(-\infty,t+\eta]
has the homotopy type of

d_{\mathsf{M}}^{-1}(-\infty,t-\eta]\cup\left(e_{1,d_{1}}\cup\ldots\cup e_{l,d_{l}}\right),

where the cells
e_{1,d_{1}},\ldots,e_{l,d_{l}}
are attached along their boundaries (and the homotopy equivalence is compatible with the inclusion of
d_{\mathsf{M}}^{-1}(-\infty,t-\eta]
).

We would like to compute the Mayer-Vietoris sequence of

d_{\mathsf{M}}^{-1}(-\infty,t-\eta]
and
e_{1,d_{1}}\cup\ldots\cup e_{l,d_{l}}
, but their interiors do not cover
d_{\mathsf{M}}^{-1}(-\infty,t-\eta]\cup\left(e_{1,d_{1}}\cup\ldots\cup e_{l,d_{l}}\right)
; we simply replace them by homotopically equivalent sets.
Let
A
be an open “thickening” of
d_{\mathsf{M}}^{-1}(-\infty,t-\eta]
in
d_{\mathsf{M}}^{-1}(-\infty,t-\eta]\cup\left(e_{1,d_{1}}\cup\ldots\cup e_{l,d_{l}}\right)
, i.e. the union of
d_{\mathsf{M}}^{-1}(-\infty,t-\eta]
with a small open neighborhood of the union of the boundaries
\partial e_{j,d_{j}}
of the cells
e_{j,d_{j}}
(so that
d_{\mathsf{M}}^{-1}(-\infty,t-\eta]\cup\left(e_{1,d_{1}}\cup\ldots\cup e_{l,d_{l}}\right)\backslash A
is the union of
l
closed disks of dimension
d_{1},\ldots,d_{l}
respectively).
Similarly, let
B
be the union of
l
open disks
D_{1},\ldots,D_{l}
of dimension
d_{1},\ldots,d_{l}
respectively with
D_{i}\subset e_{i,l_{i}}
, such that
A\cup B=d_{\mathsf{M}}^{-1}(-\infty,t-\eta]\cup\left(e_{1,d_{1}}\cup\ldots\cup e_{l,d_{l}}\right)
, and
A\cap B
is homeomorphic to
\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{l}\partial e_{j,d_{j}}=\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{l}S^{d_{j}-1}
, where
S^{d}
is the
d
-dimensional sphere.

We can now consider the Mayer–Vietoris exact sequence with coefficients
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
associated to the pair
\left(A,B\right)
.
As
A\cap B
is homotopically equivalent to
\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{l}\partial e_{j,d_{j}}=\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{l}S^{d_{j}-1}
, the homology group
H_{k}(A\cap B)
is isomorphic to
({\mathbb{Z}}_{2})^{n_{k+1}}
for
k\geq 1
, where
n_{k}
is the number of
k
-dimensional cells among
e_{1,d_{1}},\ldots,e_{l,d_{l}}
.
Furthermore, we have
H_{k}(B)=H_{k}(e_{1,d_{1}}\cup\ldots\cup e_{l,d_{l}})=0
for
k\geq 1
.

Around
k\geq 1
, the Mayer-Vietoris sequence is isomorphic to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{k+1}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{k+1}(A\cup B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{n_{k+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{k}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{k}(A\cup B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{k+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{k}}

Let us identify
H_{i}(A)\simeq H_{i}(d_{\mathsf{M}}^{-1}(-\infty,t-\eta])
and
H_{i}(A\cup B)\simeq H_{i}(d_{\mathsf{M}}^{-1}(-\infty,t+\eta])
; the dimension
D_{k+1,b}
of the cokernel of
H_{k+1}(d_{\mathsf{M}}^{-1}(-\infty,t-\eta])\xrightarrow{i_{k+1}}H_{k+1}(d_{\mathsf{M}}^{-1}(-\infty,t+\eta])
is precisely the number of births of intervals between
t-\eta
and
t+\eta
(hence precisely at
t
) in the persistence module of the filtration.
Similarly, the dimension
D_{k,d}
of the kernel of
H_{k}(d_{\mathsf{M}}^{-1}(-\infty,t-\eta])\xrightarrow{i_{k}}H_{k}(d_{\mathsf{M}}^{-1}(-\infty,t+\eta])
is the number of deaths of intervals at
t
in the persistence module of the filtration.
Using the exactness of the sequence, one finds that
n_{k+1}=D_{k+1,b}+D_{k,d}
, meaning that each
(k+1)
-cell among
e_{1,d_{1}},\ldots,e_{l,d_{l}}
corresponds exactly either to the birth of an interval for the homology of degree
k+1
, or to the death of an interval for the homology of degree
k
(in particular, a
k
-cell and a
(k+1)
-cell cannot “cancel each other out”). An almost identical reasoning applies for
k=0
.
Hence we have shown that the non-zero birth and death values of the intervals of the filtration are in bijection with the critical points of
d_{\mathsf{M}}
.

Now let
\rho>0
and consider an embedding
i^{\prime}
close enough to
i
that all the previous claims stand and such that
{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}=i(M)
is
\rho
-close to
{\mathsf{M}}
for the Hausdorff distance. This means that
\|d_{{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}}-d_{{\mathsf{M}}}\|_{\infty}\leq\rho
, and the bottleneck stability theorem
[
CSEH05
]
ensures that the two persistence diagrams
\mathrm{dgm}({\mathsf{M}})
and
\mathrm{dgm}({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})
associated to the sublevel filtrations of
d_{{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}}
and
d_{{\mathsf{M}}}
are
\rho
-close (for the bottleneck distance). When
\rho
is small enough, this implies that the points of
\mathrm{dgm}({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})
have at least as many non-zero coordinates (counted with multiplicity if several points share a coordinate) as those of
\mathrm{dgm}({\mathsf{M}})
, hence that
d_{{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}}
has at least as many critical points as
d_{{\mathsf{M}}}
.
As
\phi:Z({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})\to Z({\mathsf{M}})
is injective, it must then be bijective, and in particular surjective. This proves the claim, and thence the theorem.
∎

9
Density of Conditions
(P1-4)

Let as usual
M
be a compact abstract manifold of regularity
C^{k}
with
k\geq 2
.
In this section, we show that the set of
C^{k}
embeddings
i:M\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
such that
i(M)
satisfies geometric properties
(P1-4)
is dense in
\text{Emb}^{k}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
for the Whitney
C^{k}
-topology, which completes the proof of the Genericity Theorem
1.1
.

It would not be too difficult to show that these geometric properties are “locally generic” using elementary methods. However, the difficulty resides in controlling
i(M)
globally to make sure that situations contradicting one of the properties
(P1-4)
do not emerge anywhere.

To do so, we make repeated use of one of the many variants of R. Thom’s transversality theorem (see e.g.
[
GG74
]
for more on this theorem and its applications), following the examples of Yomdin
[
Yom81
]
, Mather
[
Mat83
]
or Damon and Gasparovic
[
DG17
]
(among others) who applied similar methods.

Remember that if
X,Y
and
Z\subset Y
are manifolds of regularity at least
C^{1}
, a mapping
\phi:X\rightarrow Y
is
transverse
to
Z
if for each
x\in\phi^{-1}(Z)
we have
\mathrm{Im}(d\phi_{x})+T_{\phi(x)}Z=T_{\phi(x)}Y
.
Informally, most versions of Thom’s theorem state that for any such
X,Y,Z
, the set of mappings
\phi:X\rightarrow Y
such that
\phi
is transverse to
Z
is dense (or sometimes residual, or even open and dense) in the set of such mappings; in other words, “most” mappings are transverse to
Z
. The exact statement depends on the exact set of mappings considered, the topology with which we equip it, and whether or not we consider
(multi)jets
, of which we now give a terse definition.

9.1
A brief introduction to jets and multijets

Jets and multijets are useful tools that help us study and control the values and derivatives of maps between manifolds; these, in turn, determine the geometric properties that are of interest to us when considering the special case of embeddings
i:M\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
. As we make extensive use of multijets in the next subsections, we choose to succinctly introduce them here for completeness; the reader already familiar with the definitions can safely skip this subsection. Our presentation mostly follows
[
GG74
]
(see also
[
KMS13
]
for more details).

Let
X
and
Y
be
C^{k}
manifolds (in the cases of interest to us,
X
will be our compact manifold
M
and
Y
will be the euclidean space
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
), and let
x\in X
and
y\in Y
.
For
0\leq r\leq k
, let us consider the set of mappings
C^{r}(X,Y)_{x,y}:=\{f\in C^{r}(X,Y):\;f(x)=y\}
and let us define the following relation of equivalence on
C^{r}(X,Y)_{x,y}
:

Let
U
be a neighborhood of
x
in
X
and
V
be a neighborhood of
y
in
Y
, and let
\phi:U\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{\dim X},x\mapsto 0
and
\psi:V\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{\dim Y},y\mapsto 0
be charts of
X
and
Y
respectively. Then we say that
f,g\in C^{r}(X,Y)_{x,y}
have
r
-th order contact at
x
, which we write as “
f\sim_{r}g
at
x
”, if the differentials
d^{l}(\psi\circ f\circ\phi^{-1})_{0}
and
d^{l}(\psi\circ g\circ\phi^{-1})_{0}
in
0
of order
l=0,\ldots,r
coincide.

It is easy to see that if the property is verified for a choice of charts
\phi,\psi
around
x
and
y
, then it is verified for any such pair of charts; hence, the definition is coherent. Note that the equivalence class of
f\in C^{r}(X,Y)_{x,y}
depends only on the germ of
f
at
x
.
The
r
-jets

J^{r}(X,Y)_{x,y}
from
x
to
y
are defined as the set
C^{r}(X,Y)_{x,y}
quotiented by the equivalence relation “
\sim_{r}
at
x
”, and the
r
-jets
from
X
to
Y
are defined as

J^{r}(X,Y):=\bigcup_{x\in X,y\in Y}J^{r}(X,Y)_{x,y}.

We call
x
the
source
and
y
the
target
of an
r
-jet of
J^{r}(X,Y)_{x,y}
.
Out of convenience, given an
r
-jet
a\in J^{r}(X,Y)
, we explicitly specify its source
x
and target
y
by writing it as

a=(x,y,[f]),

where
f\in C^{r}(X,Y)_{x,y}
is any representative of the equivalence class
a
.

Let
A_{n}^{r}
be the vector space of polynomials in
n
variables of degree less than or equal to
r
with
0
as their constant term, and let
B^{r}_{n,m}:=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}A_{n}^{r}
. It is a real vector space of dimension
m\dim_{\mathbb{R}}A_{n}^{r}=m\left(\binom{n+r}{n}-1\right)
.
Given an open set
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and a
C^{r}
map
g:U\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
, define
T^{r}g:U\rightarrow A_{n}^{r}
as the map that associates to
x\in U
the terms of degree
1
to
r
of the Taylor series of
g
in
x
.
Now let
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be an open set, and consider a
C^{r}
map
f=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}):U\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
. For any
x\in U
, the polynomials
T^{r}(f)(x):=(T^{r}f_{1}(x),\ldots,T^{r}f_{m}(x))\in B^{r}_{n,m}
fully describe the first
r
derivatives
d^{r}f_{x}
of
f
in
x
(excluding the “
0
-th derivative”
f(x)
). Conversely, for any
P\in B^{r}_{n,m}
and any
x\in U
, there exists a
C^{r}
map
g:U\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
such that
T^{r}(g)(x)=P
. In other words, there is a canonical bijection between
B^{r}_{n,m}
and the possible values of the first
r
derivatives of a
C^{r}
map
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\supset U\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
at any point
x\in U
.

Given as before two
C^{k}
manifolds
X
and
Y
, this means that the space of
r
-jets
J^{r}(X,Y)
(for
0\leq r\leq k
) can naturally be seen as a
C^{k-r}
manifold of dimension
\dim X+\dim Y+\dim B^{r}_{\dim X,\dim Y}=\dim X+\dim Y\binom{\dim X+r}{\dim X}
. Indeed, a pair of charts
\phi:U\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{\dim X}
and
\psi:V\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{\dim Y}
of
X
and
Y
induces a map

\begin{split}\xi_{\phi,\psi}:\;\{(x,y,[f])\in J^{r}(X,Y):\;x\in U,y\in V\}&\longrightarrow\phi(U)\times\psi(V)\times B^{r}_{\dim X,\dim Y}\\
a=(x,y,[f])&\longmapsto(\phi(x),\psi(y),T^{r}(\psi\circ f\circ\phi^{-1})(\phi(x))).\end{split}

In other words, given the
r
-jet
a
, we simply consider the first
r
derivatives in the source
x
of
a
of one of its representatives
f
expressed in the system of coordinates induced by the charts
\phi
and
\psi
. It is easy to show that the family of such maps
\{\xi_{\phi,\psi}\}
to which an atlas
\{\phi\}
of
X
and an atlas
\{\psi\}
of
Y
give rise does yield an atlas of
J^{r}(X,Y)
