. In what follows, we always consider
J^{r}(X,Y)
with this manifold structure and with the associated topology. We will call charts of
J^{r}(X,Y)
induced in such a way by charts of
X
and
Y

adapted charts
(this is not standard terminology).

Any map
f\in C^{k}(X,Y)
induces a canonically defined
C^{k-r}
map

\begin{split}j^{r}(f):\;X&\rightarrow J^{r}(X,Y)\\
x&\longmapsto(x,f(x),[f]).\end{split}

called the
r
-jet of
f
, which essentially encodes the first
r
-derivatives of
f
in all
x\in X
.
It can be shown that the sets

\{f\in C^{k}(X,Y):\;j^{r}(f)(X)\subset U\},

where
U
is any open set of
J^{r}(X,Y)
, form a basis for the Whitney
C^{r}
-topology (again, see
[
GG74
]
for details) which we introduced in the special case where
X
is a compact manifold and
Y={\mathbb{R}}^{D}
in Section
2
; in fact, this is how the topology is often defined.

The space of jets and the
r
-jet
j^{r}(f)
associated to a mapping
f\in C^{k}(X,Y)
can help us monitor the
r
first derivatives of
f
in each
x\in X
separately, but the geometric properties that are of interest to us require that we control the image and derivatives of a mapping in several points simultaneously and with respect to each other; we want embeddings
i:M\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
for which “there is no
m_{1},\ldots,m_{s}\in M
such that
i(m_{1}),\ldots,i(m_{s})
and the (higher) derivatives of
i
in
m_{1},\ldots,m_{s}
are in a certain configuration”. To that end, we need to consider
multijets
, defined as follows :

Consider the generalized diagonal

\Delta^{(s)}=:\{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})\in X^{s}:\;\text{there exist }1\leq i<j\leq s\text{ s.t. }x_{i}=x_{j}\}

and define
X^{(s)}:=X^{s}\backslash\Delta^{(s)}
, the product
s
times of
X
without repetitions. It is an open subset of
X^{s}
. Let
\alpha:J^{r}(X,Y)\rightarrow X
be the source map, i.e.
\alpha
maps
(x,y,[f])
to its source
x
. One can consider the product map
\alpha^{s}=\alpha\times\ldots\times\alpha:J^{r}(X,Y)^{s}\rightarrow X^{s}
.
Then

J_{s}^{r}(X,Y):=(\alpha^{s})^{-1}\left(X^{(s)}\right)

is the
s
-fold
r
-jet bundle
from
X
to
Y
; in other words, the space of
s
-tuples of
r
-jets with pairwise distinct sources. A
multijet bundle
is such a space for any
s\geq 2
.
As
\alpha^{s}
is continuous, the set of multijets
J_{s}^{r}(X,Y)
is an open set of
J^{r}(X,Y)^{s}
and inherits a
C^{k-r}
manifold structure as a result (where
X
and
Y
are as above
C^{k}
manifolds for some
k\geq r
). An obvious but important property of
J_{s}^{r}(X,Y)
is that its set of source points
X^{(s)}
is not compact, even if
X
is; this will be the cause of some distress later on.
As for
J^{r}(X,Y)
, we call charts of
J_{s}^{r}(X,Y)
induced by charts of
X
and
Y

adapted charts
.

In line with our previous notations, we usually write an
s
-fold
r
-jet
a\in J_{s}^{r}(X,Y)
as

a=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},y_{1},\ldots,y_{s},[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{s}]),

where
[f_{i}]
is a class of equivalence for the relation “
\sim_{r}
at
x_{i}
”.
Just as before, a map
f\in C^{k}(X,Y)
induces a multijet
C^{k-r}
map

\begin{split}j^{r}_{s}(f):\;&X^{(s)}\longrightarrow J_{s}^{r}(X,Y)\\
&(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})\longmapsto(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},f(x_{1}),\ldots,f(x_{s}),[f],\ldots,[f])\end{split}

(which is simply the map
j^{r}(f)\times\ldots\times j^{r}(f):X^{(s)}\rightarrow J_{s}^{r}(X,Y)
up to our choice of notations).

9.2
Two transversality theorems

We are now ready to quote the two statements that will be our main tools in the remainder of the section. The first one is a particularly complete variant of Mather’s adaptation of Thom’s transversality theorem to multijets (in
[
Mat70
]
), which we get from Damon’s work. Before that, a few definitions are required.

Remember that a closed subset
F
of a manifold
X
is
Whitney stratified
if it is a locally finite disjoint union of smooth submanifolds
F_{\alpha}
called
strata
, such that if
F_{\alpha}\cap\overline{F_{\beta}}\neq\emptyset
(where
\overline{F_{\beta}}
is the closure of
F_{\beta}
in
X
), then
F_{\alpha}\subset\overline{F_{\beta}}
, and such that each pair of strata satisfies two technical conditions on their tangent spaces and secant lines, called
Whitney’s conditions a) and b)
.
As such a stratification is usually not unique, one often says that
F
is
Whitney stratifiable
to say that it admits at least one such stratification. The dimension of a Whitney stratified set is the maximum of the dimensions of its strata (it can be shown that it does not depend on the choice of stratification).

If
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a map between two
C^{k}
manifolds and
F\subset X
,
G\subset Y
are closed Whitney stratified sets with strata
\{F_{\alpha}\}
and
\{G_{\beta}\}
, we say that
f
is
transverse to
G
on
F
if for each pair of strata
F_{\alpha}
and
G_{\beta}
, the map
f|_{F_{\alpha}}:F_{\alpha}\rightarrow Y
is transverse to
G_{\beta}
in the usual sense.

In what follows, we (and the reader) will be shielded from the full complexity of Whitney stratifications; we only need to know that semialgebraic subsets of the Euclidean space (whose definition we give later in this paper) are Whitney stratifiable, as was shown by Thom in
[
Tho66
]
. To learn more about Whitney stratifications, read e.g.,
[
Mat76
]
or
[
Gib76
]
.

Finally, remember that a subset of a topological space
B
is
residual
(in
B
) if it is a countable intersection of subsets of
B
whose interiors are dense in
B
. If
B
is a
Baire space
, then its residual subsets are dense.

The theorem below is a special case of Corollary 1.11 from
[
Dam97
]
.

Theorem 9.1

(Damon)
.

Let
s\geq 2
and
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
and let
X,Y
be
C^{k}
manifolds for
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq r+1}\cup\{\infty\}
. Assume that
X
is compact, and let
A\subset X^{(s)}
and
W\subset J_{s}^{r}(X,Y)
be closed Whitney stratified sets in their respective ambient space.
Then

F_{W,A}:=\{f\in C^{k}(X,Y):\;j^{r}_{s}(f)\text{ is transverse to }W\text{ on }A\text{ in }J_{s}^{r}(X,Y)\}

is a residual subset of
C^{k}(X,Y)
for the Whitney
C^{k}
-topology. Furthermore, if
A
is compact, then
F_{W,A}
is also open in the Whitney
C^{r+1}
-topology.

Remark 9.2
.

Note that being dense in the Whitney
C^{k}
-topology implies being dense in the Whitney
C^{l}
-topology for all
l\leq k
, and being open in the Whitney
C^{r+1}
-topology implies being open in the Whitney
C^{l}
-topology for all
r+1\leq l\leq k
.

Proof.

This statement is an altered, simplified and less general version of
[
Dam97
, Corollary 1.11]
for increased readability. Nonetheless, should a distrustful reader want to precisely compare the two to check that
9.1
is indeed a direct consequence of Damon’s theorem, let us give a few remarks (using his notations).

Firstly, the distinction that he makes between the weak
C^{r+1}
(which he calls “regular”) and the Whitney
C^{r+1}
topology is moot as they coincide in our case (because we assume
X
to be compact). We also let the set
\mathscr{H}
from Damon’s statement be the set of all mappings
C^{k}(X,Y)
(and the map
\Phi
is simply the identity). As observed in the
Note
below Damon’s definition 1.8 in the same article, it is a standard fact that
C^{k}(X,Y)
satisfies what he calls “having smooth image in
J_{s}^{r}(X,Y)
off
\Delta^{(s)}
”; furthermore (still using his notations),
\prescript{}{s}{\mathscr{H}}^{(r)}=\prescript{}{s}{C^{k}(X,Y)}^{(r)}
is simply equal to
\{j^{r}_{s}(f)(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}):\;f\in C^{k}(X,Y),(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})\in X^{(s)}\}=J_{s}^{r}(X,Y)
, hence the condition that “
\Phi
be transverse to
W
in
J_{s}^{r}(X,Y)
” is trivially satisfied.

Another difference is that Damon’s theorem is for
C^{\infty}
maps from
X
to
Y
, but as he points out in the remark before his Definition 1.1, the same statement works for
C^{k}
maps for
k\geq r+1
.

Finally, Damon’s original result only states that
F_{W,A}
is dense in
C^{\infty}
for the Whitney
C^{r+1}
-topology (rather than for the Whitney
C^{k}
-topology). However, the proof of his theorem shows that we also have
F_{W,A}
dense in the Whitney
C^{m}
-topology for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
r+1\leq m\leq k
. This is our statement if
k
is finite, and if
k=\infty
, the set
F_{W,A}
being dense in the Whitney
C^{m}
-topology for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
implies that
F_{W,A}
is dense in the Whitney
C^{\infty}
-topology by its definition.
∎

We also need another transversality statement that specifically deals with the case of distance functions in Euclidean spaces. For
M
a manifold and
f\in C^{k}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
, let us define

\begin{split}\rho_{f}:M\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}&\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}\\
(m,z)&\longmapsto\|f(m)-z\|^{2}.\end{split}

If we see
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
as a parameter and write
\rho_{f,z}:=\rho_{f}(-,z)\in C^{k}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
, we can consider the multijet map of
\rho_{f}
with respect to
m\in M
:

\begin{split}(j^{r}_{s})_{M}\rho_{f}:M^{(s)}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}&\longrightarrow J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}})\\
(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},z)&\longmapsto(j^{r}(\rho_{f,z})(m_{1}),\dots,j^{r}(\rho_{f,z})(m_{s})).\end{split}

In what follows, we will write an
s
-fold
r
-jet
a\in J^{r}_{s}(X,Y)
in the special case where
X=M
is a compact manifold and
Y={\mathbb{R}}
as

a=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},r_{1},\ldots,r_{s},[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{s}]),

with
m_{i}\in M
and
r_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
We will say that a subset
F\subset J_{s}^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
is invariant under addition if it is invariant under the action of
{\mathbb{R}}
defined as

t+(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},r_{1},\ldots,r_{s},[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{s}]):=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},r_{1}+t,\ldots,r_{s}+t,[f_{1}+t],\ldots,[f_{s}+t])

for any
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Then the following statement is true, which is a variant of a theorem by Looijenga
[
Loo74
]
; this version in particular comes (modulo some alterations and simplifications) from Damon’s and Gasparovic’s
[
DG17
]
.

Theorem 9.3

(Damon, Gasparovic)
.

Let
s\geq 2
and
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
and let
M
be a compact
C^{k}
manifold for
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq r+1}\cup\{\infty\}
. Let
A\subset M^{(s)}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
and
W\subset J_{s}^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
be closed Whitney stratified sets in their respective ambient space. Furthermore, assume that each stratum of
W
is invariant under addition.
Then

D_{W,A}:=\{f\in C^{k}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D}):\;(j^{r}_{s})_{M}\rho_{f}\text{ is transverse to }W\text{ on }A\text{ in }J_{s}^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}})\}

is a residual subset of
C^{k}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
for the Whitney
C^{k}
-topology. Furthermore, if
A
is compact, then
D_{W,A}
is also open in the Whitney
C^{r+1}
-topology.

Proof.

As for Theorem
9.1
, we briefly explain how the statement is a direct adaptation of Theorem 16.5 in
[
DG17
]
.

The remarks we made in the proof of
9.1
regarding the weak and Whitney
C^{r+1}
topologies, the compacity of
M
, the topologies in which
F_{W,A}
(here
D_{W,A}
) is dense and the distinctions between
C^{\infty}
and
C^{k}
maps remain valid.

Two new elements are to be commented. First, we let
E=\emptyset
and
Y=M^{(s)}
in Damon and Gasparovic’s statement. Secondly, similarly to the “proof” of Theorem
9.1
, we let
\mathscr{H}
be
C^{k}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
. Then it remains to show that, in the words of Damon and Gasparovic, the map

\begin{split}\rho:C^{k}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})&\longrightarrow C^{k}(M\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D},{\mathbb{R}}^{D})\\
f&\longmapsto\rho_{f}:(m,z)\mapsto\|f(m)-z\|^{2}\end{split}

(which corresponds to
\Psi
in their statement) is “transverse to
W
” (on
X
) (this is defined in Definition 16.4 of
[
DG17
]
) for any closed Whitney stratified set
W\subset J_{s}^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
whose strata are invariant under addition. This is shown in the same way as in Looijenga’s proof of the original statement (in
[
Loo74
]
; one can also read C. Wall’s account
[
Wal77
]
, which is easier to find online).
∎

9.3
Geometric properties
(P1-4)
as transversality conditions

Our plan in this subsection is to translate the geometric properties
(P1-4)
that we introduced earlier in terms of transversality of the multijet map
j^{r}_{s}(i)
of the embedding
i:M\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
to some well-chosen set
W\subset J_{s}^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
(respectively the transversality of the multijet map
(j^{r}_{s})_{M}\rho_{i}
to
W\subset J_{s}^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
); later, in Subsection
9.4
, we use Theorems
9.1
and
9.3
to show that the transverse embeddings are a dense set among all embeddings. There will be two subcases: either
W
represents configurations to be absolutely avoided and the intersection of the image of
j^{r}_{s}(i)
(respectively
(j^{r}_{s})_{M}\rho_{i}
) with
W
will be empty due to a dimension argument, or the intersection will be non-empty but the transversality will constrain the geometry of the configurations in the intersection to be well-behaved.
As in the previous subsections, we write
a\in J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
as

a=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},r_{1},\ldots,r_{s},[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{s}])

and
b\in J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
as
b=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[g_{1}],\ldots,[g_{s}])
with
m_{i}\in M
,
r_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
x_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
.
As usual, we let
M
be a compact
C^{k}
manifold of dimension
m
, but unlike in the remainder of this article, we have to assume in this subsection that
k\geq 3
rather than
k\geq 2
.
The reason for this is that if
i\in\mathrm{Emb}^{k}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
, then
j^{r}_{s}(i)
is of regularity
C^{k-r}
, hence the map
j^{r}_{s}(i)
being transverse to some set
W\subset J_{s}^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
only makes sense if
r+1\leq k
. In what follows, some of the sets
W\subset J_{s}^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
which we will consider can only be defined if
r\geq 2
, hence we need
k\geq r+1=3
to ask that
j^{2}_{s}(i)
be transverse to them.
We will see in the next subsection that this subtlety matters not, and that the density part of Theorem
1.1
remains true when
M
is only
C^{2}
(and in fact even only
C^{1}
, as suggested by Remark
1.3
).

9.3.1
Condition
(P1)
, part 1

The definition of the first set
W
and its properties come from Yomdin’s
[
Yom81
]
. It allows us to handle a weaker version of Condition
(P1)
that we call
(P0)
. We take care of the second part of
(P1)
later in Subsubsection
9.3.3
.

Definition 9.4
.

For any
s,r\in\mathbb{N}
with
s\geq 2
and
r\leq k-1
, we define the following set:

W_{0}^{(s)}:=\{a\in J_{s}^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}):\;r_{1}=\ldots=r_{s}\}.

Remark 9.5
.

W_{0}^{(s)}
is clearly a submanifold of
J_{s}^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
of codimension
s-1
(hence in particular a Whitney stratified set) and invariant under addition.

Lemma 9.6

(Yomdin,
[
Yom81
]
)
.

For any
0\leq r\leq k-1
, if
i\in\mathrm{Emb}^{k}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
is such that
(j^{r}_{s})_{M}\rho_{i}:M^{(s)}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\rightarrow J_{s}^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
is transverse to
W_{0}^{(s)}
for
s=2,\ldots,D+2
, then
{\mathsf{M}}=i(M)
satisfies condition

(P0)

For any critical point
z\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
, the projections
\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z)
are the vertices of a non-degenerate simplex of
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
. In particular,
z
has at most
D+1
projections.

Proof.

This is proved by Yomdin in
[
Yom81
]
. In fact, he proves it for the points
z
in the cut locus of
i(M)
, which is in general larger than its medial axis.
∎

Remark 9.7
.

It is very easy to find manifolds that do not verify condition
(P0)
; e.g., the center
z
of a (Euclidean) sphere
S
of dimension greater than
1
is such that
\pi_{S}(z)
has infinitely many points. Examples where the points of
\pi_{i(M)}(z)
are in finite numbers but are not affinely independent are equally easy to construct.

The rest of the subsection is modelled after these first definition, remark and lemma: we will define sets
W
, show that they are Whitney stratified and of the correct dimension, and show that embeddings that are transverse to them have nice geometric properties (proving that such embeddings are dense will come later).

9.3.2
Conditions
(P2)
and
(P3)

Definition 9.8
.

For any
s,r\in\mathbb{N}
with
s\geq 2
and
1\leq r\leq k
, we let
W_{2}^{(s)}
be the set of multijets
(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{s}])\in J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
such that there exists
z\in\mathrm{Conv}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})
with
d(x_{1},z)=\ldots=d(x_{s},z)
and such that for all
i\in[s]
,
\mathrm{Im}((df_{i})_{m_{i}}:T_{m_{i}}M\rightarrow T_{x_{i}}{\mathbb{R}}^{D})\perp(z-x_{i})
.

Remark 9.9
.

As for
(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{s}])\in J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
the image of
(df_{i})_{m_{i}}
in
T_{x_{i}}{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
does not depend on the choice of representative
f_{i}
for
[f_{i}]\subset C^{k}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})_{m_{i},x_{i}}
, the set
W_{2}^{(s)}
is well-defined.

The definition of
W_{2}^{(s)}
should be understood as follows: let
i:M\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
be an embedding. If
z_{0}
is a critical point of
i(M)
with
\pi_{i(M)}(z_{0})=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\}
, then

(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[i],\ldots,[i])\in W_{2}^{(s)}\cap\mathrm{Im}(j^{r}_{s}(i):M^{(s)}\rightarrow J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D}))

for some
m_{1},\ldots,m_{s}\in M
with
z_{0}
satisfying the constraints for
(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[i],\ldots,[i])
in the definition of
W_{2}^{(s)}
. This is proved further below with Lemma
9.15
.
Note that the set of
W_{2}^{(s)}
also depends on
r
, though we hide that dependency in our notations.

Lemma 9.10
.

For any
s,r\in\mathbb{N}
with
2\leq s\leq D+1
and
1\leq r\leq k-1
, the set
W_{2}^{(s)}
is a finite union of closed Whitney stratifiable subsets
W_{2,\alpha}^{(s)}
of
J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
of codimension at least
sm
.

To prove Lemma
9.10
, rather than to identify possible strata and to manually show that they verify Whitney’s conditions, we will use the fact that semialgebraic subsets of an Euclidean space are Whitney stratifiable (see
[
Tho66
]
).
Remember that a set
A\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is called
semialgebraic set
if it can be written as

A=\bigcup_{i=1}^{l}\bigcap_{j=1}^{k_{i}}\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}:\;P_{i,j}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\;\text{op}_{i,j}\;0\},

where
P_{i,j}
is a polynomial in
n
variables and
\text{op}_{i,j}\in\{<,=,>\}
for all
i=1,\ldots,l
and
j=1,\ldots,k_{i}
. This definition is deceptively simple, as there are some semialgebraic sets that one would not naively expect to be. To learn more on semialgebraic sets, read e.g.,
[
BR90
]
.

Proof.

We fix
s\in\{2,\dots,D+1\}
in the proof, and write
W_{2}=W_{2}^{(s)}
to alleviate notation.
First of all, let us define the set

\begin{split}\tilde{W}_{2}:=&\{(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{s}])\in J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}\;:\;\exists z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\text{ s.t. }\\
&\qquad\|x_{1}-z\|^{2}=\ldots=\|x_{s}-z\|^{2},\;\forall i\in[s],\;\mathrm{Im}((df_{i})_{m_{i}}:T_{m_{i}}M\rightarrow T_{x_{i}}{\mathbb{R}}^{D})\perp(z-x_{i})\ \;\\
&\qquad\text{and }\exists\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{s}\in[0,1]\text{ s.t. }\sum_{i=1}^{s}\lambda_{i}=1\text{ and }z=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\lambda_{i}x_{i}\}.\end{split}

(29)

There are two differences between
W_{2}
and
\tilde{W}_{2}
: the first is that it’s defined as a subset of
J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}
rather than of
J_{s}^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})\subset J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}
, the second is that the constraints in its definitions are the same, but have been rewritten in a more visibly algebraic fashion.
As a result, we have that
W_{2}=\tilde{W}_{2}\cap J_{s}^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
. We will use
\tilde{W}_{2}
to circumvent the lack of compactness of
M^{(s)}
.

Closedness.

Let us first show that
\tilde{W}_{2}
is closed. Let
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\tilde{W}_{2}
be such that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}a_{n}=a\in J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}
, and let us write
a_{n}=(m_{1,n},\ldots,m_{s,n},x_{1,n},\ldots,x_{s,n},[f_{1,n}],\ldots,[f_{s,n}])
. We want to show that
a\in\tilde{W}_{2}
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
z_{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
be a point such that
d(x_{1,n},z_{n})=\ldots=d(x_{s,n},z_{n})
,
\mathrm{Im}((df_{i,n})_{m_{i,n}}:T_{m_{i,n}}M\rightarrow T_{x_{i,n}}{\mathbb{R}}^{D})\perp(z_{n}-x_{i,n})
for
i\in[s]
and
z_{n}\in\mathrm{Conv}(x_{1,n},\ldots,x_{s,n})
(in fact, such a point
z_{n}
is unique). Write
z_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\lambda_{i,n}x_{i,n}
for some
\lambda_{i,n}\in[0,1]
.

We can find a subsequence for which
(\lambda_{1,n},\ldots,\lambda_{s,n})
converges to
(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{s})
, hence for which
z_{n}
converges to
z:=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\lambda_{i}x_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
.
Then by definition
z\in\mathrm{Conv}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
and by continuity
d(x_{1},z)=\ldots=d(x_{s},z)
. Furthermore, choose charts of
M
around
m_{1},\ldots,m_{s}
, and consider the induced adapted chart of
J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}
around
a
.
In the coordinates given by this chart, the derivative
(df_{i,n})_{m_{i,n}}:T_{m_{i,n}}M\rightarrow T_{x_{i,n}}{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
(for each
i=1,\ldots,s
) can be represented by
D
degree
1
homogeneous polynomials in
m
variables, or equivalently by a
D\times m
matrix
A_{i,n}
.
The condition that
\mathrm{Im}((df_{i,n})_{m_{i,n}})\perp(z_{n}-x_{i,n})
can be written as
(A_{i,n})^{\top}(z_{n}-x_{i,n})=0
(as soon as
n
is large enough for
m_{i,n}
to belong to the domain of the chart around
m_{i}
), which is a closed constraint, hence
\mathrm{Im}((df_{i})_{m_{i}}:T_{m_{i}}M\rightarrow T_{x_{i}}{\mathbb{R}}^{D})\perp(z-x_{i})
and
z
satisfies all three conditions in the definition of
\tilde{W}_{2}
. This shows that
a\in\tilde{W}_{2}
, and thus that
\tilde{W}_{2}
is closed in
J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}
. As
W_{2}=\tilde{W}_{2}\cap J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
, we get that
W_{2}
is also closed in
J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
.

Finite union of closed Whitney stratified sets.

A preliminary remark: as was mentioned earlier, it was shown by Thom in
[
Tho66
]
that semialgebraic subsets of Euclidean space are Whitney stratifiable. As
\tilde{W}_{2}
is semialgebraic in the coordinates of any adapted chart of
J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}
(see below), its intersection with the domain of any such chart is Whitney stratifiable. We are somewhat convinced that this should easily imply that
\tilde{W}_{2}
is globally Whitney stratifiable (on the other hand, we cannot in general expect
\tilde{W}_{2}
to be globally semialgebraic), but we could not find any such statement in the literature. Consequently, we have to consider each chart separately,
6
6

6

In fact, we think that a variant of the argument exposed below could be used to show that
\tilde{W}_{2}
as a whole is Whitney stratifiable, though it is not required for our purposes.
as follows.

For any
(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{s}])\in J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}
, we can find charts
\phi_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
of
M
with domains
U_{i}\ni m_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,s
, and from them get an adapted chart

\phi:U\longrightarrow\phi_{1}(U_{1})\times\ldots\times\phi_{s}(U_{s})\times({\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}\times(B^{r}_{m,D})^{s}

with domain
U:=\{(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{s}])\in J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}:\ m_{i}\in U_{i}\text{ for }i\in[s]\}
(we do not need charts around the targets
x_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, as
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
is already an Euclidean space).
For any such chart
U
, let us choose
\varepsilon_{U}>0
small enough that for
i\in[s]
we have

\overline{B}(\phi_{i}(m_{i}),\varepsilon_{U})\subset\phi_{i}(U_{i})\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{m}.

Using the compactness of
M^{s}
, we can find a finite collection of such charts
(\phi_{\alpha},U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
(where
A
is a finite collection of indices) such that

\bigcup_{\alpha\in A}\phi_{1,\alpha}^{-1}(\overline{B}(\phi_{1,\alpha}(m_{1,\alpha}),\varepsilon_{U_{\alpha}}))\times\ldots\times\phi_{s,\alpha}^{-1}(\overline{B}(\phi_{s,\alpha}(m_{s,\alpha}),\varepsilon_{U_{\alpha}}))=M^{s},

which is equivalent to the charts
(\phi_{\alpha},U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
corestricted to

\phi_{1,\alpha}^{-1}(\overline{B}(\phi_{1,\alpha}(m_{1,\alpha}),\varepsilon_{U_{\alpha}}))\times\ldots\times\phi_{s,\alpha}^{-1}(\overline{B}(\phi_{s,\alpha}(m_{s,\alpha}),\varepsilon_{U_{\alpha}}))\times({\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}\times(B^{r}_{m,D})^{s}

covering
J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}
.

In the coordinates of any such chart, the set
\tilde{W}_{2}
(intersected with the domain of the chart) is semialgebraic. Indeed, a consequence of the Tarski-Seidenberg theorem (see e.g.
[
BR90
, Theorem 2.3.4]
) is that any set defined as

\{X\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}:\ \exists Z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}\text{ s.t. }(P_{1}(X,Z)\text{ op}_{1}\;0)\land\ldots\land(P_{q}(X,Z)\text{ op}_{q}\;0)\}

is semialgebraic, where
P_{i}
is a polynomial and
\text{op}_{i}\in\{>,\geq,=\}
for
i=1,\ldots,q
and
\land
is the
AND
logical operator (in fact, more general formula featuring
OR
operators, negations and parentheses are allowed, but this is enough for our purposes).
In the coordinates of a chart
(\phi_{\alpha},U_{\alpha})
, the set
\tilde{W}_{2}
is of this form. Indeed, its definition (see (
29
)) is a combination of:

•

A
\exists
operation for
z,\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{s}
.

•

Algebraic constraints on
z
and the targets
x_{i}
,

•

A constraint that the image of the derivatives
(df_{i})_{m_{i}}
must be perpendicular to
z-x_{i}
. As noted earlier, this is equivalent to the transpose
(A_{i})^{\top}
of the matrix representing
(df_{i})_{m_{i}}
in the coordinates of the charts verifying
(A_{i})^{\top}(z-x_{i})=0
, which is an algebraic constraint on the coordinates representing
(df_{i})_{m_{i}}
,
z
and
x_{i}
.

•

Some more algebraic constraints on the
\lambda_{i}
,
x_{i}
and
z
.

Furthermore, the closed ball
\overline{B}(\phi_{s,\alpha}(m_{s,\alpha}),\varepsilon_{U_{\alpha}})
is also semialgebraic in the coordinate of the chart
(\phi_{\alpha},U_{\alpha})
, as it is defined using a polynomial inequality.
Consequently, the intersection

\tilde{W}_{2,\alpha}:=\tilde{W}_{2}\cap(\phi_{1,\alpha}^{-1}(\overline{B}(\phi_{1,\alpha}(m_{1,\alpha}),\varepsilon_{U_{\alpha}}))\times\ldots\times\phi_{s,\alpha}^{-1}(\overline{B}(\phi_{s,\alpha}(m_{s,\alpha}),\varepsilon_{U_{\alpha}}))\times({\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}\times(B^{r}_{m,D})^{s})

is semialgebraic in the coordinates of
(\phi_{\alpha},U_{\alpha})
, and as a result it is Whitney stratifiable in
J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}
(the image of a Whitney stratifiable set by a diffeomorphism is also Whitney stratifiable). It is also closed.

As Whitney’s conditions a) and b) are purely local, it is trivial to check that the intersection of a Whitney stratified set with an open set of its ambient manifold is still Whitney stratified.
As
J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
is an open set of
J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}
, the intersection

W_{2,\alpha}:=\tilde{W}_{2,\alpha}\cap J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})

is therefore still Whitney stratifiable (and closed) in
J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
. We also have

W_{2}=\tilde{W}_{2}\cap J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})=\left(\bigcup_{\alpha\in A}\tilde{W}_{2,\alpha}\right)\cap J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})=\bigcup_{\alpha\in A}W_{2,\alpha}.

Dimension.

We only have to show that the Whitney stratifiable sets
W_{2,\alpha}
are of codimension
sm
in
J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
to conclude. To do so, consider the following auxiliary set:

\begin{split}W^{\prime}_{2}:=&\{((m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{s}]),z)\in J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}:\;\\
&\qquad\|x_{1}-z\|^{2}=\ldots=\|x_{s}-z\|^{2},\;\forall i\in[s],\;\mathrm{Im}((df_{i})_{m_{i}}:T_{m_{i}}M\rightarrow T_{x_{i}}{\mathbb{R}}^{D})\perp(z-x_{i}),\\
&\qquad\text{rank}((df_{i})_{m_{i}})=m,\;\exists\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{s}\in]0,1[\text{ s.t. }\sum_{i=1}^{s}\lambda_{i}=1,\;z=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\lambda_{i}x_{i}\\
&\qquad\text{and }\dim\text{Aff}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})=s-1\}.\end{split}

(30)

Note the four differences compared to
\tilde{W}_{2}
: the point
z
is among the coordinates rather than the object of a
\exists
statement, we ask that the points
x_{i}
be affinely independent and that the derivatives
(df_{i})_{m_{i}}
be of full rank, and the coefficients
\lambda_{i}
cannot be trivial.
We claim that
W_{2}^{\prime}
is a (non-closed) submanifold of
J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
of codimension
D+sm
, hence of dimension
\dim J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}-sm
.

Assume for now that the claim is true, and consider the projection

\pi:J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\rightarrow J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}.

Restrict it to
U_{\alpha}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, where
U_{\alpha}
is the domain of one of the charts defined in the previous section of the proof. In the coordinates induced by this chart, the set
W_{2}^{\prime}
is semialgebraic
7
7

7

In this paragraph, we are guilty of a small abuse of notations: when we say that
W_{2}^{\prime}
is semialgebraic in the coordinates of the chart, we mean that its intersection with the domain of the chart is semialgebraic in the coordinates (and same for
\pi(W_{2}^{\prime})
and
\overline{\pi(W_{2}^{\prime})}
).
(for the same reasons as
\tilde{W}_{2}
), and the map
\pi
is semialgebraic in the coordinates, as it is simply a projection from one vector space to another (see e.g.,
[
BR90
, Section 2.3]
). Thus
\pi(W_{2}^{\prime})
is also semialgebraic in the coordinates of the chart,
8
8

8

In fact, the projection
\pi
restricted to
W_{2}^{\prime}
is a diffeomorphism onto its image, but it is not needed for the proof.
and so is its closure
\overline{\pi(W_{2}^{\prime})}
(see e.g.,
[
Cos02
, Proposition 1.12]
). Furthermore, it is easy to see that the closure
\overline{\pi(W_{2}^{\prime})}
of
\pi(W_{2}^{\prime})
in
J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}
is equal to
\tilde{W}_{2}
.
Hence (see
[
BR90
, 2.5.4]
)

\dim\tilde{W}_{2}=\dim(\overline{\pi(W_{2}^{\prime})})=\dim(\pi(W_{2}^{\prime}))\leq\dim W_{2}^{\prime}=\dim J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}-sm,

as the same inequalities are true when considering the intersections of those sets with the domain
U_{\alpha}
(respectively
U_{\alpha}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
) of any of the charts
(\phi_{\alpha},U_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
.
As
W_{2}
is the intersection of
\tilde{W}_{2}
with the open set
J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
of
J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}
, we have
\dim W_{2}\leq\dim\tilde{W}_{2}\leq\dim J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}-sm=\dim J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})-sm
, and each of the closed subsets
W_{2,\alpha}
of
W_{2}
is of codimension at least
sm
in
J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
.

The only thing left is to prove the claim that
W_{2}^{\prime}
is a submanifold of
J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
of codimension
D+sm
. This is a local property; in what follows, we implicitly place ourselves in the coordinates of an adapted chart
(\phi,U)
, without explicitly saying that we are considering the intersection of
W_{2}^{\prime}
with
U\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
or constantly specifying ”in the coordinates of…”.

Let
(a,z)
be a point of
W_{2}^{\prime}
, where
a\in J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}
. The conditions that
\dim\text{Aff}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})=s-1
and that
\text{rank}((df_{i})_{m_{i}})=m\text{ for }i=1\in[s]
are open, and the conditions that
\|x_{1}-z\|^{2}=\ldots=\|x_{s}-z\|^{2}
and that there exist
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{s}\in]0,1[
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{s}\lambda_{i}=1
and
z=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\lambda_{i}x_{i}
mean that
z
must be the (unique) center of the circumsphere of
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}
, and that this center must belong to the relative interior of the convex hull of
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}
, which is another open condition on
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}
. Hence we can find a small neighborhood
V
of
a
such that these three open conditions are satisfied on the neighborhood
V\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
of
(a,z)
. For any point
(b,z)=((m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{s}]),z)
in
V\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, the point
z
must again be the center of the circumsphere of
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}
, and for such a point
(b,z)\in V\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, the coordinates of
z
are a (smooth) function
z(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})
of the coordinates of
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}
(see
[
Cox30
]
).

Identify the first order derivatives at
m_{i}
of the functions
f_{i}
with
D\times m
matrices
A_{i}
and let
d^{\geq 2}f_{i}
represent all derivatives at
m_{i}
of higher order, and consider the function
F:V\longrightarrow({\mathbb{R}}^{m})^{s}
given by

F(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},A_{1},d^{\geq 2}f_{1},\ldots,A_{s},d^{\geq 2}f_{s})=(A_{1}^{\top}(z-x_{1}),\ldots,A_{s}^{\top}(z-x_{s})),

(31)

where
z=z(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})
.
Then
F
is a submersion, as the derivative of
(A_{i},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})\mapsto A_{i}^{\top}(z(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})-x_{i})
with respect to
A_{i}
is surjective (since
z-x_{i}\neq 0
). Consequently, the set

\displaystyle F^{-1}(\{0\})=

\displaystyle\{(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},A_{1},d^{\geq 2}f_{1},\ldots,A_{s},d^{\geq 2}f_{s})\in V\;:

\displaystyle\qquad\forall i\in[s],\ A_{i}^{\top}(z(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})-x_{i})=0\}

is a submanifold of codimension
sm
in
J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}
, and

W_{2}^{\prime}\cap(V\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D})

is simply the graph of the function

\begin{split}F^{-1}(\{0\})&\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\\
(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{s}])&\longmapsto z(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}).\end{split}

Thus it is a submanifold of codimension
sm+D
in
J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, as claimed.
∎

Remark 9.11
.

We are quite convinced that
W_{2}^{(s)}
is in fact a Whitney stratifiable subset of
J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
(rather than a union) - we know how to prove it in the case where
M
is smooth rather than just
C^{k}
using Nash-Tognoli’s theorem, but it should be easy to prove in general using some theorem along the lines of “any subset of a manifold
X
that is semialgebraic in the coordinates of a well-chosen atlas of
X
is Whitney stratifiable”; however, we are no specialists of semialgebraic sets and could not find such a statement in the literature.

Lemma
9.10
gives the existence of a decomposition
W_{2}^{(s)}=\cup_{\alpha\in A}W_{2,\alpha}^{(s)}
of
W_{2}^{(s)}
into a finite union of closed Whitney stratifiable sets of codimension at least
sm
, but this decomposition is not unique.
We choose for a given
M
and for each
s,r\in\mathbb{N}
with
2\leq s\leq D+1
and
1\leq r\leq k-1
such a decomposition
\{W_{2,\alpha}^{(s)}\}_{\alpha\in A}
, and we simply refer to it as “the decomposition from Lemma
9.10
” or “the sets
\{W_{2,\alpha}^{(s)}\}_{\alpha\in A}
from Lemma
9.10
” in what follows.

We now need another set that is very similar to
W_{2}^{(s)}
, except that it will allow us to select embeddings
i\in\mathrm{Emb}^{k}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
such that for each critical point
z_{0}\in Z(i(M))
and each
x\in\pi_{i(M)}(z_{0})
, the sphere
S(z_{0},d_{i(M)}(z_{0}))
does not osculate
i(M)
at
x
.

Definition 9.12
.

For any
s,r\in\mathbb{N}
with
s\geq 2
and
2\leq r\leq k
, we let
W_{3}^{(s)}
be the set of multijets
(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{s}])\in J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
that belong to
W_{2}^{(s)}
and such that there exists
i\in[s]
for which the quadratic form
v\mapsto\|d(f_{i}\circ\phi)_{0}(v)\|^{2}-\langle z-x_{i},d^{2}(f_{i}\circ\phi)_{0}(v,v)\rangle
is degenerate for any chart
\phi:(U,0)\rightarrow(M,m_{i})
.

Remark 9.13
.

The set
W_{3}^{(s)}
is well-defined. Indeed, the condition on the quadratic form only depends on the derivatives of order less than
2
of the functions
f_{i}
; hence it is independent of the choice of representative
f_{i}\in[f_{i}]\subset C^{k}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})_{m_{i},x_{i}}
. Furthermore, as

\langle z-x_{i},d^{2}(f_{i}\circ\phi)_{0}(v,v)\rangle=\langle z-x_{i},d^{2}(f_{i})_{m_{i}}(d\phi_{0}(v),d\phi_{0}(v))\rangle
(because
\mathrm{Im}((df_{i})_{m_{i}})\perp(z-x_{i})
),
it does not depend on the choice of the chart
\phi
either - if it is true for one chart, it is true for all.

Lemma 9.14
.

For any
s,r\in\mathbb{N}
with
2\leq s\leq D+1
and
2\leq r\leq k-1
, the set
W_{3}^{(s)}
is a finite union of closed Whitney stratifiable subsets
W_{3,\beta}^{(s)}
of
J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
of codimension at least
sm+1
.

Proof.

The proof is almost identical to that of Lemma
9.10
; we omit most details and only comment on the few meaningful differences. As in the previous proof, we fix
s\in\{2,\dots,D+1\}
and write
W_{3}=W_{3}^{(s)}
and
W_{2}=W_{2}^{(s)}
.
The set
W_{3}
can be written as
W_{3}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{s}W_{3}^{i}
, where
W_{3}^{i}
is the set of multijets
(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{s}])\in W_{2}
for which the quadratic form
v\mapsto\|d(f_{i}\circ\phi)_{0}(v)\|^{2}-\langle z-x_{i},d^{2}(f_{i}\circ\phi)_{0}(v,v)\rangle
is degenerate for any chart
\phi:(U,0)\rightarrow(M,m_{i})
.
Our goal is to conclude using the same arguments as in the proof of Lemma
9.10
that each
W_{3}^{i}
is a finite union of closed Whitney stratifiable subsets of
J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
of codimension at least
sm+1
, hence that
W_{3}
can also be written as such a union.

The condition for a point
(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{s}])\in J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
that
v\mapsto\|d(f_{i}\circ\phi)_{0}(v)\|^{2}-\langle z-x_{i},d^{2}(f_{i}\circ\phi)_{0}(v,v)\rangle
be degenerate is equivalent to a closed condition on the first and second derivative of the function
[f_{i}]
(that does not depend on the choice of the representative
f_{i}
), and this condition is algebraic in adapted charts of
J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
; indeed, in such a system of coordinates, it is equivalent to asking that the matrix
A_{i}^{\top}A_{i}-B_{i}
has zero determinant, where
A_{i}
represents the first differential of
f_{i}
and
B_{i}
represents the bilinear form that applies the second differential of
f_{i}
to a pair of vectors and returns the scalar product of the result with
z-x_{i}
(both being represented in the chosen system of coordinates).

It remains to show that the added condition does increase the codimension by one. To that end, we would like the condition on the determinant of the matrix to be transverse to our other conditions, but sadly it is not even transverse to the ambient space: the determinant is not a submersion from the space of
m\times m
matrices to
{\mathbb{R}}
(in particular the set of all singular matrices is semialgebraic, but is not a manifold). However, this minor difficulty can be avoided. As the differential of the determinant at a matrix
K
is equal to
d(\det)_{K}:H\mapsto\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{adj}(K)H)
, where
\mathrm{adj}(K)
is the adjugate of
K
and
\mathrm{tr}
is the trace, the determinant is indeed a submersion at any matrix
K
of rank at least
m-1
.

In the proof of Lemma
9.10
, we defined an auxiliary set
W_{2}^{\prime}\subset J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
such that
\overline{\pi(W_{2}^{\prime})}\cap J_{s}^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})=W_{2}
and such that
\dim W_{2}\leq\dim W_{2}^{\prime}
, see (
30
). Similarly, we define the set
(W^{i}_{3})^{\prime}
of pairs
((m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{s}]),z)\in W_{2}^{\prime}
such that the form
v\mapsto\|d(f_{i}\circ\phi)_{0}(v)\|^{2}-\langle z-x_{i},d^{2}(f_{i}\circ\phi)_{0}(v,v)\rangle
is of rank
m-1
.
We claim that the set
(W^{i}_{3})^{\prime}
is a submanifold of codimension
sm+D+1
in
J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
(compared to the codimension
sm+D
of
W_{2}^{\prime}
). If this claim is true, the rest of the proof is the same as that of Lemma
9.10
(with the additional fact that the matrices of rank
m-1
are dense in the set of all degenerate matrices).

To prove the claim, note that the only difference with
W_{2}^{\prime}
is the condition that the form
v\mapsto\|d(f_{i}\circ\phi)_{0}(v)\|^{2}-\langle z-x_{i},d^{2}(f_{i}\circ\phi)_{0}(v,v)\rangle
be of rank
m-1
.
This is the intersection of the condition that it be degenerate (as in the definition of
W_{3}
), or equivalently that the determinant of the associated matrix be
0
, and the condition that it be of rank at least
m-1
, which is an open condition.
Similarly to the proof of Lemma
9.10
, any point in
(W_{3}^{i})^{\prime}
admits an open neighborhood
V\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\subset J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
such that the intersection of the set
(W^{i}_{3})^{\prime}
with this neighborhood is the graph by the function
(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{s}])\mapsto z(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})
of the intersection
F^{-1}(\{0\})\cap G_{i}^{-1}(\{0\})
of the zero sets of two maps. Here, the map
F
is defined in (
31
) and the map
G_{i}:V\to{\mathbb{R}}
is given by

G_{i}(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},A_{1},d^{2}f_{1},\ldots,A_{s},d^{2}f_{s},d^{\geq 3}f_{1},\ldots,d^{\geq 3}f_{s})=\det(A_{j}^{\top}A_{j}-B_{j})

(32)

where the
D\times m
matrix
A_{i}
represents the first differential of
f_{i}
at
m_{i}
and the
m\times m
matrix
B_{i}
represents the bilinear form that applies the second differential
(d^{2}f_{i})_{m_{i}}
of
f_{i}
(which we simply write
d^{2}f_{i}
here) to a pair of vectors and returns the scalar product of the result with
z-x_{i}
.
We already know that
F
is a submersion; so is
G_{j}
, because
V
is chosen (among other open conditions) so that the matrix
A_{i}^{\top}A_{i}-B_{i}
be of rank at least
m-1
for any point in
V
. Furthermore, it is easy to show that the conditions “
F=0
” and “
G_{j}=0
” are transverse to each other, essentially because “
F=0
” does not constrain
(d^{2}f_{i})_{m_{i}}
in any way. Hence
F^{-1}(\{0\})\cap G_{j}^{-1}(\{0\})\subset V
is a submanifold of codimension
sm+1
in
J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}
, and
(W^{i}_{3})^{\prime}\cap(V\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
is a submanifold of codimension
sm+D+1
in
J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
. This proves the claim, and thence the lemma.
∎

As for Lemma
9.10
, we choose for a given
M
and for each
s,r\in\mathbb{N}
with
2\leq s\leq D+1
and
2\leq r\leq k-1
a decomposition
W_{3}^{(s)}=\cup_{\beta\in B}W_{3,\beta}^{(s)}
of
W_{3}^{(s)}
into a finite union of closed Whitney stratifiable sets of codimension at least
sm+1
whose existence is guaranteed by Lemma
9.14
, and we simply refer to it as “the decomposition from Lemma
9.14
” or “the sets
\{W_{3,\beta}^{(s)}\}_{\beta\in B}
from Lemma
9.14
” in what follows.

Lemma 9.15
.

For any
2\leq r\leq k-1
, if
i\in\mathrm{Emb}^{k}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
is such that
{\mathsf{M}}=i(M)
satisfies condition
(P0)
and that for all
s=2,\dots,D+1
,
j^{r}_{s}(i):M^{(s)}\rightarrow J_{s}^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
is transverse to the sets
\{W_{2,\alpha}^{(s)}\}_{\alpha\in A}
from Lemma
9.10
and to the sets
\{W_{3,\beta}^{(s)}\}_{\beta\in B}
from Lemma
9.14
, then
{\mathsf{M}}=i(M)
satisfies conditions

(P2)

The set
Z({\mathsf{M}})
is finite.

(P3)

For every
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
and every
x_{0}\in\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})
, the sphere
S(z_{0},d_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0}))
is non-osculating
{\mathsf{M}}
at
x_{0}
.

As will become apparent in the proof of Lemma
9.15
, condition
(P3)
is a consequence of the transversality to the sets
\{W_{3,\beta}^{(s)}\}_{\beta\in B}
only. On the other hand, we need transversality to both collections of sets to get condition
(P2)
: transversality to the sets
\{W_{2,\alpha}^{(s)}\}_{\alpha\in A}
is required so that the dimension of the set of critical points
Z(i(M))
be
0
, and transversality to the sets
\{W_{3,\beta}^{(s)}\}_{\beta\in B}
so that
Z(i(M))
have no accumulation point.

Proof.

Fix
2\leq r\leq k-1
and
i\in\mathrm{Emb}^{k}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
as in the statement.
As we assume that
i(M)
satisfies condition
(P0)
, we know that any critical point
z
of
i(M)
is such that the set
\pi_{i(M)}(z)
of its projections on
i(M)
is of cardinality at most
D+1
.
Let us write as
Z(i(M))_{s}
the points
z\in Z(i(M))
such that
\pi_{i(M)}(z)
is of cardinality exactly
2\leq s\leq D+1
.

Let
z\in Z(i(M))_{s}
and let us write
\pi_{i(M)}(z)=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\}
. We also call
m_{1},\ldots,m_{s}\in M
the points such that
i(m_{j})=x_{j}
.
By definition
d(x_{1},z)=\ldots=d(x_{s},z)
, and
T_{x_{j}}i(M)=\mathrm{Im}(di_{m_{j}}:T_{m_{j}}M\rightarrow T_{x_{j}}{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
must be perpendicular to
z-x_{j}
for
j\in[s]
for
x_{j}
to belong to
\pi_{i(M)}(z)
.
Furthermore, the point
z
must belong to the convex hull of
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}
, as it is equal to
m(\{x_{1},\dots,x_{s}\})
, the center of the smallest enclosing ball of
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{s}\}
(see Section 2.1 of
[
CCSL06
]
).

This shows that
j^{r}_{s}(i)(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s})=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[i],\ldots,[i])
and
z
satisfy the conditions in the definition of
W_{2}^{(s)}
, hence that
j^{r}_{s}(i)(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s})
belongs to
W_{2}^{(s)}\cap\mathrm{Im}(j^{r}_{s}(i))\subset J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
. In other words, each point
z
of
Z(i(M))_{s}
corresponds to a point
j^{r}_{s}(i)(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s})
in the intersection
W_{2}^{(s)}\cap\mathrm{Im}(j^{r}_{s}(i))
. Note that the converse is not true: there can be points
(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[i],\ldots,[i])\in\mathrm{Im}(j^{r}_{s}(i))
and
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
that satisfy the conditions of
W_{2}^{(s)}
, yet such that
z
is not a critical point of
i(M)
(simply because the points
x_{j}
are not the closest points to
z
in
i(M)
).

Furthermore, for any point
(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{s}])\in J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
, there can be at most a single point
z
for which the conditions of
W_{2}^{(s)}
are satisfied, this point
z
being equal to the center of the smallest enclosing ball of
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{s}\}
(see Section 2.1 of
[
CCSL06
]
).
Consequently, there is an injection
\psi:Z(i(M))_{s}\xhookrightarrow{}W_{2}^{(s)}\cap\mathrm{Im}(j^{r}_{s}(i))
.

Consider
z
a point of
Z(i(M))_{s}
with
\pi_{i(M)}(z)=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\}
and

\psi(z)=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[i],\ldots,[i])

the associated point of
W_{2}^{(s)}\cap\mathrm{Im}(j^{r}_{s}(i))
. Then by definition of
W_{3}^{(s)}\subset W_{2}^{(s)}
,
\psi(z)\in W_{3}^{(s)}
if and only if there exists
j\in[s]
such that for any chart
\phi:(U,0)\rightarrow(M,m_{j})
the form
v\mapsto\|d(i\circ\phi)_{0}(v)\|^{2}-\langle z-x_{j},d^{2}(i\circ\phi)_{0}(v,v)\rangle
is degenerate. For a fixed
j\in[s]
, this condition is equivalent to the sphere
S(z,d_{i(M)}(z))
osculating
i(M)
at
x_{j}
, as shown in the Osculation Characterization Lemma
3.1
.
In other words, we only need
W_{3}^{(s)}\cap\mathrm{Im}(j^{r}_{s}(i))
to be empty for
s=2,\ldots,D+1
for the embedding
i
to satisfy condition
(P3)
.

But the strata of the Whitney stratified sets
W_{3,\beta}^{(s)}
have codimension at least
sm+1
(see Lemma
9.14
), and the map
j^{r}_{s}(i):M^{(s)}\rightarrow J_{s}^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
intersects them transversally by hypothesis.
As
M^{(s)}
is of dimension
sm
, the image
\mathrm{Im}(j^{r}_{s}(i))
can only have empty intersections with the strata of each
W_{3,\beta}^{(s)}
, hence with
W_{3}^{(s)}
as a whole. We have shown that
i
verifies condition
(P3)
.

Similarly, the strata of the Whitney stratified sets
W_{2,\alpha}^{(s)}
have codimension at least
sm
, hence the image
\mathrm{Im}(j^{r}_{s}(i))
can only have a non-empty intersection with the strata of codimension
sm
of
W_{2,\alpha}^{(s)}
, and that intersection must be of dimension
0
and be discrete. The same holds for
W_{2}^{(s)}\cap\mathrm{Im}(j^{r}_{s}(i))
.

Let us assume by contradiction that
i
does not satisfy condition
(P2)
; there must be an index
2\leq s\leq D+1
such that
Z(i(M))_{s}
contains infinitely many points. As each critical point
z
and its projections
\pi_{i(M)}(z)
belong to the compact set
\mathrm{Conv}(i(M))
, we can find a sequence of critical points
(z_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with projections
\pi_{i(M)}(z_{n})=\{x_{n,1},\ldots,x_{n,s}\}
, whose preimages by
i
we write
\{m_{n,1},\ldots,m_{n,s}\}\subset M
, such that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}z_{n}=z
,
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}m_{n,j}=m_{j}
and
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}x_{n,j}=x_{j}
for some points
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
,
m_{1},\ldots,m_{s}\in M
and
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\in i(M)
(with of course
i(m_{j})=x_{j}
).

If the points
m_{j}
(or equivalently the points
x_{j}
) are pairwise distinct, the sequence of points
\psi(z_{n})=(m_{n,1},\ldots,m_{n,s},x_{n,1},\ldots,x_{n,s},[i],\ldots,[i])\in W_{2}^{(s)}\cap\mathrm{Im}(j^{r}_{s}(i))
converges to

(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[i],\ldots,[i])\in J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D}),

which does belong to
W_{2}^{(s)}\cap\mathrm{Im}(j^{r}_{s}(i))
as well (as
W_{2}^{(s)}\cap\mathrm{Im}(j^{r}_{s}(i))
is closed in
J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
). Hence we have found an infinite sequence with an accumulation point in
W_{2}^{(s)}\cap\mathrm{Im}(j^{r}_{s}(i))
, contradicting its discrete nature - this is impossible.

Let us now consider the case where the points
m_{j}
are not pairwise distinct. As
i(M)
has positive reach, there cannot be critical points arbitrarily close to it; consequently
z
does not belong to
i(M)
. Furthermore, as the norm
\|\nabla d_{i(M)}\|
of the generalized gradient is lower semicontinuous, we have
\nabla d_{i(M)}(z)=0
, hence
z
is a critical point. By continuity, the limit points
x_{j}
are also such that
d(x_{j},z)=d(i(M),z)
, hence
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\}\subset\pi_{i(M)}(z)
.
As the points
m_{j}
are not pairwise distinct, we can assume (up to permuting some indices) that
m_{1}=m_{2}
, hence that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}x_{n,1}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}x_{n,2}=x_{1}
. This means that for
n
large enough, there are points
z_{n}
arbitrarily close to a critical point
z
such that they have two distinct projections
x_{n,1}
and
x_{n,2}
themselves arbitrarily close to a projection
x_{1}
of
z
. As we have shown earlier that
i
satisfies condition
(P3)
, we know that the sphere
S(z,d_{i(M)}(z))
does not osculate
i(M)
at any of the
x_{j}
: this yields a contradiction with the local unicity of the projection from Lemma
4.1
.
Hence
i
satisfies condition
(P2)
as well, as claimed.
∎

It might have been possible to handle all cases
s\in\{2,\ldots,D+1\}
at once, and in particular the case of the sequences of projections
x_{n,1}
and
x_{n,2}
having the same limit, using some compactification of
J^{r}_{D+1}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
such as the one recently defined in
[
AL23
]
. As there is no version of Thom and Mather’s transversality theorem adapted to such a compactification yet, we favoured a more elementary approach.

Remark 9.16
.

A tube in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
is a simple example (that can easily be compactified) of a manifold that satisfies neither
(P2)
nor
(P3)
.

9.3.3
Condition
(P1)
, part 2

We will now handle the second part of Condition
(P1)
, i.e. the fact that each critical point
z\in Z(i(M))
must belong to the relative interior of the convex hull of its projections on
i(M)
, in order to avoid the situation illustrated earlier in Example
8.2
.

Definition 9.17
.

For any
s,r\in\mathbb{N}
with
s\geq 3
and
1\leq r\leq k
, we let
W_{1}^{(s)}
be the set of multijets
(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{s}])\in J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
that belong to
W_{2}^{(s)}
and such that the (unique) point
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
satisfying the conditions in the definition of
W_{2}^{(s)}
belongs to
\mathrm{Conv}(x_{1},\dots,x_{s-1})
.

Lemma 9.18
.

For any
s,r\in\mathbb{N}
with
3\leq s\leq D+1
and
1\leq r\leq k-1
, the set
W_{1}^{(s)}
is a finite union of closed Whitney stratifiable subsets
W_{1,\gamma}^{(s)}
of
J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
of codimension at least
sm+1
.

Proof.

The proof is yet again almost identical to that of Lemma
9.10
; we omit most details and only comment on the few meaningful differences. As in the previous proofs, we fix
s\in\{3,\dots,D+1\}
and write
W_{1}=W_{1}^{(s)}
.
The definition of
W_{1}
is the same as that of
W_{2}
, except that we ask that
z\in\mathrm{Conv}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1})
rather than
z\in\mathrm{Conv}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1},x_{s})
. This simply replaces one closed semialgebraic condition by another; the only non-trivial point is to show that this change does increase the codimension by one compared to
W_{2}
.

In the proof of Lemma
9.10
, we defined an auxiliary set
W_{2}^{\prime}\subset J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
such that
\overline{\pi(W_{2}^{\prime})}\cap J_{s}^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})=W_{2}
and such that
\dim W_{2}\leq\dim W_{2}^{\prime}
, see (
30
). Similarly, we can define the set

\begin{split}W^{\prime}_{1}:=&\{((m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{s}]),z)\in J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}:\;\\
&\qquad\|x_{1}-z\|^{2}=\ldots=\|x_{s}-z\|^{2},\;\forall i\in[s],\;\mathrm{Im}((df_{i})_{m_{i}}:T_{m_{i}}M\rightarrow T_{x_{i}}{\mathbb{R}}^{D})\perp(z-x_{i}),\\
&\qquad\text{rank}((df_{i})_{m_{i}})=m,\;\exists\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{s-1}\in]0,1[\text{ s.t. }\sum_{i=1}^{s-1}\lambda_{i}=1,\;z=\sum_{i=1}^{s-1}\lambda_{i}x_{i}\\
&\qquad\text{and }\dim\text{Aff}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})=s-1\}.\end{split}

(33)

We claim that the set
W_{1}^{\prime}
is a submanifold of codimension
sm+D+1
in
J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
(compared to the codimension
sm+D
of
W_{2}^{\prime}
). Once this has been shown, the rest of the proof is the same as that of Lemma
9.10
.
This is a local property; in what follows, we implicitly place ourselves in the coordinates of an adapted chart
(\phi,U)
of
J_{s}^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
, without explicitly saying that we are considering the intersection of
W_{1}^{\prime}
with
U\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
or constantly specifying ”in the coordinates of…”.

Let
(a,z)
be a point of
W_{1}^{\prime}
, with
a\in J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}
. As in the proof of Lemma
9.10
, the conditions that
\dim\text{Aff}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})=s-1
, that
\text{rank}((df_{i})_{m_{i}})=m\text{ for }i=1,\ldots,s
and that the center of the circumsphere of
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1}
belongs to the relative interior of
\mathrm{Conv}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1})
are open, and remain true in a small neighborhood
V
of
a
, hence in the neighborhood
V\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
around
(a,z)
.
Likewise, for
(b,z)=((m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{s}]),z)
in
V\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, the conditions that
\|x_{1}-z\|^{2}=\ldots=\|x_{s-1}-z\|^{2}
and that there exist
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{s-1}\in]0,1[
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{s-1}\lambda_{i}=1
and
z=\sum_{i=1}^{s-1}\lambda_{i}x_{i}
means that the coordinates of
z
are a (smooth) function
z(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1})
of the coordinates of
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1}
, as
z
must be the center of the circumsphere of
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1}
.

Similarly to the proofs of Lemmas
9.10
and
9.10
, the set
W_{1}^{\prime}\cap(V\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D})\subset J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
is the graph by the function
(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{s}])\mapsto z(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1})
of the intersection
F^{-1}(\{0\})\cap H^{-1}(\{0\})
of the zero sets of two maps. Here, the map
F
is defined in (
31
) and the map
H:V\to{\mathbb{R}}
is given by

H(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},d^{\geq 1}f_{1},\ldots,d^{\geq 1}f_{s})=\|z-x_{s}\|^{2}-\|z-x_{1}\|^{2},

(34)

where
z=z(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1})
and
d^{\geq 1}f_{i}
represents the differentials of
f_{i}
of order greater than or equal to
1
at
m_{i}
.

We already know that
F
is a submersion and so is
G
(in particular because
z\neq x_{s}
). The conditions “
F=0
” and “
H=0
” are transverse to each other essentially because “
H=0
” does not constrain the differentials
(df_{i})_{m_{i}}
(represented by the matrices
A_{i}
) in any way.
Hence
F^{-1}(\{0\})\cap H^{-1}(\{0\})\subset V
is a submanifold of codimension
sm+1
in
J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}
, and
W_{1}^{\prime}\cap(V\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
is a submanifold of codimension
sm+D+1
in
J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})^{s}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
. This proves the claim, and thence the lemma.
∎

As for Lemmas
9.10
and
9.14
, we choose for a given
M
and for each
s,r\in\mathbb{N}
with
3\leq s\leq D+1
and
1\leq r\leq k-1
a decomposition
W_{1}^{(s)}=\cup_{\gamma\in C}W_{1,\gamma}^{(s)}
of
W_{1}^{(s)}
into a finite union of closed Whitney stratifiable sets of codimension at least
sm+1
whose existence is guaranteed by Lemma
9.18
, and we simply refer to it as “the decomposition from Lemma
9.18
” or “the sets
\{W_{1,\gamma}^{(s)}\}_{\gamma\in C}
from Lemma
9.18
” in what follows.

Lemma 9.19
.

For any
1\leq r\leq k-1
, if
i\in\mathrm{Emb}^{k}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
is such that
{\mathsf{M}}=i(M)
satisfies condition
(P0)
and that for all
s=3,\ldots,D+1
,
j^{r}_{s}(i):M^{(s)}\rightarrow J_{s}^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
is transverse to the sets
\{W_{1,\gamma}^{(s)}\}_{\gamma\in C}
from Lemma
9.18
, then
{\mathsf{M}}
satisfies condition

(P1)

For every
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
, the projections
\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})
are the vertices of a non-degenerate simplex of
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
. In particular,
z_{0}
has at most
D+1
projections. Moreover, the point
z_{0}
belongs to the relative interior of the convex hull of
\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})

Proof.

Fix
1\leq r\leq k-1
and
i\in\mathrm{Emb}^{k}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
as in the statement.
As we assume that
i
satisfies condition
(P0)
, we know that any critical point
z
of
i(M)
is such that its projections
\pi_{i(M)}(z)
are the vertices of a non-degenerate simplex of
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, and that in particular there can be no more than
D+1
of them. We only have to show that each such
z
must belong to the relative interior of this simplex for
(P1)
to be true.

Let
z\in Z(i(M))
with
\pi_{i(M)}(z)=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\}
for some
s=2,\ldots,D+1
. Let us assume, by way of contradiction, that
z
does not belong to the relative interior of
\mathrm{Conv}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})
. As we have shown in the proof of Lemma
9.15
that
z
does belong to
\mathrm{Conv}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})
, it must then belong to a strict face of the simplex, hence to the convex hull of a strict subset of its vertices. If
s=2
, we already find a contradiction (as
z
cannot be equal to any of the
x_{i}
). Otherwise, up to reordering the vertices, we can assume that
z\in\mathrm{Conv}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1})
. As in the proof of Lemma
9.15
, we also have that the point
j^{r}_{s}(i)(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s})=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[i],\ldots,[i])
and
z
are such that
d(x_{1},z)=\ldots=d(x_{s},z)
and that
\mathrm{Im}((df_{i})_{m_{i}})\perp(z-x_{i})
for
i=1,\ldots,s
. Thus, the point
j^{r}_{s}(i)(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s})
must belong to
W_{1}^{(s)}\cap\mathrm{Im}(j^{r}_{s}(i))
.

But the strata of the Whitney stratified sets
W_{1,\gamma}^{(s)}
have codimension at least
sm+1
(see Lemma
9.14
), and the map
j^{r}_{s}(i):M^{(s)}\rightarrow J_{s}^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
intersects them transversally by hypothesis.
As
M^{(s)}
is of dimension
sm
, the image
\mathrm{Im}(j^{r}_{s}(i))
can only have empty intersections with the strata of each
W_{1,\gamma}^{(s)}
, hence with
W_{1}^{(s)}
as a whole. This yields a contradiction, thus we have shown that
i(M)
verifies condition
(P1)
.
∎

9.3.4
Condition
(P4)

The somewhat delicate case of condition
(P4)
is all that remains; to tackle it, we will need the results from Section
6
.
Similarly to how we proceeded earlier, we want to define a family of sets
W_{4}^{(s)}\subset J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
such that transversality to them will yield the Big Simplex Property
(BSP)
, and consequently condition
(P4)
.
To do so, we will use the expression from Lemma
4.3
of the differential of the projection in terms of charts of our
C^{k}
compact manifold
M
, as it is compatible with the formalism of jets.

Let
m_{0}\in M
,
x_{0},z_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
and
\phi:(V,u_{0})\rightarrow(M,m_{0})
be any chart of
M
around
m_{0}
. Let also
f\in C^{2}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
be some function that maps
m_{0}
to
x_{0}
such that

df_{m_{0}}
has full rank, that
\mathrm{Im}(df_{m_{0}})\perp z_{0}-x_{0}
, and that the quadratic form

v\mapsto\|d(f\circ\phi)_{u_{0}}(v)\|^{2}-\langle z_{0}-x_{0},d^{2}(f\circ\phi)_{u_{0}}(v,v)\rangle

is positive definite. Note that these conditions depend only of the value and the first and second differentials of
f
at
m_{0}
.
Then, Lemma
4.3
guarantees the existence of
\delta>0
and of a neighborhood
U\subset M
of
m_{0}
such that

f|_{U}:U\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
is an embedding and that each
z\in B(z_{0},\delta)
admits a unique projection
p(z)
on
\overline{f(U)}
with
p(z)\in f(U)
. The lemma also gives an expression for the differential
dp_{z_{0}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\rightarrow\mathrm{Im}(df_{m_{0}})
at
z_{0}
of
p:B(z_{0},\delta)\longrightarrow f(U)
, and this expression also only depends on the first two differentials of
f
at
m_{0}
.
Hence, given
r\geq 2
, a jet
(m,x,[f])\in J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
such that
df_{m}
has full rank and a point
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
such that
\mathrm{Im}(df_{m})\perp z-x
, and assuming that
v\longmapsto\|d(f\circ\phi)_{0}(v)\|^{2}-\langle z-x,d^{2}(f\circ\phi)_{0}(v,v)\rangle
is positive definite (for some chart
\phi:(V,0)\rightarrow(M,m)
), we can unambiguously define the differential at
z
of the projection
p
on the image of
f
, independently from the choice of representative
f\in[f]
.

Definition 9.20
.

When properly defined, we write as
P_{[f],x,z}
the differential at
z
of the projection
p
on the image of
f
around
x
.

Now we can formulate an analogue in the language of jets of the quadratic form
B
from Lemma
6.7
.

Definition 9.21
.

Let
r,s\geq 2
and let
a=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{s}])\in J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
and
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
. Assume that for
i\in[s]
the map
(df_{i})_{m_{i}}
is of rank
m
, the quadratic form
v\longmapsto\|d(f_{i}\circ\phi_{i})_{0}(v)\|^{2}-\langle z-x_{i},d^{2}(f_{i}\circ\phi_{i})_{0}(v,v)\rangle
is positive definite (for some chart
\phi_{i}:(V,0)\rightarrow(M,m_{i})
) and
\mathrm{Im}((df_{i})_{m_{i}})\perp(z-x_{i})
.

We define the quadratic form

B_{a,z}(h):=\sum_{i,j=1}^{s}\det(A_{a,z,i}(h)^{\top}A_{a,z,j}(h))

on
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, where
A_{a,z,i}(h)
is the
D\times s
matrix whose
j
-th column is equal to
x_{j}-z
if
j\neq i
and whose
i
-th column is
P_{[f_{i}],x_{i},z}(h)-h
.

We will typically consider the case where
z\in\mathrm{Conv}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})
and
h\in\text{Vec}(x_{1}-z,\ldots,x_{s}-z)^{\perp}
, but this is not needed for the definition to make sense.

We can now finally formulate (the negation of) the Big Simplex Property
(BSP)
in a way that is compatible with jets and thence define
W_{4}^{(s)}
.

Definition 9.22
.

For any
s,r\in\mathbb{N}
with
s\geq 2
and
2\leq r\leq k
, we let
(W_{4}^{\circ})^{(s)}
be the set of multijets
(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{s}])\in J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
that belong to
W_{2}^{(s)}
and such that

•

for all
i\in[s]
,
\mathrm{rank}((df_{i})_{m_{i}})=m
;

•

for all
i\in[s]
and for any chart
\phi:(U,0)\rightarrow(M,m_{i})
, the form
v\mapsto\|d(f_{i}\circ\phi)_{0}(v)\|^{2}-\langle z-x_{i},d^{2}(f_{i}\circ\phi)_{0}(v,v)\rangle
is positive definite;

•

and the quadratic form
B_{a,z}
is degenerate on
\mathrm{Vec}(x_{1}-z,\ldots,x_{s}-z)^{\perp}
.

Furthermore, we let
W_{4}^{(s)}
be the closure of
(W_{4}^{\circ})^{(s)}
in
J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
.

Lemma 9.23
.

For any
s,r\in\mathbb{N}
with
2\leq s\leq D+1
and
2\leq r\leq k-1
, the set
W_{4}^{(s)}
is a finite union of closed Whitney stratifiable subsets
W_{4,\nu}^{(s)}
of
J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
of codimension at least
sm+1
.

Proof.

The proof is yet again similar to that of Lemma
9.10
; as for Lemmas
9.14
and
9.18
, we omit details and focus on the differences of import. As in the previous proofs, we fix
s\in\{2,\dots,D+1\}
and write
W_{4}=W_{4}^{(s)}
.

The definition of
W_{4}^{\circ}
is similar to that of
W_{2}
, but three conditions have been added:
the first is that for
(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{s}])\in J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
the rank of
(df_{i})_{m_{i}}
must be
m
for
i\in[s]
. This is an algebraic constraint in any adapted chart, and an open condition, and does not add any difficulty to the proof. The second is that for any
i\in[s]
and any chart
\phi:(U,0)\rightarrow(M,m_{i})
, the form
v\mapsto\|d(f_{i}\circ\phi)_{0}(v)\|^{2}-\langle z-x_{i},d^{2}(f_{i}\circ\phi)_{0}(v,v)\rangle
must be positive definite (where
z
, if it exists, is the only point in
\mathrm{Conv}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})
such that
d(x_{1},z)=\ldots=d(x_{s},z)
). We know from the proof of Lemma
9.14
that this condition is algebraic in adapted charts, and it is also open. The third and last addition is that we ask that the form
B_{a,z}
be degenerate on
\text{Vec}(x_{1}-z,\ldots,x_{s}-z)^{\perp}
. If we prove that this condition is semialgebraic in adapted charts and that it does increase the codimension by one, the same arguments as in Lemmas
9.10
,
9.14
and
9.18
show that
W_{4}^{\circ}
is locally semialgebraic of codimension
sm+1
, hence that so is its closure
W_{4}
, which can then be decomposed as a union of finitely many closed Whitney stratified sets
\{W_{4,\nu}\}_{\nu\in N}
of codimension at least
sm+1
(for some finite set of indices
N
), as claimed.

Let
(m,x,[f])\in J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
and
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
be such that the conditions of Lemma
4.3
are satisfied by
x,f,z
. Let
\phi:(V,0)\rightarrow(M,m)
be a chart of
M
around
m
, and let us place ourselves in the adapted chart of
J^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
to which
\phi
gives rise.
In Lemma
4.3
, we defined the linear endomorphism
\Lambda:\mathrm{Im}(df_{m})\rightarrow\mathrm{Im}(df_{m})
associated to
x,z,f
by stating that
\Lambda(v)
must be the only vector in
\mathrm{Im}(df_{m})
such that

\langle z-x,d^{2}(f\circ\phi)_{0}(d(f\circ\phi)_{0}^{-1}(v),d(f\circ\phi)_{0}^{-1}(w))\rangle=\langle\Lambda(v),w\rangle

for any
w\in\mathrm{Im}(df_{m_{0}})
.
Let
A
be the
D\times m
matrix that represents
d(f\circ\phi)_{0}:{\mathbb{R}}^{m}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, and let
B
be the
m\times m
matrix that represents the bilinear form
{\mathbb{R}}^{m}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{m}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}},(v,w)\mapsto\langle z-x,d^{2}(f\circ\phi)_{0}(v,w)\rangle
. Then
\tilde{\Lambda}:=d(f\circ\phi)_{0}^{-1}\circ\Lambda\circ d(f\circ\phi)_{0}:{\mathbb{R}}^{m}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
is such that

\langle z-x,d^{2}(f\circ\phi)_{0}(v,w)\rangle=\langle d(f\circ\phi)_{0}(\tilde{\Lambda}(v)),d(f\circ\phi)_{0}(w)\rangle

for any
v,w\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
, which in terms of matrices means that

w^{\top}Bv=w^{\top}A^{\top}A\tilde{\Lambda}(v)

for all
v,w\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
, or equivalently

(A^{\top}A)^{-1}Bv=\tilde{\Lambda}(v)

(where we use the same notation for the map
\tilde{\Lambda}
and the matrix that represents it in the chosen system of coordinates).
We also see that the map
d(f\circ\phi)_{0}^{-1}\circ\pi_{\mathrm{Im}(df_{m})}:{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
is represented by the pseudo-inverse matrix

(A^{\top}A)^{-1}A^{\top}.

This expression is well-defined because
A
is of full rank, and the coefficients of the resulting matrix are polynomial fractions in the coefficients of
A
.

Finally, remember that we defined
P_{[f],x,z}
as the differential at
z
on the image of
f
, which was shown in Lemma
4.3
to be equal to

P_{[f],x,z}=(\mathrm{id}-\Lambda)^{-1}\circ\pi_{\mathrm{Im}(df_{m})}:{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\rightarrow\mathrm{Im}(df_{m}).

This can be rewritten as

\displaystyle P_{[f],x,z}

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{id}-\Lambda)^{-1}\circ\pi_{\mathrm{Im}(df_{m})}=d(f\circ\phi)_{0}d(f\circ\phi)_{0}^{-1}(\mathrm{id}-\Lambda)^{-1}d(f\circ\phi)_{0}d(f\circ\phi)_{0}^{-1}\pi_{\mathrm{Im}(df_{m})}

\displaystyle=d(f\circ\phi)_{0}(\mathrm{id}-d(f\circ\phi)_{0}^{-1}\Lambda d(f\circ\phi)_{0})^{-1}d(f\circ\phi)_{0}^{-1}\pi_{\mathrm{Im}(df_{m})}

\displaystyle=d(f\circ\phi)_{0}(\mathrm{id}-\tilde{\Lambda})^{-1}d(f\circ\phi)_{0}^{-1}\pi_{\mathrm{Im}(df_{m})}

which is thus represented by the
D\times D
matrix

A(I_{m}-(A^{\top}A)^{-1}B)^{-1}(A^{\top}A)^{-1}A^{\top}

whose coefficients are polynomial fractions in the coordinates of the adapted chart.

For
a=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[f_{1}],\ldots,[f_{s}])\in J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
and
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, we defined
B_{a,z}
as

B_{a,z}(h):=\sum_{i,j=1}^{s}\det(A_{a,z,i}(h)^{\top}A_{a,z,j}(h))

for
h\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, where
A_{a,z,i}(h)
is the
D\times s
matrix whose
j
-th column is equal to
x_{j}-z
if
j\neq i
and whose
i
-th column is
P_{[f_{i}],x_{i},z}(h)-h
.
Sums, scalar products and determinants are all polynomial operations, and we have shown that the coefficients of the matrices representing the maps
P_{[f_{i}],x_{i},z}
are polynomial fractions in the coordinates, hence so are the coefficients of the
D\times D
matrix
O
representing
B_{a,z}
.
One can locally define a linear map
\iota:{\mathbb{R}}^{D-m}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
such that the image of
\iota
is
E^{\perp}=\text{Vec}(x_{2}-x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}-x_{2})^{\perp}
and such that it is represented by a matrix
J
whose coefficients are polynomial fractions in the coordinates of
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}
; then
B_{a,z}
is degenerate when restricted to
E^{\perp}
if and only if the determinant of
J^{\top}OJ
is zero, and this determinant is a polynomial fraction in the coordinates of the adapted chart (possibly restricted to a smaller domain).
This proves that asking that
B_{a,z}
be degenerate is a semialgebraic constraint in the adapted charts.

We now turn to the codimension of
W_{4}^{\circ}
. In what follows, we restrict ourselves to an adapted chart of sufficiently small domain
U
of
J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
that
there exists a polynomial
P
in the coordinates of the chart and in those of
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
such that wherever
B_{a,z}
is well-defined, it is degenerate on
E^{\perp}
if and only if
P(a,z)=0

(to simplify notations, we write
P(a,z)
for the value of
P
evaluated in the coordinates corresponding to
a
and
z
).
Such a polynomial exists because we have shown that the determinant of
B_{a,z}
can be locally expressed as a polynomial fraction, which is
0
if and only if its numerator is
0
.

We only need to show that the intersection of
W_{4}^{\circ}
with the domain of such a chart is of codimension at least
sm+1
: then the same arguments as in the proofs of Lemmas
9.10
,
9.14
and
9.18
are enough to conclude.
In what follows, we do not always specify ”in the coordinates of the chart” or ”restricted to the domain of the chart” and write
f_{i}
for the precomposition of
f_{i}
with the coordinates of the chart.

We face two challenges: the first is geometric in nature. Indeed, it is not trivial that asking that the form
B_{a,z}
be degenerate on
E^{\perp}
truly increases the codimension by one; after all, the form
B_{a,z}

is
degenerate on
{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
. Nonetheless, we will show that it is true. The second difficulty is that as in the proof of Lemma
9.10
, the polynomial constraint
P(a,z)=0
is not a priori everywhere transverse to the other constraints, as
P
might have singularities. A few tricks are enough to circumvent this mostly technical problem.

Write
a\in U
as
a=(a_{\leq 1},a_{\geq 2})
, where

\displaystyle a_{\leq 1}

\displaystyle:=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},(df_{1})_{m_{1}},\ldots,(df_{s})_{m_{s}})

\displaystyle a_{\geq 2}

\displaystyle=((d^{2}f_{1})_{m_{1}},\ldots,(d^{2}f_{s})_{m_{s}},(d^{\geq 3}f_{1})_{m_{1}},\ldots,(d^{\geq 3}f_{s})_{m_{s}}),

(up to some reordering of the coordinates). Let
a
be such that for some (unique)
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, we have
\dim\text{Aff}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})=s-1
,
\|x_{1}-z\|^{2}=\ldots=\|x_{s}-z\|^{2}
,
\mathrm{Im}((df_{i})_{m_{i}})\perp(z-x_{i})
and
\text{rank}((df_{i})_{m_{i}})=m
for
i=1,\ldots,s
.
Let us consider all
a^{\prime}\in U
such that the sources
m_{i}^{\prime}
, the targets
x_{i}^{\prime}
and the first differential
(df_{i}^{\prime})_{m_{i}^{\prime}}
of
a^{\prime}
are equal to those of
a
, i.e. such that
a^{\prime}_{\leq 1}=a_{\leq 1}
(we let the
a^{\prime}_{\geq 2}
vary freely). All the conditions listed above are true for all such
a^{\prime}
.

The key observation is the following: we can make the eigenvalues associated to the form

v\mapsto\langle z-x_{i},d^{2}(f_{i}^{\prime})_{m_{i}}(v,v)\rangle
arbitrarily smaller than
0
(and doing so will ensure, as a side effect, that
v\mapsto\|d(f_{i}^{\prime})_{m_{i}}(v)\|^{2}-\langle z-x_{i},d^{2}(f_{i}^{\prime})_{m_{i}}(v,v)\rangle
be positive definite). To summarize what follows, this makes the differential
P_{[f_{i}^{\prime}],x_{i},z}
at
z
of the projection on the image of
f_{i}^{\prime}
tend to
0
, which in turn makes
B_{a^{\prime},z}(h)
approximately equal to the square of the
s
-volume of the simplex
\mathrm{Conv}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},z+h)
for
h\in E^{\perp}
, which is non-zero. This shows that the polynomial that associates the differentials of degree
2
of
a^{\prime}
to
P(a^{\prime},z)
is non-trivial, thus that this polynomial is non-zero for most
a^{\prime}
and defines a codimension
1
constraint. Now let us formalize this argument.

Remember that we defined the linear endomorphism
\Lambda:\mathrm{Im}(d(f_{i}^{\prime})_{m_{i}})\rightarrow\mathrm{Im}(d(f_{i}^{\prime})_{m_{i}})
associated to
x_{i},z,f_{i}^{\prime}
by stating that
\Lambda(v)
must be the only vector in
\mathrm{Im}(d(f_{i}^{\prime})_{m_{i}})
such that (in the coordinates)

\langle z-x,d^{2}(f_{i}^{\prime})_{m_{i}}((d(f_{i}^{\prime})_{m_{i}})^{-1}(v),(d(f_{i}^{\prime})_{m_{i}})^{-1}(w))\rangle=\langle\Lambda(v),w\rangle

for any
w\in\mathrm{Im}(d(f_{i}^{\prime})_{m_{i}})
.
As the eigenvalues associated to the form

v\mapsto\langle z-x_{i},d^{2}(f_{i}^{\prime})_{m_{i}}(v,v)\rangle
go to minus infinity, so must the eigenvalues of
\Lambda
, hence the linear map

P_{[f_{i}^{\prime}],x_{i},z}=(\mathrm{id}-\Lambda)^{-1}\circ\pi_{\mathrm{Im}(d(f_{i}^{\prime})_{m_{i}})}:{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\rightarrow\mathrm{Im}(d(f_{i}^{\prime})_{m_{i}})

tends to
0
.
Then
B_{a^{\prime},z}
tends to

h\mapsto\sum_{i,j=1}^{s}\det(\tilde{A}_{a,z,i}(h)^{\top}\tilde{A}_{a,z,j}(h)),

where
\tilde{A}_{a,z,i}(h)
is the
D\times s
matrix whose
j
-th column is equal to
x_{j}-z
if
j\neq i
and whose
i
-th column is
-h
.
Now the same arguments as in the proof of Lemma
6.7
show that

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{s}\det(\tilde{A}_{a,z,i}(h)^{\top}\tilde{A}_{a,z,j}(h))=(s!)^{2}\mathrm{Vol}_{s}(\mathrm{Conv}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},z+h))^{2}+o(\|h\|^{2}).

But as
\dim\text{Aff}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})=s-1
, if
h\in E^{\perp}=\text{Vec}(x_{1}-z,\ldots,x_{s}-z)^{\perp}
then
\mathrm{Vol}_{s}(\mathrm{Conv}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},z+h))\geq C\|h\|
for some constant
C>0
that depends on the positions of
x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
. This shows that
B_{a^{\prime},z}
is non-degenerate if the eigenvalues associated to
v\mapsto\langle z-x_{i},d^{2}(f_{i}^{\prime})_{m_{i}}(v,v)\rangle
are negative enough.

For the same fixed
a=(a_{\leq 1},a_{\geq 2})\in U
and associated
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D}
, the map

P_{a_{\leq 1}}:a^{\prime}_{\geq 2}\mapsto P((a_{\leq 1},a^{\prime}_{\geq 2}),z)

is polynomial in the coordinates. As
B_{a^{\prime},z}
is non-degenerate for an appropriate choice of
a^{\prime}_{\geq 2}
, the polynomial
P_{a_{\leq 1}}
is non-trivially
0
, hence

S_{a_{\leq 1}}:=\{a^{\prime}_{\geq 2}:\;B_{(a_{\leq 1},a^{\prime}_{\geq 2}),z}\text{ is well-defined and }P_{a_{\leq 1}}(a^{\prime}_{\geq 2})=0\}

is an algebraic hypersurface (possibly with singularities) in the open set

\{a^{\prime}_{\geq 2}:\;B_{(a_{\leq 1},a^{\prime}_{\geq 2}),z}\text{ is well-defined}\},

and in particular a semialgebraic set of codimension (at least)
1
.

Consider the projection

\pi_{\leq 1}:(a_{\leq 1},a_{\geq 2})\mapsto a_{\leq 1}

defined on the domain
U
of the adapted chart. The image of the restriction of
\pi_{\leq 1}
to
W_{4}^{\circ}\cap U
is included
in the set
W_{2}=W_{2}^{(s)}
of Definition
9.8
for
r=1
. We know from Lemma
9.10
that
W_{2}
is a finite union of Whitney stratified sets of codimension
sm
in
J^{1}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
, and we can in fact assume that the image of
\pi_{\leq 1}|_{W_{4}^{\circ}\cap U}
is included in one of those Whitney stratified sets by making
U
small enough.
An easy consequence of Theorem 2.7.1 from
[
BR90
]
is that if
f:A\rightarrow B
is a semialgebraic map between
A
and
B
two semialgebraic sets, the dimension of
A
is less than or equal to the dimension of
B
plus the maximum of the dimensions of the fibers
f^{-1}(b)
(for
b\in B
).
Now
\pi_{\leq 1}:W_{4}^{\circ}\cap U\rightarrow W_{2}
is a semialgebraic map, and we have just shown that the fibers
(\pi_{\leq 1}|_{W_{4}^{\circ}\cap U})^{-1}(a_{\leq 1})
for
a_{\leq 1}\in\mathrm{Im}(\pi_{\leq 1}|_{W_{4}^{\circ}\cap U})
have codimension at least
1
. Hence
W_{4}^{\circ}\cap U
has codimension at least
sm+1
, which is enough to conclude.
∎

As for Lemmas
9.10
,
9.14
and
9.18
, we choose for a given
M
and for each
s,r\in\mathbb{N}
with
2\leq s\leq D+1
and
2\leq r\leq k-1
a decomposition
W_{4}^{(s)}=\cup_{\nu\in N}W_{4,\nu}^{(s)}
of
W_{4}^{(s)}
into a finite union of closed Whitney stratifiable sets of codimension at least
sm+1
whose existence is guaranteed by Lemma
9.23
, and we simply refer to it as “the decomposition from Lemma
9.23
” or “the sets
\{W_{4,\nu}\}_{\nu\in N}
from Lemma
9.23
” in what follows.

Lemma 9.24
.

For any
2\leq r\leq k-1
, if
i\in\mathrm{Emb}^{k}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
is such that
{\mathsf{M}}=i(M)
satisfies condition
(P1)
,
(P2)
and
(P3)
and is such that for all
s=2,\ldots,D+1
,
j^{r}_{s}(i):M^{(s)}\rightarrow J_{s}^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
is transverse to the sets
\{W_{4,\nu}^{(s)}\}_{\nu\in N}
from Lemma
9.23
, then
{\mathsf{M}}
satisfies condition

(P4)

There exist constants
C>0
and
\mu_{0}\in(0,1)
such that for every
\mu\in[0,\mu_{0})
, the set
Z_{\mu}({\mathsf{M}})
is included in a tubular neighborhood of size
C\mu
of
Z({\mathsf{M}})
, that is every point of
Z_{\mu}({\mathsf{M}})
is at distance less than
C\mu
from
Z({\mathsf{M}})
.

Proof.

Fix
2\leq r\leq k\-1
and
i\in\mathrm{Emb}^{k}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
as in the statement.
Our goal is to show that each critical point of
i(M)
satisfies the criterion in Corollary
6.8
; then the corollary will guarantee that
i(M)
satisfies Condition
(P4)
.

Let
z_{0}\in Z({\mathsf{M}})
with
\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(z_{0})=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}\}
for some
s\in\{2,\ldots,D+1\}
, and
E=\mathrm{Vec}(x_{j}-x_{l}:\ j,l\in[s]\}
. Let
\delta>0
be small enough that there exist maps
p_{j}:B(z_{0},\delta)\rightarrow B(x_{j},\delta^{\prime})\cap{\mathsf{M}}
that satisfy the conclusions of Lemma
4.8
for
j=1,\ldots,s
(and for some
\delta^{\prime}>0
). We have to prove that there exist constants
\delta>\tilde{\delta}>0
and
L>0
such that for any
h\in E^{\perp}
with
\|h\|<\tilde{\delta}
,
the
s
-volume of the
s
-simplex
\Delta(h):=\mathrm{Conv}(\{z_{0}+h\}\cup\{p_{j}(z_{0}+h):\;j\in[s]\})
satisfies

\mathrm{Vol}_{s}(\Delta(h))\geq L\|h\|.

(35)

As in the proof of Lemma
9.15
, the point
a=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s},x_{1},\ldots,x_{s},[i],\ldots,[i])\in J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
(where
i(m_{j})=x_{j}
) is such that

d(x_{1},z_{0})=\ldots=d(x_{s},z_{0})
,
z_{0}\in\mathrm{Conv}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{s})
, the rank of
di_{m_{j}}
is
m
, and for any chart
\phi:(U,0)\rightarrow(M,m_{j})
the quadratic form
{\mathbb{R}}^{m}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}},v\mapsto\|d(i\circ\phi)_{0}(v)\|^{2}-\langle z_{0}-x_{j},d^{2}(i\circ\phi)_{0}(v,v)\rangle
is positive definite (for
j=1,\ldots,s
).
The last point is true because we have assumed that
i
satisfies Condition
(P3)
, which is equivalent to this property (as was shown in Lemma
3.1
).
If the form
B_{a,z_{0}}
from Definition
9.21
was degenerate on
E^{\perp}
, the point
a
would satisfy all the conditions of the definition of
(W_{4}^{\circ})^{(s)}
and hence would belong to
W_{4}^{(s)}\cap\mathrm{Im}(j^{r}_{s}(i))\subset J^{r}_{s}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
. But by hypothesis
j_{s}^{r}(i):M^{(s)}\rightarrow J_{s}^{r}(M,{\mathbb{R}}^{D})
is transverse to the Whitney stratifiable sets
\{W_{4,\nu}^{(s)}\}_{\nu\in N}
and those are of codimension at least
sm+1
; hence the intersection of
\mathrm{Im}(j^{r}_{s}(i))
with
W_{4,\nu}^{(s)}
is trivial for any
\nu\in N
, and so is its intersection with
W_{4}^{(s)}
. Consequently, the form
B_{a,z_{0}}
is non-degenerate on
E^{\perp}
.

Remember that by definition, the form
B_{a,z_{0}}
is defined as

B_{a,z_{0}}(h):=\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}=1}^{s}\det(A_{a,z_{0},j_{1}}(h)^{\top}A_{a,z_{0},j_{2}}(h))

for
